viii 

### **INTRODUCTION** 

Defendant, Tyler James Robinson, by and through undersigned counsel, hereby moves 

the Court to exclude from the courtroom during the pendency of this case television cameras and microphones, still photographers, radio microphones and other similar implements of the electronic or broadcast media. The exclusion of these devices is necessary to ensure Mr. Robinson’s rights to due process, to a fair and impartial jury, to counsel, to be free from unreasonable searches and seizures, and to be free from cruel and unusual punishment, as required by the Fourth, Sixth, Eighth and Fourteenth Amendments to the United States Constitution and article I, sections 7, 9, 10, 12, and 14 of the Utah Constitution. 

### **REQUESTED RELIEF** 

Mr. Robinson seeks an order of this Court pursuant to the constitutional provisions cited above and Utah Rule of Judicial Administration 4-401.01 (“Rule 4-401.01”) that prohibits or restricts further electronic media coverage in this cases because: (1) such action is necessary to preserve and protect the constitutional provisions set forth above; (2) under Rule 4-401.01, further electronic media coverage should be prohibited or restricted because (a) the electronic media coverage in this case has not been and will not in the future be for the predominant purpose of journalism or dissemination of news to the public (Rule 4-401.01(2)(A) ); (b) the reasons for doing so are sufficiently compelling to outweigh any presumption that electronic media coverage by a news reporter shall be permitted (Rule 4-401.01(2)(A) ); (c) a judge may terminate or suspend electronic media coverage at any time without prior notice if the judge finds that continued electronic media coverage is no longer appropriate based upon consideration of one or more of the factors in Paragraph (2)(B) (Rule 4-401.01(3)(B) ) ; and (d) the electronic media has violated this Court’s decorum order so prohibition or restriction of electronic media coverage is warranted under Rule 4-401.01(5) . 

1 

_It is possible, of course, that in spite of …safeguards, when a President is assassinated or some similar crime is committed, the public in all parts of the country is so saturated with the publicity respecting the crime that a fair trial may indeed be impossible._ 

J. Skelly Wright, _Fair Trial-Free Press_ , 38 F.R.D. 435, 437 (1966). 

### **RELEVANT FACTS**<sup>**1**</sup> 

Even before a suspect was arrested for the killing of Mr. Kirk on September 10, 2025, 

influential politicians were appearing on national television broadcasts to proclaim they knew for a fact what the motivation for the killing was and what awaited the killer once he was

In Utah, “[a]n actor commits aggravated murder if the actor intentionally or knowingly causes the death of another individual under any of the following circumstances:… (xii) the deceased individual was a local, state, or federal public official, or a candidate for public office, and the homicide is based on, is caused by, or is related to that official position, act, capacity, or candidacy” Utah Code § 76-5-202(2)(a)(xii). Even after what appears to be an exhaustive state and federal investigation, Mr. Robinson has not been charged with this offense or with the comparable federal aggravated murder provision. _See_ 18 U.S.C. § 3592(c)(14). _See_

The electronic prejudicial pretrial publicity directed specifically at Mr. Robinson began 

early in the morning of September 12, 2025, following Mr. Robinson’s surrender to law enforcement authorities on the late evening of September 11, 2025.<sup>4</sup> It has continued relentlessly

death penalty based solely on the facts of a crime and without regard to the identity, background, or character of the person who committed it. The United States Supreme Court answered this long ago in _Woodson v. North Carolina_ : 

A process that accords no significance to relevant facets of the character and record of the individual offender or the circumstances of the particular offense excludes from consideration in fixing the ultimate punishment of death the possibility of compassionate or mitigating factors stemming from the diverse frailties of humankind. It treats all persons convicted of a designated offense not as uniquely individual human beings, but as members of a faceless, undifferentiated mass to be subjected to the blind infliction of the penalty of death.

4 This motion uses the term “prejudicial pretrial publicity” to refer not to all pretrial publicity, but only to that publicity that has been recognized by courts, legislatures, and other rule-making authorities as posing a threat to a defendant’s right to a fair trial. _See, e.g._ , _Skilling v. United States_ , 561 U.S. 358, 382 (2010) (a presumption of juror prejudice is warranted where the pretrial publicity references a “confession or other blatantly prejudicial information of the type readers or viewers could not reasonably be expected to shut from sight.”); _Gentile v. State Bar of Nevada_ , 501 U.S. 1030, 1046 (1991) (noting that public statements that are “obvious bases for a finding of prejudice “ include mention of a confession or of “evidence from searches or test results.”); _KUTV, Inc. v. Conder_ , 668 P.2d 513, 522-23 (Utah 1983) (referring to the American Bar Association’s _Standards Relating to Fair Trial and Free Press_ (1966) as “instructive” on this issue); ABA, _Fair Trial & Public Discourse_ (2013 Fourth Ed.), available at <u>https://www.americanbar.org/groups/criminal_justice/resources/standards/fair-trial-publicdiscourse/;</u> _id_ . at Standard 8-2.2 (prohibiting prosecutorial comment on “the character, credibility, or reputation of a defendant…or the …creed, religion, or sexual orientation of such person”, “the personal opinion of the prosecutor as to the guilt or innocence of the defendant”, “the existence or contents of any confession, admission, or statement given by a defendant…, or the refusal or failure of such person to make a statement”, “the performance or results of any examinations or tests, or the refusal or failure to submit to an examination or test by a defendant”, “the nature of physical evidence expected to be presented”, “the…creed, religion,…character, reputation, or credibility of” prospective witnesses or the victim, and “information that the lawyer knows or has reason to know would be inadmissible as evidence in a trial”); _id_ . at Standard 8-3.1 (same prohibitions apply to law enforcement officers and agencies); _id_ . at Standard 8-2.4 (same prohibitions apply to lawyers acting as legal commentators); Utah R. Prof’l Cond. 3.6 (providing that “[a] lawyer who is participating or has participated in the investigation or litigation of a matter shall not make an extrajudicial statement

Much of the very early prejudicial electronic media coverage in this case was directly linked to a decision by law enforcement and prosecutorial agencies to release to the media a three-page Affidavit of Probable Cause that outlined in detail the allegations against Mr. 

Robinson, including his alleged “confession” supposedly documented in online posts, and the finding of inscribed bullets at some unspecified location. Dkt. 2. The affidavit concludes with investigator Brian Davis’ personal opinion that “[b]ased on the evidence detailed in this statement, I believe there is probable cause that Tyler Robinson committed the crimes of aggravated murder by shooting Charlie Kirk in a circumstance that put many around him at grave risk of death, felony discharge of a firearm, and obstruction of justice for moving and hiding the rifle believed to be used in the shooting.” _Id_ . at 3.<sup>5</sup> Copies of this Affidavit began 

that the lawyer knows or reasonably should know will be disseminated by means of public communication and will have a substantial likelihood of materially prejudicing an adjudicative proceeding in the matter”, and noting in the Comment that “[t]here are… certain subjects that are more likely than not to have a material prejudicial effect on a proceeding, particularly when they refer to a …a criminal matter or any other proceeding that could result in incarceration”, and then listing most of the same prohibitions set forth in ABA Standard 8-2.2); Utah R. Prof’l Cond. 3.8(e) (“The prosecutor in a criminal case shall:… [e]xercise reasonable care to prevent investigators, law enforcement personnel, employees or other persons assisting or associated with the prosecutor in a criminal case from making an extrajudicial statement that the prosecutor would be prohibited from making under Rule 3.6.”). _See also In the Matter of Discipline of Leavitt_ , 2025 UT 46, ¶ 2, 579 P.3d 343 (“Rule 3.6 recognizes the risk that a lawyer’s public commentary, especially in a criminal case, may unfairly taint the jury pool or otherwise compromise the legal proceeding. As officers of the court, all attorneys have a responsibility to protect the integrity of the judicial process. This is especially true when the attorney is in a position of public trust, like an elected county attorney responsible for prosecuting criminal conduct.”; upholding disciplinary sanction against former Utah County Attorney for violating Rule 3.6).

statement, which offered the County Attorney’s personal opinion about the “senseless and needless” nature of the crime, and was clearly in the nature of a death penalty phase closing argument, highlighting the effect of the killing on Mr. Kirk’s family and on the State and Nation: 

The murder of Charlie Kirk is an American tragedy. Charlie Kirk was murdered while engaging in one of our most sacred and cherished American rights. The bedrock of our democratic Republic, the free exchange of ideas in a search for truth, understanding, and a more perfect union. It is also an offense against the state and to the peace and enjoyment of the people of Utah and of all those who visit here. But Charlie Kirk’s murder also strikes a more personal and intimate chord. Charlie Kirk was first and foremost a husband and a father to two beautiful young children. He was a son, he was a brother, and a friend. Like all murders, the senseless and needless taking of Charlie Kirk’s life has shattered the lives of those he loved and those who loved him. To Charlie’s wife, Erika, his two young children, his parents, his family, and his friends, I express my sincere condolences and offer my heartfelt prayers on your behalf. I also want to express my concern for everyone who was at Charlie’s Turning Point USA event at the Utah Valley University and all who have been impacted by this tragedy. 

As county attorney, I am charged with bringing justice to those who offend our laws. I am charged with bringing justice for those who harm… For those who are harmed. I am charged with bringing justice for Charlie Kirk. I am committed to these aims. I take this responsibility seriously. 

… Also, following the press conference, I am filing a notice of intent to seek the death penalty. I do not take this decision lightly, and it is a decision I have made independently as county attorney based solely on the available evidence and circumstances and nature of the crime. Because we are seeking 

8 The prosecutor added that “under our Constitution, the accused is presumed innocent until we the state prove to an impartial jury of defendant’s peers his guilt beyond a reasonable doubt. a platform for pundits and other attention seekers with varying levels of mental stability.” Dkt. 109, at 9. 

Respectfully, however, this statement does not go far enough. A more apt description of what is truly happening in this case with respect to prejudicial media coverage has been described by one legal researcher as a “Content Tornado.” _See_ Katie Grace Frisbee, _Caught In The Content Tornado: How To Protect Violent-Crime Trials From The Prejudicial Effects Of Live Streaming_ , 74 Duke Law J. 1053 (2025). As the author explains: 

The content tornado… refers to the whirlwind of fact, fiction, and opinion that emerges when violent-crime trials are live streamed on social media. This concept describes how live streamed trials generate emotional and moralized content that gets amplified by social media algorithms. The emotional nature of violent-crime trials—fueled by anger, grief, and public passion—draws viewers in, and as they engage with the content, they become more likely to produce their own secondary content on the subject. This user-generated secondary content—in the form of videos, comments, and posts—amplifies the tornado and mixes accurate information with misinformation, exaggerations, and public biases. 

_Id_ . at 1053 _. See also Murthy v. Missouri_ , 603 U.S. 43, 50 (2024) (“With their billions of active 

users, the world’s major social-media companies host a ‘staggering’ amount of content on their platforms.”); _United States v. Bowen,_ 799 F.3d 336, 357 (5th Cir. 2015) (warning against the “growing influence of online communications to mold public opinion in our society, and the danger of mob reactions.”) 

The devastating effect of the content tornado on the search for truth in this capital case is very well summarized 

Digital amplification intensifies legacy media pitfalls. Social platforms enable user-generated content—memes, threads, and live commentary—that evades editorial gatekeeping, propagating unverified rumors at viral speeds. Algorithms prioritize engagement via outrage, creating echo chambers where 70% of users encounter reinforcing narratives, per platform transparency reports, thus polarizing perceptions and pressuring judicial actors indirectly. 

30 

F. Longa, _The Presumption of Innocence and the Media An Examination of Fair Trial Rights in the Digital Era,_ 4 (2025):<sup>12</sup> 

This Court found in its October 27, 2025 order that “[t]his case has generated an immense amount of pre-trial publicity” and that “[a]s with many high-profile cases, public interest may both wax and wane, but it is likely to persist.” Dkt. 137, at 6. The Court’s finding that public interest in this case is “likely to persist” has certainly been borne out, as has the fact that the overwhelming amount of the most recent publicity has consisted of prejudicial pretrial publicity as defined above. Recent events have also demonstrated that the Court’s laudable efforts to put reasonable limits on the media have been to no avail. 

The futility of attempting to contain the “content tornado” in this case and to curb electronic media abuses through warnings and repositioning of cameras is nowhere better illustrated than the electronic media’s handling of the violation of the Court’s decorum order on December 11, 2025. As the Court knows, after the Court ruled that the issue concerning violation of the protective order was outside the scope of the closed hearing and must be addressed in public (SEALED Dkt. 273 at 54), the parties necessarily had to discuss the fact _on camera_ that the violation consisted in part in showing Mr. Robinson in shackles. _See_ Dkt. 256 at 7 (Visser: “It’s our understanding that his shackles are visible.”); _Id_ . at 8-9 (Hunt: “My understanding is that there was a zoom-out shot that did inadvertently show the shackles. That was improper. That was inadvertent. We apologize for that. And they’ve taken measures to make sure that the pool video that is disseminated to the rest of the pool does not contain any capture of the defendant in the shackles. We can’t do anything about the live-stream, of course, but we can control what the rest of the video is that we capture and disseminate. So that is being done.”); _Id_ . at 10 (Visser:

In light of the Media’s representations, and without having access to the offending video captures, the Court further ruled that “[t]he sanction for today is that this camera will be relocated”, and that “if there is further violations of the standing decorum order, this Court does have the right and will exercise termination of broadcast of these proceedings.” _Id_ . at 11.

The phrase “media circus”, often overused, is most typically identified with Judge Lance Ito’s mishandling of the OJ Simpson case.<sup>18</sup> But in this case, that phrase is particularly apt, at least in relation to the extent and content of the sensationalistic and prejudicial pretrial publicity that has thus far been produced in this case, and that will no doubt continue unrestrained unless this motion is granted. 

### **ARGUMENT** 

### **I. INTRODUCTION** 

This argument begins with three findings of fact the Court has already made: 

- (1) “The media interest in Mr. Robinson’s trial is arguably unprecedented in the State of Utah. Photographs and visual recordings of Mr. Robinson in jail clothing will likely be shared and disseminated in various public platforms. There is a strong likelihood that a substantial number of potential jurors in Utah County will view these images and may associate Mr. Robinson’s jail clothing with indicia of guilt. The danger of prejudice is compounded by technological advancements that allow the images to be manipulated in ways that do not accurately reflect the truth of the court proceedings” 

- (2) “This case has generated an immense amount of pre-trial publicity. As with many high- profile cases, public interest may both wax and wane, but it is likely to persist. Visual recordings and photographs of Mr. Robinson shackled may give the impression that he is dangerous and may undermine his presumption of innocence. The potential jury pool in Utah County, based on their proximity, is likely to be even more exposed to the images of these proceedings and the messages those images send.” 

- (3) “This case generates extraordinary ongoing national and international attention, some of which is threatening in nature to the parties, their representatives, and non-party participants (e.g. witnesses and court personnel).” 

Dkt. 137, at 5-6; Dkt. 266, at 2. 

18 _See, e.g._ , Andrew G.T. Moore II, _The O.J. Simpson Trial-Triumph of Justice or Debacle?_ , 41 ST. LOUIS U. L.J. 9, 10 (1996) (“Unfortunately, the [O.J. Simpson] trial became a stage for the jury, lawyers and judge to pursue their own self-serving purposes. With the defense attorneys claiming their client was the real ‘victim,’ the prosecution losing sight of its duty to present evidence fairly, a judge totally smitten with his own self-generated celebrity status, and jurors being discharged for a variety of problems, including misconduct, the whole proceeding became an embarrassing reflection of the American legal system.”). 

48 

“The problem is an ancient one. Mr. Justice Holmes stated no more than a commonplace when, two generations ago, he noted that ‘(a)ny judge who has sat with juries knows that, in spite of forms, they are extremely likely to be impregnated by the environing atmosphere.’” _Groppi v. Wisconsin_ , 400 U.S. 505, 510 (1971) (quoting _Frank v. Mangum,_ 237 U.S. 309, 349 (1915) (dissenting opinion)). 

Nowhere is this statement more true than when television cameras are allowed into the courtroom at all stages of a criminal case, and when television and internet news crews in the age of social media use livestream coverage as a platform for the speculations and falsehoods of those eager to obtain fame, fortune, or political advantage from the tragic events surrounding a notorious criminal case. 

The United States Supreme Court long ago stressed in _Estes v. Texas_ , that it was not just the electronic media that was responsible in that case for creating a media circus that deprived the defendant of a fair trial, but also the trial judge himself, who decided to allow electronic media coverage of a notorious criminal case and thereby unconstitutionally fanned the flames of prejudicial pretrial publicity: 

From the moment the trial judge announces that a case will be televised it becomes a _cause celebre_ . The whole community, including prospective jurors, becomes interested in all the morbid details surrounding it. The approaching trial immediately assumes an important status in the public press and the accused is highly publicized along with the offense with which he is charged. Every juror carries with him into the jury box these solemn facts and thus increases the chance of prejudice that is present in every criminal case. And we must remember that realistically it is only the notorious trial which will be broadcast, because of the necessity for paid sponsorship. The conscious or unconscious effect that this may have on the juror’s judgment cannot be evaluated, but experience indicates that it is not only possible but highly probable that it will have a direct bearing on his vote as to guilt or innocence. 

49 

### 381 U.S. at 545.<sup>19</sup> And in _Estes_ , 

The judge decided that the trial proceedings would be telecast. He announced no restrictions at the time. This emphasized the notorious nature of the coming trial, increased the intensity of the publicity on the petitioner and together with the subsequent televising of the trial beginning 30 days later inherently prevented a sober search for the truth. 

_Id_ . at 550-51. 

_Estes_ also addressed the effect of a judge allowing electronic media coverage of pretrial proceedings: 

It is contended that this two-day pretrial hearing cannot be considered in determining the question before us. We cannot agree. Pretrial can create a major problem for the defendant in a criminal case. Indeed, it may be more harmful than publicity during the trial for it may well set the community opinion as to guilt or innocence. Though the September hearings dealt with motions to prohibit television coverage and to postpone the trial, they are unquestionably relevant to the issue before us. All of this two-day affair was highly publicized and could only have impressed those present, and also the community at large, with the notorious character of the petitioner as well as the proceeding. 

_Id_ . at 565. 

This Court has the right and duty to prohibit or restrict broadcasts of court proceedings in 

the specific circumstances of this case under the federal and state constitutions, and under Rule 

> 19 The Court’s lead opinion in _Estes_ was written by Justice Clark and concurred in by Chief Justice Warren, and Justices Douglas and Goldberg. Chief Justice Warren wrote a separate concurring opinion in which Justices Douglas and Goldberg joined. Justice Harlan also wrote a concurring opinion and agreed with many of the points raised by Justice Clark and Chief Justice Warren and concluded that “[a]t the present juncture I can only conclude that televised trials, at least in cases like this one, possess such capabilities for interfering with the even course of the judicial process that they are constitutionally banned.” _Estes_ , 381 U.S. at 596 (Harlan, J. concurring). _See also Sinclair v. Turner_ , 434 P.2d 305, 307 n.6 (Utah 1967) (describing _Estes_ as a case where reversal was required because “there was a radical departure from the dignity and decorum proper to maintain in a courtroom, the main aspect of which was making a public drama of the trial by permitting televising and broadcasting during the trial.”); _Conder_ , 668 P.2d at 517 (citing Justice Clark’s lead opinion in _Estes_ as demonstrating the “potential prejudicial impact of televising jurors.”). 

50 

4-401.01(2)(A), (3)(B) and (5). Importantly, although Rule 4-401.01(2)(A) sets forth a state law rule-based presumption in favor of electronic media coverage if, but only if ,”the predominant purpose of the electronic media coverage request is journalism or dissemination of news to the 

public,” that presumption can be overcome based on “some or all” of the nine factors set forth in Paragraph 2(B). 

Under Paragraph 3(B), the court may also terminate or suspend electronic media coverage at any time without prior notice “if the judge finds that continued electronic media coverage is no longer appropriate based upon consideration of one or more of the factors in Paragraph (2)(B).” Finally, under Paragraph (5), upon finding that anyone violated “this rule or the court’s directives” the court may “terminate or suspend electronic media coverage[.]” Under Paragraphs (2)(C) and 2(D), and 3(B), a decision to prohibit or restrict media access made under Paragraph (2)(B) or Paragraph (3)(B) must be accompanied by particularized oral or written findings that relate to the specific circumstances of the proceedings “rather than merely reflect generalized views or preferences.” No such findings are required if the decision to prohibit or restrict media access is made under Paragraph (5).<sup>20</sup> 

20 No Utah court has yet interpreted Rule 4-401.01 and thus the Court is without any guidance as to how to apply the Rule. As a foundational objection, Mr. Robinson objects to the use of the Rule in this case because it violates the due process void for vagueness doctrine. _See_ Emily Ittner, _Technology In The Courtroom: Promoting Transparency Or Destroying Solemnity?_ , 22 COMMLAW CONSPECTUS 347, 374-375 (2014), _available at_ 

<u>https://scholarship.law.edu/cgi/viewcontent.cgi?article=1541&context=commlaw (“Utah’s rule</u> …does not provide sufficient guidance to judges to help them in deciding whether to prohibit or restrict coverage. The rule requires judges to consider “some or all” of nine factors when determining whether the presumption of electronic coverage has been overcome and should be prohibited or restricted. Offering a list of specific factors to aid judges in their decision making is a positive step. However, the rule is still ambiguous, in that it does not state how judges must weigh these factors in their determination of whether or not to limit or prohibit coverage.”). 

51 

Three separate issues thus arise under Rule 4-401.01. The first issue is whether the 

“predominant purpose” of the electronic media coverage is “journalism or dissemination of news to the public” Utah R. Jud. Admin. 4-401.01(2)(A). If it is not, then there is no statutory presumption in favor of electronic media coverage and no entitlement to such coverage. If it is, then the second issue is whether the presumption has been overcome under the facts set forth in Paragraph 2(B). The third issue is whether electronic media coverage should be prohibited or restricted under Paragraph (5) given the electronic media’s admitted violation of the Court’s decorum order, the consequences of that violation in causing the worldwide distribution of an electronic fake confession and of other bogus analyses, and its continued violation of that order as documented above. As argued below, these determinations can be made only after the Court considers both argument and evidence bearing on the factors, but in this extraordinary case, even without further evidence, the applicable factors and the electronic media’s violations of the decorum order weigh heavily in favor of prohibiting any further electronic coverage of this case. 

Moreover, regardless of how the Court determines these three state law issues, it is elementary but important to stress that a state statute or rule cannot trump the state or federal constitution principles discussed in this motion. _See United States v. Tsarnaev_ , 595 U.S. 302, 315-16 (2022) (“[S]upervisory rules cannot conflict with or circumvent a constitutional provision or federal statute” and “most relevant here, lower courts cannot create prophylactic supervisory rules that circumvent or supplement legal standards set out in decisions of this Court.”); _Chandler v. Florida_ , 449 U.S. 560, 582 (1981) (“We are not empowered by the Constitution to oversee or harness state procedural experimentation; _only when the state action infringes fundamental guarantees are we authorized to intervene.”_ (emphasis added)); _Estes_ , 381 U.S. at 566 (Warren, CJ, concurring) (noting with respect to the trial judge’s comments that “[t]his case 

52 

is not being tried under the Federal Constitution”, and that “[t]his Defendant has been brought into this Court under the state laws, under the State Constitution”, that “I find it difficult to believe that this trial judge, with over 20 years’ experience on the bench, was unfamiliar with the fundamental duty imposed on him by Article VI [the Supremacy Clause] of the Constitution of the United States.”).<sup>21</sup> 

The _constitutional_ question of whether, in the circumstances of this capital case, unlimited electronic media coverage of all aspects of this capital case should be permitted to continue when the media has violated both the letter and spirit of the Court’s decorum order, and when prejudicial electronic media pretrial publicity has been extreme and continuous and is 

unlikely to abate, cannot be answered by merely invoking a rule-based presumption or the media’s or the public’s “right of access.” 

The Supreme Court has repeatedly held that there is no constitutional right to have judicial proceedings recorded and broadcast. _Chandler_ , 449 U. S at 569; _Nixon v. Warner Communications, Inc.,_ 435 U.S. 589, 590 (1978) (“[T]here is no constitutional right to have … testimony recorded and broadcast.”); _Estes_ , 381 U.S. at 539-42, 583-84, 588 (same).<sup>22</sup> 

21 The same supremacy principle applies to the state constitution and to decisions based on the state constitution. _See State v. DeJesus_ , 2017 UT 22, ¶ 34, 395 P.3d 111 (“[O]ur constitutional decisions are set in relative stone in that [t]hey place matters resolved by them beyond the policy reach of this or other branches of government, and they establish precedent that we ourselves may be bound by under the doctrine of _stare decisis_ .” (internal quotations omitted)). 

> 22 After _Chandler_ , no Federal Circuit Court has opined that the Federal Constitution guarantees the media or the public a right to record or broadcast court proceedings. _See, e.g._ , _Westmoreland v. Columbia Broadcasting Sys., Inc.,_ 752 F.2d 16, 23-24 (2d Cir.1984) (“There is a long leap ... between a public right under the First Amendment to attend trials and a public right under the First Amendment to see a given trial televised.”) _; Whiteland Woods, L.P. v. Township of W. Whiteland_ , 193 F.3d 177, 183 (3d Cir.1999) (“The First Amendment does not require states to accommodate every potential method of recording its proceedings, particularly where the public is granted alternative means of compiling a comprehensive record.”); _United States v. Edwards_ , 785 F.2d 1293, 1295-96 (5th Cir.1986) (First Amendment does not guarantee a positive right to televise or broadcast criminal trials); _Conway v. United States_ , 852 F.2d 187, 188-89 (6th 

53 

The Supreme Court put the issue plainly and bluntly in _Nixon_ : 

The First Amendment generally grants the press no right to information about a trial superior to that of the general public. Once beyond the confines of the courthouse, a news-gathering agency may publicize, within wide limits, what its representatives have heard and seen in the courtroom. But the line is drawn at the courthouse door; and within, a reporter’s constitutional rights are no greater than those of any other member of the public. 

435 U.S. at 609 (internal quotation omitted). 

The present motion is not seeking to infringe either the media’s or the public’s “right of 

access” to Mr. Robinson’s trial. And it is that “right of access,” not any alleged but non-existent constitutional right of the electronic media to record and broadcast proceedings for profit or sensationalism, that serves important constitutional interests. _See Press-Enterprise Co. v. Superior Ct. of California, Riverside Cnty_ , 464 U.S. 501, 508 (1984) (“[T]he sure knowledge that _anyone_ is free to attend gives assurance that established procedures are being followed and that deviations will become known.” (emphasis in original)). 

Because, at most, the media in this case can assert only a “presumption” which is 

grounded in a court rule and not in a constitutional provision, for purposes of analyzing Mr. Robinson’s constitutional claims—any talk of a “strict scrutiny”, “compelling interest”, or “least intrusive means” standard has no place. _See, e.g._ , _Globe Newspaper Co. v. Superior Ct. for_ 

Cir.1988) (per curiam) (no First Amendment right to televise judicial proceedings); _United States v. Kerley_ , 753 F.2d 617, 621 (7th Cir.1985) (“exclusion of cameras from federal courtrooms is constitutional”); _Rice v. Kempker_ , 374 F.3d 675, 678 (8th Cir. 2004) (“[W]e hold that neither the public nor the media has a First Amendment right to videotape, photograph, or make audio recordings of government proceedings that are by law open to the public.”); _Radio & Television News Ass’n of S. California v. U.S. Dist. Ct. for Cent. Dist. of California_ , 781 F.2d 1443, 1447 (9th Cir. 1986) (“the media’s ‘right to gather information’ during a criminal trial is no more than a right to attend the trial and report on their observations”); _Combined Communications Corp. v. Finesilver_ , 672 F.2d 818, 821 (10th Cir. 1982) (“The First Amendment does not guarantee the media a constitutional right to televise inside a courthouse”); _United States v. Hastings_ , 695 F.2d 1278, 1280 (11th Cir. 1983) (right of access does not extend to “the right to televise, photograph, record, and broadcast federal criminal trials”). 

54 

_Norfolk Cnty_ , 457 U.S. 596, 607 n. 17 (1982) (“Of course, limitations on the right of access that resemble ‘time, place, and manner’ restrictions on protected speech, would not be subjected to …strict scrutiny.”); _United States v. Kerley_ , 753 F.2d 617, 620-21 (7th Cir. 1985) (“A limitation on the public access to a trial is not subject to the same “strict scrutiny” given a denial of access. All we have in the case before us is a limitation on the manner of news coverage; the media can do everything but televise the trial. The limitation can withstand constitutional scrutiny so long as it is reasonable and neutral, as with time, place, and manner restrictions generally.” (citing _Globe Newspaper Co_ and numerous other cases). 

Further, the Supreme Court has “always held that the atmosphere essential to the preservation of a fair trial—the most fundamental of all freedoms—must be maintained at all costs.” _Estes_ , 381 U.S at 540. _See also Press-Enterprise,_ 464 U.S. at 508 (“No right ranks higher than the right of the accused to a fair trial.”); _United States v. Tijerina_ , 412 F.2d 661, 667 (10th Cir. 1969) (“The [First Amendment] argument necessarily places freedom of speech in a preferred position above fair trial. Some decisions of the Supreme Court place First Amendment rights in a preferred position. This preferred position has never been approved in a case where a balance must be had between free speech and fair trial. Indeed, the Court has awarded the preference to fair trial.” (citation omitted).) In other words, from a constitutional perspective, a bare rule-based presumption can never outweigh Mr. Robinson’s compelling interest in a fair trial. 

Lastly, and most importantly, the fact that the State is seeking the death penalty in this case means that the Court must be especially vigilant to guard against the pernicious effects of prejudicial pretrial publicity, and do everything it can to ensure that every procedural safeguard 

55 

is observed. _See Gregg v. Georgia_ , 428 U.S. 153, 187 (1976) (“When a defendant’s life is at stake, the Court has been particularly sensitive to insure that every safeguard is observed.”) 

The President of the United States, the Vice President, the Attorney General, the 

Governor of the State of Utah, the County Attorney, law enforcement officials, and the electronic media have all condemned Mr. Robinson before his trial has even begun. The President has told a nationwide audience that “with a high degree of certainty, we have him”, that “I hope he is going to be found guilty”, that “I hope he gets the death penalty”, that “the depraved assassin who planned and executed Charlie’s killing has been arrested and charged with capital murder”, and that “ God willing, he will receive the full and ultimate punishment for his horrific crime.” 

_See supra_ , TIMES NEWS, _Donald Trump Confirms._ As the Supreme Cout put it long ago: 

With such an opinion permeating their minds, it would be difficult to say that each [juror] could exclude this preconception of guilt from his deliberations. The influence that lurks in an opinion once formed is so persistent that it unconsciously fights detachment from the mental processes of the average man. Where one’s life is at stake—and accounting for the frailties of human nature—we can only say that in the light of the circumstances here the finding of impartiality does not meet constitutional standards…. With his life at stake, it is not requiring too much that petitioner be tried in an atmosphere undisturbed by so huge a wave of public passion[.] 

_Irvin v. Dowd_ , 366 U.S. 717, 727-28 (1961) (internal citations omitted). 

Consistent with _Irvin_ , the Utah Supreme Court held in _State v. James_ , that the fact that 

the state is seeking the death penalty is an important factor in assessing the reasonable likelihood 

that media coverage will prejudice the right to a fair trial: 

This is a capital case. Not only will a jury be required to determine the guilt or innocence of defendant, but if guilt is found, the jury will probably be urged by the prosecution to impose the death penalty. In deciding whether to impose the death penalty, the jury must weigh aggravating circumstances against mitigating circumstances. This is the most momentous judgment a jury can be asked to make. The judgment should be made in an atmosphere as free from any taint of bias or prejudice as is reasonably possible. 

56 

767 P.2d 549, 555 (Utah 1989) _. See also State v. Arguelles_ , 2003 UT 1, ¶ 102, 63 P.3d 731 (“We… encourage trial judges…to continue to exercise caution in maintaining quiet and nondistracting courtroom environments in all cases, and particularly in capital proceedings.”); _Sampson v. United States_ , 724 F.3d 150, 163 (1st Cir. 2013) (“The right to an impartial jury is nowhere as precious as when a defendant is on trial for his life.”). 

The prejudicial electronic pretrial publicity in this case, with its obsession on courtroom attire and restraints, mugshots, demeanor, and other irrelevant and prejudicial factors, has imperiled not only Mr. Robinson’s constitutional right to an impartial guilt and penalty phase jury, but also his Eighth Amendment and article 1, section 9 rights to be free from cruel and unusual punishment. _See Beck v. Alabama_ , 447 U.S. 625, 638 (1980) (“To insure that the death penalty is indeed imposed on the basis of reason rather than caprice or emotion, we have invalidated procedural rules that tended to diminish the reliability of the sentencing determination. The same reasoning must apply to rules that diminish the reliability of the guilt determination.” (internal quotation omitted)); _Caldwell v. Mississippi_ , 472 U.S. 320, 340 (1985) (invalidating a procedure that was “incompatible with the Eighth Amendment’s heightened need for reliability in the determination that death is the appropriate punishment in a specific case.” (internal quotation omitted)). 

Indeed, the Supreme Court “has stressed the ‘acute need’ for reliable decisionmaking when the death penalty is at issue.” _Deck v. Missouri_ , 544 U.S. 622, 632 (2005). And it is well established that reliable decisionmaking must be grounded in admissible evidence, not on innuendo derived from the defendant’s custodial status, demeanor, or prejudicial pretrial publicity. _See_ , _Holbrook v. Flynn_ , 475 U.S. 560, 567 (1986) (“Central to the right to a fair trial, guaranteed by the Sixth and Fourteenth Amendments, is the principle that one accused of a crime is entitled to have 

57 

his guilt or innocence determined solely on the basis of the evidence introduced at trial, and not on grounds of official suspicion, indictment, continued custody, or other circumstances not adduced as proof at trial.”); _Estes_ , 381 U.S. at 564 (Warren, CJ, concurring) (“[T]he criminal trial under our Constitution has a clearly defined purpose, to provide a fair and reliable determination of guilt, and no procedure or occurrence which seriously threatens to divert it from that purpose can be tolerated.”) 

This Courts factual findings quoted above with respect to Mr. Robinson’s jail attire and shackles and the likely effect that publicity about such factors will have on a Utah County jury pool have important implications under the Sixth, Eighth, and Fourteenth Amendment to the United States Constitution, and under Utah Constitution, article I, sections 7 (due process), 9 (prohibition against cruel and unusual punishment), 10 (“In capital cases the right of trial by jury shall remain inviolate”), and 12 (right to counsel and to a public trial by an impartial jury). 

In light of the factual circumstances outlined above, continuing to broadcast the proceedings in this capital case threatens to undermine Mr. Robinson’s fundamental right to a fair and reliable trial and sentencing hearing by an impartial jury. The United States Supreme Court has noted that cameras in the courtroom can have a prejudicial effect on the performance of the jurors, the trial court judge, witnesses and the defendant. _See Estes_ , 381 U.S. at 545-50. 

While the United States Supreme Court has not adopted a per se ban on cameras in the courtroom for all cases, it has ruled that in notorious cases the broadcast of the proceedings constitutes prejudicial, reversible constitutional error. _See id_ .; _Chandler_ , 449 U.S. at 582. The present case is the type of case where broadcasting the case would cause irreparable bias and prejudice to Mr. Robinson. This case has been highly publicized and emotional since its 

58 

inception. Given the nature and history of the case, the exclusion of the electronic media is 

necessary to preserve Mr. Robinson’s rights under the United States and Utah Constitutions. 

- **II. GIVEN THE PARTICULAR CIRCUMSTANCES OF THIS CAPITAL CASE, WHERE THE AMOUNT OF PREJUDICIAL ELECTRONIC MEDIA PRETRIAL PUBLICITY IS ALREADY EXTREMELY HIGH AND IS LIKELY TO CONTINUE, AND WHERE THE ELECTRONIC MEDIA HAS ALREADY VIOLATED THE COURT’S DECORUM ORDER AND DIRECTLY CAUSED FURTHER PREJUDICE , CONTINUING TO ALLOW LIVE STREAMING OF ALL PROCEEDINGS WOULD INFRINGE MR. ROBINSON’S FUNDAMENTAL RIGHT TO A FAIR AND RELIABLE TRIAL AND SENTENCING HEARING BY AN IMPARTIAL JURY AS GUARANTEED BY THE SIXTH, EIGHTH, AND FOURTEENTH AMENDMENTS TO THE UNITED STATES CONSTITUTON AND THEIR UTAH CONSTITUTIONAL COUNTERPARTS.** 

   - **A. The Court Has a Constitutional Duty to Take Strong Measures to Ensure That Mr. Robinson’s Right to a Fair Trial by an Impartial Jury Is Protected From Prejudicial Electronic Pretrial Publicity.** 

The Utah Supreme Court has long noted, “It is well established that due process 

requirements of the Constitutions of Utah and the United States guarantee that an accused 

receive a trial before a fair and impartial jury free from outside influences.” _State v. Pierre_ , 572 P.2d 1338, 1348 (Utah 1977) (footnote omitted). Further, “[t]here can be no doubt that pretrial or during-trial publicity that comes to the attention of prospective or sitting jurors can deprive a criminal defendant of a fair trial. Appellate courts have been obliged to reverse convictions on this ground where the publicity was pervasive and prejudicial.” _Conder_ , 668 P.2d at 518. The United States Supreme Court has dealt with the detriments of excessive media involvement in cases by reversing a number of criminal convictions on the ground that excessive pretrial 

publicity, of the same or even lesser type and intensity as that present in this case, deprived the defendants of due process. _See Estes_ , 381 U.S. at 533-52 (1965) (holding that a defendant had been denied due process because a pre-trial hearing had been televised live and then rebroadcast, and because the court proceedings had been disrupted by the presence of the media); _Rideau_ , 373 U.S. at 723-27 (reversing a conviction after the defendant had been denied a change of 

59 

venue even though a local television station had broadcast his recorded confession three times, and 106,000 of the estimated 150,000-person community viewed the broadcast); _Irvin_ , 366 U.S. at 727-28 (reversing a conviction where pre-trial publicity distributed in the vicinity of the trial included, _inter alia_ , media accounts of the defendant’s juvenile record, his confessions, and previous court-martial proceedings); _Marshall v. United States_ , 360 U.S. 310 (1959) (per curiam) (reversing a conviction because seven of twelve jurors had been exposed to news accounts of evidence that was not admitted at trial). The leading case during this era, which identified the need for trial publicity reform and shaped the American Bar Association’s remedial measures, was _Sheppard v. Maxwell_ , 384 U.S. 333 (1966). _See supra_ , n. 2. There, the Court, on due process grounds, reversed the murder conviction of Sam Sheppard, whose high-profile trial had been preceded and pervaded by a media frenzy. _Id_ . at 363. Newspapers had documented Sheppard’s alleged refusal to cooperate with investigating officials and had published articles discussing incriminating evidence that was never admitted at trial. _Id_ . at 338-41. Furthermore, reporters had crowded the defense table at trial, making it very difficult for Sheppard to have private discussions with his counsel. _Id_ . Despite the chaotic conditions, the trial judge refused to allow a change of venue and failed to take steps to control the adverse effects of the publicity. _Id_ . at 354 n. 9, 358-59. 

The Supreme Court admonished the trial court in _Sheppard_ for its failure to control the 

extrajudicial publicity: 

The fact that many of the prejudicial news items can be traced to the prosecution, as well as the defense, aggravates the judge’s failure to take any action. Effective control of these sources—concededly within the court’s power—might well have prevented the divulgence of inaccurate information, rumors, and accusations that made up much of the inflammatory publicity[.] 

60 

_Id_ . at 361 (internal citation omitted).<sup>23</sup> The Court suggested how the trial judge could have minimized the prejudicial publicity, including proscribing extrajudicial statements by lawyers and other trial participants, requesting local officials to implement regulations with respect to the dissemination of trial information, and warning news media about the impropriety of publicizing material not introduced at the proceeding. _Id_ . at 361-62. Emphasizing the prejudicial effect of news media on fair trials, the Court iterated: 

Due process requires that the accused receive a trial by an impartial jury free from outside influences. Given the pervasiveness of modern communications and the difficulty of effacing prejudicial publicity from the minds of the jurors, the trial courts must take strong measures to ensure that the balance is never weighed against the accused.... [W]here there is a reasonable likelihood that prejudicial news prior to trial will prevent a fair trial, the judge should continue until the threat abates, or transfer it to another county not so permeated with publicity. 

_Id_ . at 362-63. Moreover, the Court recognized that repeatedly reversing convictions would not 

suffice as a long-term remedy for the harm of trial publicity. The Court recommended an 

alternative solution: 

But we must remember that reversals are but palliatives; the cure lies in those remedial measures that will prevent the prejudice at its inception. The courts must take such steps by rule and regulation that will protect their processes from prejudicial outside interferences. Neither prosecutors, counsel for defense, the accused, witnesses, court staff nor enforcement officers coming under the jurisdiction of the court should be permitted to frustrate its function. Collaboration between counsel and the press as to information affecting the fairness of a criminal trial is not only subject to regulation, but it is highly censurable and worthy of disciplinary measures. 

> 23 Here, in contrast to _Sheppard_ , the Court will search the substantial pretrial publicity in this case in vain for any indication that the defense lawyers representing Mr. Robinson have held press conferences or otherwise spoken to the media. 

61 

_Id_ . at 363.<sup>24</sup> 

Under _Sheppard_ and its progeny, this Court has a constitutional duty to construe Rule 4- 401.01 in a manner that adequately protects Mr. Robinson’s due process rights, including his right to a fair trial. _See Gannett Co. v. DePasquale_ , 443 U.S. 368, 378 (1979) (“a trial judge has an affirmative constitutional duty” to protect a criminal defendant’s due process rights). Because it is difficult to remedy any violation of Mr. Robinson’s constitutional rights after the fact, “our criminal justice system permits, and even encourages, trial judges to be over-cautious in ensuring that a defendant will receive a fair trial.” _Id_ . at 379 n. 6.; _also id._ at 379 (“And because of the Constitution’s pervasive concern for these due process rights, a trial judge may surely take 

protective measures even when they are not strictly and inescapably necessary.”). 

- **B. The Prejudicial Pretrial Electronic Media Coverage in This Case Clearly Meets the Threshold Standard Set Forth in** **_Sheppard_ for Invoking This Court’s Affirmative Constitutional Duty to Take Protective Measures to Protect Mr. Robinson’s Due Process Rights by Prohibiting Further Electronic Media Live Streaming.** 

### **1. The** **_Sheppard_ Standard Has Clearly Been Met.** 

_Sheppard’s_ strictures that trial courts must take “strong measures to ensure that the 

balance is never weighed against the accused”, and that “courts must take such steps by rule and regulation that will protect their processes from prejudicial outside interferences”, retain special vitality today, in the age of the widespread dissemination of information and disinformation on internet/social media platforms by those whose opinions hold special weight in our increasingly polarized society. For example, when President Trump, as a criminal defendant, used social media to make threats to and derogatory comments about trial participants in his pending federal 

24 Mr. Robinson discusses below why this and other traditional means short of prohibiting further electronic media coverage will not be effective in this unique case. 

62 

criminal case, the district court stepped in and issued a gag order after the President continued with such posts after being specifically warned not to do so. _See United States v. Trump_ , 88 F.4th 990, 998 (D.C. Cir. 2023) (“Before and after the district court’s warning, Mr. Trump repeatedly used his public platform to denigrate and attack those involved in the criminal case against him.”). 

In upholding the essential part of that gag order on appeal, and concluding that “the district court had the authority to prevent Mr. Trump from laundering communications concerning witnesses and addressing their potential trial participation through social media postings or other public comments”, the appellate court relied primarily on _Sheppard_ and its progeny, reasoning, “[W]hen a case involves extensive media coverage and public interest, or when the parties are trying the case in the media rather than the courtroom, a court cannot sit back and wait for a “carnival atmosphere” to descend before acting. Quite the opposite. _Id_ . at 1004 (citing _Sheppard_ , 384 U.S. at 356-63). Quoting _United States v. Nixon_ , 418 U.S. 683, 707 (1974), the _Trump_ Court noted, “[T]he primary constitutional duty of the Judicial Branch [is] to do justice in criminal prosecutions[.]” Thus, “[a]s part of that duty, courts must ‘prevent the prejudice’ to the trial process “at its inception.’” _Id_ . (quoting _Sheppard_ , 384 U.S. at 363) (citing to _Nebraska Press Ass’n. v. Stuart_ , 427 U.S. 539, 553 (1976) (The cure for prejudice to the trial “lies in those remedial measures that will prevent the prejudice at its inception.”)) Indeed, 

That is because waiting until the trial is over and reversing the conviction would be an ineffective, costly, and wasteful “palliative,” inflicting the additional burdens on a defendant and extra expenses on the taxpayers of a retrial in an already contaminated public atmosphere, with witness recall and evidence growing staler all the while. 

_Id_ . (quoting _Sheppard_ , 384 U.S. at 363). The _Trump_ Court thus instructed: 

As a result, courts have an ongoing obligation to ensure that speech about a criminal case does not divert the trial from the very purpose of a court system, 

63 

which is to adjudicate controversies, both criminal and civil, in the calmness and solemnity of the courtroom according to legal procedures. Due process demands that the conclusions to be reached in a case will be induced only by evidence and argument in open court, and not by any outside influence, whether of private talk or public print. _Id_ . at 351… (quoting _Patterson v. Colorado ex rel. Attorney General_ , 205 U.S. 454, 462… (1907)); The courts’ duty to protect trials from outside influence includes protecting court personnel from both the reality and the appearance of undue outside pressure. The Supreme Court “has recognized that the unhindered and untrammeled functioning of our courts is part of the very foundation of our constitutional democracy.” _Cox_ , 379 U.S. at 562[.]. 

_Id_ . at 1004-05 (additional internal citations and quotation marks omitted; cleaned up). _See also_ 

_Geders v. United States_ , 425 U.S. 80, 86-87 (1976) (“If truth and fairness are not to be sacrificed, 

the judge must exert substantial control over the proceedings.”). 

Significant to the present case, where President Trump has expressed his strong opinion 

through electronic media that Mr. Robinson is part of a “radical left” conspiracy, and a 

“depraved assassin”<sup>25</sup> who should be convicted and executed, the court also upheld the gag order 

in part because “President Trump’s words have real-world consequences.” _Trump_ , 88 F.4th at 

1011. The _Trump_ Court elaborated: 

The record also shows that former President Trump’s words have realworld consequences. Many of those on the receiving end of his attacks pertaining to the 2020 election have been subjected to a torrent of threats and intimidation from his supporters. A day after Mr. Trump’s “IF YOU GO AFTER ME, I’M COMING AFTER YOU!” post, someone called the district court and said: “Hey you stupid slave n[****]r[.] * * * If Trump doesn’t get elected in 2024, we are coming to kill you, so tread lightly b[***]h. * * * You will be targeted personally, publicly, your family, all of it.” Special Counsel Br. 5; _see United States v. Shry_ , No. 4:23-cr-413, ECF 1 at 3 (Criminal Complaint) (S.D. Tex. Aug. 11, 2023). The Special Counsel also has advised that he has received threats, and that a prosecutor in the Special Counsel’s office whom Mr. Trump has singled out for criticism has been “subject to intimidating communications.” Special Counsel Mot. 12. 

The former President has repeatedly attacked both the presiding judge and his law clerk in a New York state-law lawsuit. Since those attacks, the judge’s 

25 _See, e.g., supra_ , DIARIO AS, _TRUMP calls for DEATH PENALTY._ 

64 

chambers have been “inundated with hundreds of harassing and threatening phone calls, voicemails, emails, letters, and packages.” _New York v. Trump_ , No. 452564/2022, NYSCEF No. 1631 at 2 (N.Y. Sup. Ct. Nov. 3, 2023). In addition to threatening death or serious harm, callers have labeled the judge and clerk “Nazi[s],” “dirty Jews,” and child molesters. See _Trump v. Engoron_ , No. 2023-05859, NYSCEF No. 9, Ex. E at 3-5 (N.Y. App. Div. Nov. 22, 2023). 

Election officials involved in the 2020 election were subjected to similar attacks. One election official explained: “After the [then-]President tweeted at me by name, calling me out the way that he did, the threats became much more specific, much more graphic, and included not just me by name but included members of my family by name, their ages, our address, pictures of our home. Just every bit of detail that you could imagine. That was what changed with that tweet.” … Another state official explained that he avoided “commenting on most things publicly” because it was “the safest thing to do” after thenPresident Trump tweeted about him.… And a local election official had to evacuate his home after then-President Trump criticized his office and a supporter posted his address online. …. 

Likewise, after former President Trump publicly condemned and then fired a federal official for making statements reassuring the public about the 2020 election’s security, one of Mr. Trump’s campaign’s lawyers publicly stated that the official “should be drawn and quartered. Taken out at dawn and shot.” …. After receiving death threats, the official and his family had to evacuate their home.… 

Others too have had their lives turned upside down after coming within Mr. Trump’s verbal sights. For example, a temporary Georgia election worker testified before Congress that she and her daughter endured “horrible, racist threats” after then-President Trump falsely accused them of election misconduct.….. She testified that she “had to move out of [her] house because the FBI said it wasn’t safe.” ….. People would send messages “say[ing] things like, ‘We know where you live, and we’re coming to get you, n[****]r.’ “ …. Some would show up at her home to confront her, and one person even tried to force her way into the election worker’s mother’s home to effectuate a citizen’s arrest of the election worker. …. The election worker explained: “Do you know how it feels to have the President of the United States to target you? * * * [W]hen someone as powerful as the President of the United States eggs on a mob, that mob will come.” …. 

Mr. Trump himself recognizes the power of his words and their effect on his audience, agreeing that his supporters “listen to [him] like no one else.” Transcript of CNN’s Town Hall with Former President Donald Trump, CNN (May 11, 2023). 

65 

Based on that record, the district court made a factual finding that, “when Defendant has publicly attacked individuals, including on matters related to this case, those individuals are consequently threatened and harassed.” …. Mr. Trump has not shown that factual finding to be clearly erroneous, and we hold that the record amply supports it. 

_Id_ . at 1011-12 (internal citations to case filings and select committee transcript omitted). 

This finding is highly relevant in this case in assessing the prejudicial impact of 

statements made by President Trump and his supporters, including Vice President Vance, U.S. Attorney General Bondi, FBI Director Patel, Deputy FBI Director Bongino, White House staff member Miller, and Governor Cox, who specifically mentioned during his Meet the Press national broadcast that he was appearing at the request of the White House. _See Leavitt_ , 2025 UT 46, ¶ 32 (Important factors to consider in assessing the prejudicial impact of statements to the media are, among other things, “when the statement was made in relation to trial, the attention the case and comments received, and the _role and reputation of the speaker_ .” (emphasis added)). 

Notably, in the 2024 presidential election, President Trump received 66.65% of the 308,981 votes cast in Utah County, as compared to 27.82% received by Ms. Harris. _See Countywide Summary Results for Utah County_ , 2024 Gen. Election (Nov. 5, 2024), _available at_ <u>https://vote.utahcounty.gov/cms/uploads/Summary_Results_FINAL_ec1e91cd43.pdf. Statewide,</u> Mr. Trump received 59.38% of the vote, compared to Ms. Harris receiving 37.79% of the vote. _See 2024 General Election_ (Nov. 5, 2024), _available at_ 

<u>https://electionresults.utah.gov/results/public/Utah/elections/general11052024. As Mr. Trump</u> himself put it, his supporters “listen to [him] like no one else.” _Trump_ , 88 F.4th at 1011. 

In a national public opinion poll taken shortly after Mr. Kirk’s death, just 6% of Americans said political violence was not much of a problem (5%) or not a problem (1%). 87% said it is a problem, with a majority (59% of U.S. adults) saying political violence is a very big 

66 

problem and 28% calling it somewhat of a problem. Republicans were more likely than Democrats to say political violence was a very big problem (67% vs. 58%). _See_ David Montgomery, _What Americans really think about political violence_ , YOUGOV (Sep. 12, 2025, at 4:21 PM MT), https://today.yougov.com/politics/articles/52960-charlie-kirk-americans-political- <u>violence-poll</u> 

In a national public opinion poll conducted in November 2025, 61% of respondents to an NBC poll said they believed extreme rhetoric was to blame for the killing of Mr. Kirk while 28% said it was “caused by a disturbed person,” and just 4% said they felt that both factors had played a part. Republicans apportioned the blame 73% to 19% in favor of violent rhetoric, Democrats put it at 54% to 34%, and independents at 53% to 28%. _See_ Joe Sommerland, _Majority of Americans say ‘extreme political rhetoric’ led to the killing of Charlie Kirk, poll finds_ , INDEPENDENT (Nov. 17, 2025, at 13:09 ET), https://www.the- 

<u>independent.com/news/world/americas/us-politics/charlie-kirk-political-rhetoric-pollb2866842.html</u> 

Given these opinion polls, the fact that Mr. Trump’s popularity in Utah County and in Utah generally is extremely high, the vehemence, scope, and frequency of the attacks that he and his supporters have mounted against Mr. Robinson, and this Court’s existing findings of fact quoted above, it should be concluded that he easily meets the standard set forth in _Sheppard_ for determining whether a notorious criminal case has reached the point where the trial court has a duty to take “strong measures” to ensure a defendant’s fundamental right to a fair trial by an impartial jury. That standard is that the defendant need only show that there is “a reasonable likelihood that prejudicial news prior to trial will prevent a fair trial.” _Sheppard_ , 384 U.S. at 363. This is the same standard used by the Utah Supreme Court in assessing the need for a change of 

67 

venue. _See James_ , 767 P.2d at 555. And it is the same standard used in Rule 4-401.01(2)(B) to assess whether electronic media should be prohibited or restricted as a matter of state law. 

A full application of that standard to this case is set forth below in relation to the analysis of Rule 4-401.01(2)(B). Here, Mr. Robinson emphasizes a critical point which leaves no doubt that he has met the _Sheppard_ standard. More specifically, the Trump-related prejudicial publicity concerning Mr. Robinson’s alleged connection with the “radical left” is certainly highly prejudicial and is alone sufficient to satisfy the _Sheppard_ / _James_ reasonable likelihood standard , but it is only one of many judicially-recognized categories of prejudicial pretrial publicity that are present in this case. _See_ , fn. 4, _supra_ . It will be sufficient for present purposes to discuss only four of those categories. 

First, there are thousands of posts relating to the prosecutor’s allegation that Mr. 

Robinson confessed to his roommate and parents, and the alleged lip reader video confession has also been widely circulated. As noted above in _Rideau_ , the Supreme Court reversed a conviction solely because the media had publicized a similar video confession. 373 U.S. at 723-27. And as _Skilling_ and numerous other cases make clear, pretrial publicity about an alleged confession gives rise to a presumption of prejudice. _See, e.g._ , _Skilling_ , 561 U.S. at 382 (a presumption of juror prejudice is warranted where the pretrial publicity references a “confession or other blatantly prejudicial information of the type readers or viewers could not reasonably be expected to shut from sight.”); _Chicago Council of Laws. v. Bauer_ , 522 F.2d 242, 254 (7th Cir. 1975) (“DR 7-107(B)(3) prevents comments relative to confessions or statements by the accused. Disclosures concerning such matters are fraught with almost incurable prejudice.”). 

Second, the Utah Governor, FBI Director Patel, and FBI Deputy Bongino, and others have all commented that Mr. Robinson is not cooperating with law enforcement, and the 

68 

electronic media has dutifully broadcast these comments nationally, often with screen shots such as the following:

Hannity Staff, _HANNITY MONOLOGUE: This Out to be a Moment Where the Whole Country is United [WATCH]_ , HANNITY, at 1:14 (Sep. 16, 2025), https://hannity.com/media-room/hannity- <u>monologue-this-ought-to-be-a-moment-where-the-whole-country-is-united-watch/.</u> 

The Seventh Circuit’s decision in _Chicago Council of Laws. v. Bauer_ , is helpful on this point as well: 

Disclosures concerning such matters are fraught with almost incurable prejudice. They could infringe on a defendant’s Fifth Amendment privilege. In view of the natural tendency to draw an adverse inference from the assertion of the privilege and the careful attempts to avoid comment about such a choice during a trial, it seems that courts at least can require that attorneys who publicly discuss such matters must prove that their comment was not of the type that poses a serious and imminent threat of interference with a fair trial. 

522 F.2d at 254. 

69 

The State will not be able to produce such proof in this case. Discovery Bates 000007 at 

p. 20 indicates that on the evening of his arrest “Tyler Robinson declined to be interviewed 

without having an attorney present.” A video of the encounter at Bates 003996-R2 at timestamp

If you cannot afford a lawyer, one will be appointed for you before any questioning begins. And if you decide to answer questions now, you can stop at any time. Okay. Um, so are you, are you good to proceed? I’d love to talk to you.

These are facts that the Governor, the FBI Director, the FBI Deputy Director, and every other 

law enforcement officer who told the media that Mr. Robinson was refusing to cooperate with 

law enforcement knew or should have known. Yet not one governmental official who made such statements, or any of the electronic media persons who reported such statements, ever mentioned 

that Mr. Robinson had a Fifth and Sixth Amendment right to remain silent until his attorney was 

present, and that listeners should not draw an adverse inference from such silence. _See Doyle v._ 

70 

_Ohio,_ 426 U.S. 610, 619 (1976) (“We hold that the use for impeachment purposes of petitioners’ silence, at the time of arrest and after receiving _Miranda_ warnings, violated the Due Process Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment.”); _State v. Wiswell_ , 639 P.2d 146, 147 (Utah 1981) (“The continued attempts by the prosecutor to put the defendant’s silence before the jury after his having been advised of his right to remain silent amounts to prosecutorial misconduct. The references to defendant’s silence are fundamental error, which could have affected the result and are therefore prejudicial.”). _See also, Florida. v. Bostick_ , 501 U.S. 429, 437 (1991) (“[A]an individual may decline an officer’s request without fearing prosecution…. We have consistently held that a refusal to cooperate, without more, does not furnish the minimal level of objective justification needed for a detention or seizure.”). 

Third, another type of prejudicial electronic media coverage that gives rise to a presumption of prejudice in this case is the electronic media’s insistence on giving prominent attention to demeaning and grossly unfair characterizations. For example, President Trump has called Mr. Robinson a “monster”, and a “depraved assassin.”, Vice President Vance described Mr. Robinson as a person who is “everything that the far left wants from our young men [who] rejected the conservatism and the spirituality, the values of a small-town family [and]…moved into a small apartment, he became addicted to porn, he became addicted to hate, and he ended up sleeping with somebody who doesn’t know whether they’re a man or a woman”. U.S. Attorney General Bondi called Mr. Robinson a “horrible human being”. Governor Cox called Mr. Robinson an “evil human being” and described him as a person “deeply indoctrinated by leftist ideology”. A Retired FBI Agent Parker deemed Mr. Robinson a “true sociopath”. And political commentator and attorney Megyn Kelly called Mr. Robinson an “evil, evil human being”. 

71 

This type of character assassination has also been held condemned in numerous cases _See, e.g,_ , _United States v. Casellas-Toro_ , 807 F.3d 380, 387 (1st Cir. 2015) (prejudice presumed where “[l]ike the broadcasts of Rideau’s confession, the media here publicized ‘blatantly prejudicial information of the type readers or viewers could not reasonably be expected to shut from sight.’. The media reported rumors about Casellas’s character—that he was a drug user, threatened people with firearms, was involved in a hit-and-run vehicle accident, and bragged about assassinating the then-governor of Puerto Rico. The public took to Facebook and Twitter to publicly discuss Casellas’s case.” (quoting _Skilling_ , 561 U.S. at 382); _Bauer_ , 522 F.2d at 254 (“DR 7-107(B)(1) prohibits communication concerning ‘character, reputation, or prior criminal record.’ The possible prejudicial effect of negative comments on such matters is self-evident.”) 

Yet a fourth area of presumptive prejudice that is involved in this case is the numerous statements by the President, the Governor, and law enforcement officials stating their opinion as to Mr. Robinson’s guilt, or, in the case of the County Attorney, as to the existence of aggravating factors relating to the victim, and the propriety of the death penalty. _See Leavitt_ , 2025 UT 46, ¶ 61 (upholding disciplinary sanction against former Utah County District Attorney because “his expression of belief in Baum’s guilt, and his other inflammatory statements did a disservice to the legal system by creating a substantial risk of tainting the jury pool, thereby jeopardizing a criminal defendant’s constitutional right to a fair and impartial trial. Further, these comments impinged upon the presumption of innocence that is so important to our criminal justice system.”); _Id_ . ¶ 39 (“Leavitt’s position as the County Attorney made the potential for prejudice even higher. His statements likely carried more weight in the eyes of the public.”). 

As stated in _Bauer_ , “[t]he prohibition on opinion as to guilt or innocence is permissible since this is a traditional ethical requirement in the legal profession and such an opinion could 

72 

never properly be offered in court.” 522 F.2d at 254. The same statement is also obviously true with respect to a statement of opinion about aggravating factors relating to the victim or the appropriateness of the death penalty, either by lawyers, or of even more concern here, by influential politicians and the victim’s family. _See Leavitt_ , 2025 UT 46, ¶ 38 (“Additionally, Leavitt made the remarks at a live-streamed press conference in which he announced that his Office was seeking the death penalty against Baum. This was a high-profile case that had already received much media attention. And the press was in attendance that day. So Leavitt knew that his statements would be broadcasted and reported on, including in Utah County where the prospective jury pool lived.”); _Weaver v. Bowersox_ , 438 F.3d 832, 840-41 (8th Cir. 2006) (petitioner was entitled to habeas relief based in part on the prosecutor’s improper statements during closing argument in the penalty phase “about his personal belief in the death penalty”; concluding that “[s]]tatements about the prosecutor’s personal belief in the death penalty are inappropriate and contrary to a reasoned opinion by the jury,” and noting that “[a] prosecutor should not emphasize his or her position of authority in making death penalty determinations because it may encourage the jury to defer to the prosecutor’s judgment.”); _Bates v. Bell,_ 402 F.3d 635, 644 (6th Cir. 2005) (“In the capital sentencing context, prosecutors are prohibited from expressing their personal opinion as to the existence of aggravating or mitigating circumstances and the appropriateness of the death penalty. Jurors are mindful that the prosecutor represents the State and are apt to afford undue respect to the prosecutor’s personal assessment.”). _See also_ , _supra_ n. 16. 

In sum, the prejudicial pretrial electronic media coverage in this case clearly meets the threshold standard set forth in _Sheppard_ for invoking this Court’s affirmative constitutional duty to take protective measures to protect Mr. Robinson’s due process rights. 

73 

**2. The United States Supreme Court’s Camera-In-The Courtroom Cases Strongly Support Granting This Motion and Establish Mr. Robinson’s Right to an Evidentiary Hearing to Present Empirical Data and Other Evidence in Support of This Motion.** 

As indicated above, the Supreme Court first addressed the broadcasting of criminal 

proceedings in _Estes v. Texas,_ 381 U.S. 532 (1965). There, the Court reversed a state court defendant’s criminal conviction because it found that the televised broadcasting of the trial had created such a strong likelihood of prejudice that it violated the defendant’s due process rights under the Fourteenth Amendment. _Id_ . at 542. Justice Clark’s lead opinion began by noting that 

At the outset the motion should be dispelled that telecasting is dangerous because it is new. It is true that our empirical knowledge of its full effect on the public, the jury or the participants in a trial, including the judge, witnesses and lawyers, is limited. However, the nub of the question is not its newness but, as Mr. Justice Douglas says, ‘the insidious influences which it puts to work in the administration of justice.’ Douglas, _The Public Trial and the Free Press_ , 33 Rocky Mt. L. Rev. 1 (1960). 

_Id_ . at 541. 

The Court then noted the “numerous situations in which [televising a trial] might cause actual unfairness—some so subtle as to defy detection by the accused or control of the judge.” _Id_ . at 544-45. In particular, it explained that broadcasting criminal trials might undermine a court’s ability to invoke Rule 615 of the Federal Rules of Evidence, which permits the exclusion of potential witnesses from the courtroom to prevent them from being influenced by others’ testimony. Broadcasting would enable excluded witnesses to hear others’ testimony and adjust their own testimony accordingly. _Id_ . at 547. Likewise, broadcasting would amplify the risk that jurors could be tainted from exposure to the coverage. _Id_ . at 545-46. Finally, the Court noted that cameras in the courtroom could subject trial participants—jurors, witnesses, attorneys, and judges—to psychological pressures from the knowledge that their actions would be reproduced 

74 

and disseminated publicly, potentially distorting the trial process by causing participants to conduct themselves differently. _Id_ . at 545-50. 

As indicated above, Justice Harlan wrote a concurring opinion and agreed with many of the points raised by Justice Clark’s lead opinion and Chief Justice Warren’s concurring opinion and he ultimately concluded that “[a]t the present juncture I can only conclude that televised trials, at least in cases like this one, possess such capabilities for interfering with the even course of the judicial process that they are constitutionally banned.” _Estes_ , 381 U.S. at 596 (Harlan, J. concurring). 

Subsequently, in _Chandler v. Florida_ , the Supreme Court clarified that “ _Estes_ did not announce a constitutional rule that all photographic or broadcast coverage of criminal trials is inherently a denial of due process.” 449 U.S. at 573-74. In the Court’s view, the critical language in Justice Harlan’s concurring opinion that resulted in a five member majority was his statement at the beginning of his opinion that “‘[m]y conclusion is that there is no constitutional requirement that television be allowed in the courtroom, and, at least _as to a notorious criminal trial such as this one_ , the considerations against allowing television in the courtroom so far outweigh the countervailing factors advanced in its support as to require a holding that what was done in this case infringed the fundamental right to a fair trial assured by the Due Process Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment.’” _Id_ . at 571 (quoting _Estes_ , 381 U.S. at 587 (Harlan, J. concurring) (emphasis added)). 

In _Chandler_ , which was a routine burglary case, the Court upheld the facial constitutionality of an experimental program in Florida allowing electronic media coverage in any criminal case, even over a defendant’s objection. 449 U.S. at 566. The defendant contended that the program was unconstitutional because _Estes_ established a per se ban against electronic 

75 

recordings in all cases, regardless of circumstances. _Id_ . at 570. The Court rejected that contention and upheld the Florida program because it contained certain essential procedural safeguards. _Id_ . at 566. Most importantly, it provided that “[t]he judge has sole and plenary discretion to exclude coverage of certain witnesses, and the jury may not be filmed. The judge has discretionary power to forbid coverage whenever satisfied that coverage may have a deleterious effect _on the paramount right of the defendant to a fair trial_ .” _Id_ . (emphasis added). The Court further stressed that “Florida admonishes its courts to take special pains to protect certain witnesses—for 

example, children, victims of sex crimes, some informants, and even the very timid witness or party—from the glare of publicity and the tensions of being ‘on camera’”, and that “the Florida guidelines place on trial judges positive obligations to be on guard to protect the _fundamental right of the accused to a fair trial_ .” _Id_ . at 576-77. The Court also noted that the program guaranteed the right to an evidentiary hearing: 

Inherent in electronic coverage of a trial is a risk that the very awareness by the accused of the coverage and the contemplated broadcast may adversely affect the conduct of the participants and the fairness of the trial, yet leave no evidence of how the conduct or the trial’s fairness was affected. Given this danger, it is significant that Florida requires that objections of the accused to coverage be heard and considered on the record by the trial court. In addition to providing a record for appellate review, a pretrial hearing enables a defendant to advance the basis of his objection to broadcast coverage and allows the trial court to define the steps necessary to minimize or eliminate the risks of prejudice to the accused. Experiments such as the one presented here may well increase the number of appeals by adding a new basis for claims to reverse, but this is a risk Florida has chosen to take after preliminary experimentation. Here, the record does not indicate that appellants requested an evidentiary hearing to show adverse impact or injury. Nor does the record reveal anything more than generalized allegations of prejudice. 

_Id._ at 577 (internal citation omitted).
