The Court went on to observe that the existing empirical studies in favor of allowing 

electronic media coverage of trials were “limited” and “non-scientific”. _Id._ at 576, n. 11 (citation 

76 

omitted). The Court went on to observe that “further experimentation is necessary to evaluate the potential psychological prejudice associated with broadcast coverage of trials. Further 

developments and more data are required before this issue can be finally resolved.” _Id_ . at 579, n. 

12. 

The Court further stressed the need for the defendant to present evidence by observing: 

To say that the appellants have not demonstrated that broadcast coverage is inherently a denial of due process is not to say that the appellants were in fact accorded all of the protections of due process in their trial. As noted earlier, a defendant has the right on review to show that the media’s coverage of his case—printed or broadcast—compromised the ability of the jury to judge him fairly. Alternatively, a defendant might show that broadcast coverage of his particular case had an adverse impact on the trial participants sufficient to constitute a denial of due process. Neither showing was made in this case. 

_Id_ . at 581. 

Finally, the Court made it clear that it was distinguishing the routine burglary case before 

it from the kind of “notorious case” that five members of the Court in ruled could not 

constitutionally be televised: 

The concurring opinion of Chief Justice Warren joined by Justices Douglas and Goldberg in _Estes_ can fairly be read as viewing the very broadcast of some trials as potentially a form of punishment in itself —a punishment before guilt. This concern is far from trivial. But, whether coverage of a few trials will, in practice, be the equivalent of a “Yankee Stadium” setting—which Justice Harlan likened to the public pillory long abandoned as a barbaric perversion of decent justice—must also await the continuing experimentation. 

_Id_ . at 580-81. 

The risk of prejudice to particular defendants is ever present and must be examined carefully as cases arise. Nothing of the “Roman circus” or “Yankee Stadium” atmosphere, as in _Estes_ , prevailed here, however, nor have appellants attempted to show that the unsequestered jury was exposed to “sensational” coverage, in the sense of _Estes_ or of _Sheppard v. Maxwell_ , 384 U.S. 333…. Absent a showing of prejudice of constitutional dimensions to these defendants, there is no reason for this Court either to endorse or to invalidate Florida’s experiment. 

_Id_ . at 582. 

77 

_Chandler_ is important to the present case for four reasons. 

**_First_** , it establishes that to pass muster under the federal constitution, a local rule 

permitting electronic media coverage over the defendant’s objection must contain certain procedural safeguards, including the right to an evidentiary hearing and a standard guiding the decision that provides a trial court with “discretionary power to forbid coverage whenever satisfied that coverage may have a deleterious effect _on the paramount right of the defendant to a fair trial_ .” _Id_ . at 566 (emphasis added). 

**_Second_** , it establishes that independent of any state rule, a defendant has a due process right to an evidentiary hearing to demonstrate that “the media’s coverage of his case—printed or broadcast—compromised the ability of the jury to judge him fairly. Alternatively, a defendant might show that broadcast coverage of his particular case had an adverse impact on the trial participants sufficient to constitute a denial of due process. Neither showing was made in this case.” _Id_ . at 581. 

**_Third_** , _Chandler_ expressly recognized in 1981 that “further experimentation [was] 

necessary to evaluate the potential psychological prejudice associated with broadcast coverage of trials”, and that “[f]urther developments and more data are required before this issue can be finally resolved.” _Id_ . at 579, n. 12. As Exhibit 2, the Declaration of Dr. Christine L. Ruva, PH.D, demonstrates, the requested studies have now been done by her and other researchers. Under _Chandler_ , Mr. Robinson is entitled to present the results of those studies and other evidence through the testimony of Dr. Ruva and by other means. 

**Lastly** , _Chandler_ establishes that the consensus reached by five members of the Court in _Estes_ that electronic media coverage is unconstitutional per se in notorious cases is still good 

78 

law. By any definition, the present case is a “notorious case” within the meaning of _Estes_ and therefore electronic media coverage is banned under the federal constitution. 

This last conclusion is supported by the most recent case in which the Supreme Court has considered the issue of cameras in the courtroom. _See Hollingsworth v. Perr_ y, 558 U.S. 183 (2010) (per curiam). In that case, the Court granted a stay of an order of the United States District Court for the Northern District of California permitting audio and video broadcast of a bench trial to a number of federal courthouses around the country. _Id_ . at 184. The order had been made pursuant to a hastily adopted local rule that enacted without sufficient opportunity for notice and comment. _Id_ . at 184-85. The Court agreed that the local rule was invalid for this reason. _Id._ at 199. The importance of _Hollingsworth_ for the present case lies in the reasons the Court articulated for why public comment on the rule was important, and the court’s discussion of cameras in the courtroom. 

According to _Hollingsworth_ , “[t]he question whether courtroom proceedings should be broadcast has prompted considerable national debate. Reasonable minds differ on the proper resolution of that debate and on the restrictions, circumstances, and procedures under which such broadcasts should occur. We do not here express any views on the propriety of broadcasting court proceedings generally.” _Id_ . at 189. Despite this general comment, the Court went on to observe that: 

The need for a meaningful comment period was particularly acute in this case. Both courts and legislatures have proceeded with appropriate caution in addressing this question. In 1996, the Judicial Conference of the United States adopted a policy opposing the public broadcast of court proceedings. This policy was adopted after a multiyear study of the issue by the Federal Judicial Center which drew on data from six district and two appellate courts, as well as state-court data. In light of the study’s findings, the Judicial Conference concluded that “the intimidating effect of cameras on some witnesses and jurors [is] cause for concern.” … 

79 

In more than a decade since its adoption the Judicial Conference has continued to adhere to its position on the broadcast of court proceedings. While the policy conclusions of the Judicial Conference may not be binding on the lower courts, they are “at the very least entitled to respectful consideration.” …Before abandoning its own policy—one consistent with the Judicial Conference’s longstanding views—it was incumbent on the District Court to adopt a proposed rule only after notice and an adequate period for public comment. 

_Id_ . at 193-94 (internal citations omitted). The Court ultimately concluded: 

Applicants also have shown that irreparable harm will likely result from the denial of the stay. Without a stay, the District Court will broadcast the trial. It would be difficult—if not impossible—to reverse the harm from those broadcasts. The trial will involve various witnesses, including members of same-sex couples; academics, who apparently will discuss gender issues and gender equality, as well as family structures; and those who participated in the campaign leading to the adoption of Proposition 8. _This Court has recognized that witness testimony may be chilled if broadcast. See Estes v. Texas, 381 U.S. 532, 547, 85 S.Ct. 1628, 14 L.Ed.2d 543 (1965); id., at 591, 85 S.Ct. 1628 (Harlan, J., concurring)_ . Some of applicants’ witnesses have already said that they will not testify if the trial is broadcast, and they have substantiated their concerns by citing incidents of past harassment. …These concerns are not diminished by the fact that some of applicants’ witnesses are compensated expert witnesses. There are qualitative differences between making public appearances regarding an issue and having one’s testimony broadcast throughout the country. Applicants may not be able to obtain adequate relief through an appeal. The trial will have already been broadcast. It is difficult to demonstrate or analyze whether a witness would have testified differently if his or her testimony had not been broadcast. And witnesses subject to harassment as a result of broadcast of their testimony might be less likely to cooperate in any future proceedings. 

_Id_ . at 195-96 (emphasis added) (additional internal citation omitted). 

The Court concluded that a central flaw in the local rule was that “[t]he District Court here 

attempted to revise its rules in haste, contrary to federal statutes and the policy of the Judicial Conference of the United States. _It did so to allow broadcasting of this high-profile trial without any considered standards or guidelines in place._ ” _Id_ . at 196 (emphasis added). 

The fact that the trial was of a high-profile case was of significance because “If Local 

Rule 77-3 had been validly revised, questions would still remain about the District Court’s 

80 

decision to allow broadcasting of this particular trial, in which several of the witnesses have stated concerns for their own security. Even districts that allow trials to be broadcast . . . recognize that a district judge’s discretion to broadcast a trial is limited, … _Id_ . at 198 (internal citations omitted) (citing _Hamilton v. Accu-Tek_ , 942 F. Supp. 136, 138 (E.D.N.Y.1996) (broadcast forbidden unless “there is no interference with the due process, the dignity of litigants, jurors and witnesses, or with other appropriate aspects of the administration of justice”)). The Court noted distinguishing factors for allowing broadcast of proceedings: “ _the basis that those cases were not high profile_ ,” _id_ . (citing _E*Trade Financial Corp. v. Deutsche Bank AG_ , 582 F. Supp. 2d 528, 535 (S.D.N.Y. 2008)); “ _or did not involve witnesses_ ”, _id_ . (citing _Marisol A. v. Giuliani_ , 929 F. Supp. 660, 661 (S.D.N.Y. 1996); _Katzman v. Victoria’s Secret Catalogue_ , 923 F. Supp. 580, 586-87 (S.D.N.Y. 1996)). 

The Court highlighted one court that “did not allow the broadcasting of its proceedings because the case ‘involv[ed] very sensitive issues.’” _Id_ . (citing _Schoeps v. Museum of Modern Art_ , 599 F. Supp. 2d 532, 534 (S.D.N.Y. 2009)). The Court found that like _Schoeps_ , the matter “involve[d] issues subject to intense debate in our society” and that “[t]he District Court intends not only to broadcast the attorneys’ arguments but also witness testimony.” _Id_ . And thus, the case was “not a good one for a pilot program. Even the studies that have been conducted thus far have not analyzed the effect _of broadcasting in high-profile_ , divisive cases.” _Id_ . (emphasis added). 

_Hollingsworth_ establishes that at least five members of the present Court believe that Justice Harlan’s concurring opinion in _Estes_ is speaking for “the Court”, and that these same five members share the view of the _Estes_ majority that televising of a notorious case is improper. In any event, however one reads _Hollingsworth_ , the constitutional rule established by five members of the Court in _Estes_ is that the televising of a notorious case is unconstitutional. Again, because 

81 

of its nature and the extent of prejudicial pretrial publicity in this case, it must be categorized as a notorious case within the meaning of _Estes_ , and _Estes_ must be followed. _See Bosse_ , 580 U.S. at 3 (“[I]t is this Court’s prerogative alone to overrule one of its precedents…..Our decisions remain binding precedent until we see fit to reconsider them, regardless of whether subsequent cases have raised doubts about their continuing vitality.”(internal quotation and citations omitted) (Thomas, J., concurring)). 

**3. Lower Court Decisions Also Support the Constitutional Necessity of Protecting Mr. Robinson’s Due Process Rights by Prohibiting Further Electronic Media Live Streaming OR Photographing** 

The only comparable recent cases in America where a President and his supporters have 

been so directly involved in attempting to influence a criminal prosecution or in being the source of negative pretrial publicity are the _Trump_ case discussed above, the Timothy McVeigh and 

Terry Nichols cases in Oklahoma, and the 9/11 trial of Zacarias Moussaoui in Virginia. All four of these cases strongly support granting the present motion on constitutional grounds. 

Initially, in _United States v. McVeigh_ , where, as here, “national media coverage of the [tragedy] was extremely comprehensive”, “[t]he immediate reactions of the President, the Attorney General, and an FBI spokesman were broadcast across the nation”, “[t]here were extensive interviews with injured victims, members of the families of the dead …and residents of Oklahoma City”, and “[t]he Governor of the State of Oklahoma was a continual presence in the media coverage”, Judge Matsch considered whether to grant a change of venue under the standard set forth in Federal Rule of Criminal Procedure Rule 21(a), which is even higher than that set forth in _Sheppard_ and _James_ .<sup>26</sup> 918 F. Supp. 1467, 1470-71 (W.D. Okla. 1996). He 

> 26 Fed. R. Crim. P. 21(a) provides: 

> The court upon motion of the defendant shall transfer the proceeding as to that defendant to another district whether or not such district is specified in the 

82 

concluded based on the review of the publicity summarized above that there is “so great a prejudice against these two defendants in the State of Oklahoma that they cannot obtain a fair and impartial trial at any place fixed by law for holding court in that state. The court also finds and concludes that an appropriate alternative venue is in the District of Colorado.” _Id_ . at 1474. Of course, such an inter-state transfer is not an option in Mr. Robinson’s case and as discussed below other traditional options are also unavailable, leaving the termination of electronic media coverage the one thing the Court can do to lessen the flow of electronic media coverage. 

Notably, the court in _McVeigh_ did not abandon its duty under _Sheppard_ to take strong remedial measures under some misguided notion that the existing prejudicial publicity was already so pervasive and widespread that it was futile to take any remedial action. _See Sheppard_ , 384 U.S. at 363. Nor did the court simply adopt the preferences of the numerous victims’ families. Instead, the court indicated that although he was “acutely aware of the wishes of the victims of the Oklahoma City explosion to attend this trial and that it will be a hardship for those victims to travel to Denver”, he was obligated to rule upon a proper weighing of constitutional interests that “[t[he interests of the victims in being able to attend this trial in Oklahoma are outweighed by the court’s obligation to assure that the trial be conducted with fundamental fairness and with due regard for all constitutional requirements.” _McVeigh,_ 918 F. Supp. at 147475. 

Subsequently, Judge Matsch prevented electronic coverage of the trial because he was required to do so under the strict ban on electronic coverage imposed by Federal Rule of 

defendant’s motion if the court is satisfied that there exists in the district where the prosecution is pending so great a prejudice against the defendant that the defendant cannot obtain a fair and impartial trial at any place fixed by law for holding court in that district. 

83 

Criminal Procedure 53.<sup>27</sup> _See United States v. McVeigh_ , 931 F. Supp. 753, 754 (D. Colo. 1996). In the midst of continued pervasive publicity after the case was transferred to Colorado, he continued to discharge his duty under _Sheppard_ , imposing on the attorneys “a blanket bar on out of court comments because no lesser restriction would adequately protect against a substantial likelihood of prejudicing the proceedings”, and finding that “[t]he compelling interest is the protection of jurors from being influenced by any such statements which may break through the barriers constructed by them under the court’s instructions.” _United States v. McVeigh_ , 964 F. Supp. 313, 316 (D. Colo. 1997). As indicated below, a variation of that remedy has been used in this case and has had no effect on the excesses of the electronic media. 

Following the conviction of both McVeigh and Nichols in federal court, and the imposition of a death verdict on McVeigh but not on Nichols, the State of Oklahoma filed a capital prosecution against Nichols. _See Nichols v. Dist. Ct. of Oklahoma Cnty_ , 2000 OK 12, ¶ 2, 6 P.3d 506. Numerous media organizations filed a motion asking the trial judge to permit them to televise the preliminary hearing, trial, and other proceedings in this case. Both the State and the defense objected to the media’s motion. _Id_ . 

On May 8, 2000, the trial judge issued an order granting the Media the right to broadcast 

any and all court proceedings in this matter. _Id_ . The trial judge acknowledged that Canon 

27 Fed. R. Crim. P. 53 provides: 

Except as otherwise provided by a statute or these rules, the court must not permit the taking of photographs in the courtroom during judicial proceedings or the broadcasting of judicial proceedings from the courtroom. 

As explained in _United States v. Al-Marimi_ , 761 F. Supp. 3d 16, 23 (D.D.C. 2024), “the only legal challenges to Rule 53 have been filed by criminal defendants or media organizations. In each of those cases, courts concluded that Rule 53’s ban on broadcasting is both constitutional and justified given the serious risks broadcasting poses to both the defendant’s and government’s right to a fair trial.” 

84 

3(B)(9)(e)(2) of the Code of Judicial Conduct explicitly prohibited broadcast over the objection of the defendant. _Id_ . However, he determined the Canon violated the State and Federal Constitutions and therefore need not control his decision. _Id_ . He found the trial judge or 

presiding judge has final authority to order, manage and control the courtroom and that camera coverage would not adversely affect Petitioner’s right to a fair trial. _Id_ . He made this finding despite the overwhelming prejudicial publicity concerning Nichols, including that he had been convicted and sentenced in federal court. 

Ruling on a pre-preliminary hearing for a writ of mandamus to overturn the ruling, the Court of Criminal Appeals of Oklahoma overturned the trial court’s order on constitutional 

grounds. _Id_ . ¶ 10. The Court placed primary reliance on the _Estes_ decision: 

In _Estes_ , the U.S. Supreme Court found the use of television, however, “cannot be said to contribute materially to [the] objective of a fair trial.” [381 U.S. at 544]. “Rather its use amounts to the injection of an irrelevant factor into court proceedings. In addition experience teaches that there are numerous situations in which it might cause actual unfairness-some so subtle as to defy detection by the accused or control by the judge.” [ _Id_ . at 544-45]. The potential impact of television on the jurors, the possibility the quality of the testimony in criminal trials may be impaired, the additional responsibilities the presence of television places on the trial judge, and the impact of courtroom television on the defendant were among the concerns mentioned by the Court. [ _Id_ . at 545549]. 

The requirement of a public trial is satisfied by the opportunity of members of the public and the press to attend the trial and to report what they have observed. [ _Nixon, Inc._ , 435 U.S. at 610]. No constitutional provision guarantees a right to televise trials. First Amendment rights of the press are subject to reasonable time, place, and manner restrictions. [ _Hastings_ , 695 F.2d at 1282. The federal courts prohibit televising of trials by specific rule. _See_ Rule 53, Federal Rules of Criminal Procedure. 

Television is a powerful medium. At its best, it educates and informs. At its worst, it has the potential for “distorting the integrity of the judicial process bearing on the determination of the guilt or innocence of the accused, and, more particularly, for casting doubt on the reliability of the fact-finding process carried on under such conditions.” _Estes_ , 381 U.S. at 592[ ] (Harlan J., concurrence). Petitioner’s fundamental right to a fair trial assured by the Due 

85 

Process Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment takes precedence over the interest of the press in televising the proceedings in this case. 

_Id_ . ¶¶ 6-8. 

After finding that allowing electronic coverage would also violate the state constitution, 

the court concluded that “[i]n order to ensure the solemnity of the judicial proceedings, to minimize the potential tainting of a possible future jury pool, to curtail outside interference with these criminal proceedings, and to ensure the reliability of the result ultimately determined in this case”, the application for a writ was granted and the trial court’s order was vacated. _Id_ . ¶ 10. As in _McVeigh_ , the court found no significance in the fact that by the time the court issued its ruling, Nichols’s case had already been exposed to massive nationwide publicity. 

Similarly, in _United States v. Moussaoui_ , Court TV filed a motion to record and telecast the pretrial and trial proceedings. 205 F.R.D. 183, 184 (E.D. Va. 2002). Stressing the “extraordinary national and international public interest in this prosecution, which involves allegations that the defendant was a member of the al Qaeda conspiracy to blow up the World Trade Center in Manhattan and government buildings in the Washington, D.C. area”, COURT TV asked the court to “ignore or declare unconstitutional the well-established ban on 

photographing and broadcasting federal criminal pretrial and trial proceedings.” _Id_ . The United States vigorously opposed the constitutional challenge to the ban and raised “compelling objections to the photographing and broadcasting of these proceedings in any format.” _Id_ . Defendant opposed any broadcast of pretrial proceedings out of concern that it might taint the potential jury pool, and he opposed any televised replay of the trial if the jury was not sequestered; if the jury was sequestered, he supported Court TV’s motion. _Id_ . 

The court’s ruling was three-fold. First, the court ruled that it had no discretion to disregard the present ban on the photographing and broadcasting of federal criminal proceedings. 

86 

_Id_ . Second, the court found that the ban did not violate the constitutional rights of either the public or the broadcast media. _Id._ Third, the court ruled that “even if the mandatory ban were declared unconstitutional, given the issues raised in the indictment, any societal benefits from photographing and broadcasting these proceedings [were] heavily outweighed by the significant dangers worldwide broadcasting of this trial would pose to the orderly and secure administration of justice.” _Id_ . at 184. The court’s decision on the first two issues was in line with all of the court of appeal rulings on those issues cited above in footnote 17. The court’s ruling on the third issue addressed many arguments that are likely to be raised by the media in this case. 

The court began its analysis as follows: 

Even if Rule 53 and Local Rule 83.3 were found unconstitutional, this Court would deny the intervenors’ motion. The right of access is not absolute. _See_ [ _Globe Newspaper_ , 457 U.S. at 606]; _Westmoreland_ , 752 F.2d at 19. It may be tempered by a defendant’s right to a fair trial, see [ _Press-Enterprise_ , 478 U.S. at 14-15], the need to preserve the secrecy of Grand Jury proceedings, see [ _Branzburg v. Hayes,_ 408 U.S. 665, 684 (1972)], or concerns about witness security, _see_ [ _Globe Newspaper,_ 457 U.S. at 608-09]. Limitations on access may be necessary to “preserve higher values.” [ _Press-Enterprise Co_ ., 478 U.S. at 9]. 

Significant concerns about the security of trial participants and the integrity of the fact- finding process justify a ban on photographing and broadcasting this trial. In particular, we find that audio or visual broadcasting of any portion of these proceedings is likely to intimidate witnesses and jurors, as well as threaten the security of the courtroom and all those involved in this trial. 

_Id_ . at 186. 

The court next addressed several arguments raided by Court TV. First, 

Intervenors contend that televising this trial will enhance its truth seeking aspects. They argue that allowing the public to participate through the lens of the television camera will serve as a check on the judicial process. However, “it is impossible to believe that the reliability of a trial as a method of finding facts and determining guilt or innocence increases in relation to the size of the crowd watching it.” [ _Estes_ , 381 U.S. at 595] (Harlan, J., concurring). The presence of interested spectators, attorneys, jurors and a judge will satisfy the 

87 

safeguards of a public trial and ensure the integrity of these proceedings. _See id_ .; _see also_ [ _Globe Newspaper_ , 457 U.S. at 606]. 

### _Id_ . 

Second, the court considered the following argument: 

Intervenors stress that the state of audio-visual technology has reached such a degree of miniaturization and efficiency as to avoid the tangle of wires and obtrusiveness of cameras, microphones and lighting which led the Supreme Court to hold that televising the trial so interfered with the orderly process of justice that it violated the defendant’s rights to due process and his Sixth Amendment right to a fair trial. _See_ [ _Estes_ , 381 U.S. at 550-51] We agree with the intervenors that broadcast technology has changed dramatically since 1965 and that many of the specific problems discussed in _Estes_ may no longer apply. 

Advances in broadcast technology, however, have also created new threats to the integrity of the fact- finding process. The traditional public spectator or media representative who attends a federal criminal trial leaves the courtroom with his or her memory of the proceedings and any notes he or she may have taken. These spectators do not leave with a permanent photograph. However, once a witness’ testimony has been televised, the witness’ face has not just been publicly observed, it has also become eligible for preservation by VCR or DVD recording, digitizing by the new generation of cameras or permanent placement on Internet web sites and chat rooms. Today, it is not so much the small, discrete cameras or microphones in the courtroom that are likely to intimidate witnesses, rather, it is the witness’ knowledge that his or her face or voice may be forever publicly known and available to anyone in the world. 

As the United States argues, this intimidation could lead foreign prosecution witnesses, outside the jurisdiction of the Court, to refuse to testify or withhold their full testimony out of reasonable fears for their personal safety. It could similarly lead witnesses favorable to the defense to refrain from coming forward for fear of being ostracized. The permanent preservation of images of law enforcement witnesses could also jeopardize their future careers or personal safety. How could an agent whose face was known throughout the world ever be able to work undercover or interview witnesses on the street effectively? 

Knowledge that the proceedings were being broadcast may also intimidate jurors. Excluding cameras and other recording devices from the courtroom will help preserve the anonymity of the jurors who are selected to serve and minimize the potential for a “popular verdict.” _See_ [ _Estes_ , 381 U.S. at 592] (Harlan, J., concurring). 

88 

The intervenors, obviously sensitive to these concerns, argue that they would agree to mask the faces of all jurors or witnesses who did not wish to be photographed. That response is insufficient for two reasons. First, it creates an additional management issue for the Court in what is an already complex case. As a practical matter, names of trial witnesses in criminal cases are not usually exchanged in advance. Therefore, witness requests not to be photographed would likely have to be addressed just as the witness is called to testify, and directions would have to be given to camera operators not to photograph those witnesses. How much time that colloquy might take and the impact on the witness is hard to determine, but it would certainly add to any nervousness that person might have about testifying and could delay the trial while the camera was being adjusted. Second, mistakes can be made. If a witness’ or juror’s face were inadvertently televised, the harm cannot be undone. Given that this case involves allegations that members of the al Qaeda conspiracy, a world wide terrorist organization with cells operating within this country, monitor trial proceedings involving its members, any mistake could expose the juror or witness to serious risks to their personal safety. 

Even if every witness’ face were obscured, the faces of the attorneys, court staff, and security officers would be exposed, subjecting them to the possibility of long-term security problems. In addition, publicly broadcast photographic images of the set- up of the courtroom, the location of the cell block, the number of deputy marshals, where the deputies stand in the courtroom, placement of doors and other courtroom details could jeopardize the security of these proceedings. Intervenors have offered no solutions to these problems. 

_Id_ . at 186-87. 

This aspect of the court’s ruling is especially compelling in this case where there have already been multiple violations of the court’s decorum order directing the media to use its “best efforts” to avoid visually recording court security staff, clerks, and judicial assistants, and counsel for the media has expressed the cavalier attitude with respect to even more serious violations that “[t]hey’re not perfect; mistakes happen.” Dkt. 256, at 9; _see also_ Dkt. 265. 

Next, the court adopted an argument urged by the United States: 

The United States also argues that any retrial of this already highly publicized case would be rendered extremely difficult if the trial proceedings have been broadcast. A potential juror who had watched portions of the first trial could be tainted. If witnesses experienced any recrimination from members of the public for testifying in the first trial, those witnesses may be unwilling or hesitant to testify at a retrial. 

89 

_Id._ at 187. 

The _Moussaoui_ Court also ruled that another factor justifying denial of the motion was that “world- wide broadcasting of these proceedings, either by television, radio or the Internet, would be an open invitation to any trial participant to engage in showmanship or make a public spectacle for the world to see or hear.” _Id_ . 

Lastly, the court addressed the intervenor’s argument that the intense national and 

international interest in the case, coupled with the needs of the victims, justified allowing Court TV to televise the proceedings: 

We are very aware of the extensive, legitimate public interest in this case, and understand that many thousands of people in this country and throughout the world, suffered devastating losses on September 11, 2001. It is understandable that they might want to watch this trial. However, contrary to what intervenors have argued, the purpose of this trial is not to provide catharsis to the victims or to educate the world about the American legal system. Instead, the purpose is to determine the innocence or guilt of this defendant for the specific crimes charged in the Indictment. It remains a bedrock principle of our legal system that the courts have the right “to conduct their business in an untrammeled way.” [ _Wood v. Georgia_ , 370 U.S. 375, 383 (1962)]. To ensure that both this defendant and the United States receive a fair and orderly trial, none of these proceedings should be photographed or broadcast in any form. 

We find that all of these concerns provide compelling reasons for prohibiting any photographing or broadcasting of either the pretrial or trial proceedings. Accordingly, the intervenors’ Motion to Record and Telecast will be denied by an Order issued with this Memorandum Opinion. 

_Id_ . at 188. 

To summarize, under _Sheppard_ and its progeny, once it is shown that there is “a 

reasonable likelihood that prejudicial electronic media coverage prior to trial will prevent a fair trial”, the Federal Constitution imposes a duty on a trial court to take “strong measures” to 

protect to the fullest extent possible a capital defendant’s Sixth, Eighth, and Fourteenth 

90 

Amendment and Utah Constitutional rights to a fair and reliable trial and sentencing hearing by an impartial jury. That standard has clearly been met in this case. Both the Supreme Court’s camera-in-the-courtroom cases and lower court decisions support granting the present motion on constitutional grounds as a necessary remedy to fully protect Mr. Robinson’s state and federal constitutional rights. 

- **C. The Fact That the State and Its Agents Released Extremely Prejudicial Information and Opinions to the Media in Violation of Applicable Law and Standards Weighs in Favor of Granting This Motion.** 

### **1. Introduction** 

“A very important factor in many of these cases is the fact that the publicity has been 

engendered by reports given by government agents. It has been suggested that where government agents have been responsible for the dispensing of information, thereby giving such information their imprimatur, a court is more likely to consider [remedial relief].” _United States v. Houlihan_ , 926 F. Supp. 14, 16, n. 3 (D. Mass. 1996) (collecting cases); _see also Sheppard_ , 384 U.S. at 363 (“The courts must take such steps by rule and regulation that will protect their processes from prejudicial outside interferences. Neither prosecutors, counsel for defense, the accused, 

witnesses, court staff nor enforcement officers coming under the jurisdiction of the court should be permitted to frustrate its function. Collaboration between counsel and the press as to 

information affecting the fairness of a criminal trial is not only subject to regulation, but is highly censurable and worthy of disciplinary measures.”); _Bauer_ , 522 F.2d at 253 (7th Cir. 1975) 

(special rules regulating release of information by prosecutors and law enforcement officials are justified because “[t]hey have the ability to influence and ensure proper governmental procedure without resort to public opinion” and “they know what charges may be brought and are a prime source of damaging statements.”); _U.S. ex rel. Bloeth v. Denno_ , 313 F.2d 364, 372 (2d Cir. 1963) (en banc) (reversing a conviction where the defendant was denied his right to an impartial jury 

91 

by prejudicial pretrial publicity, and emphasizing the fact that “much of the prejudicial matter, particularly that from the files of a state institution, and in part the attack on the insanity defense, came from the prosecution.”). 

As indicated above, law enforcement and prosecutorial agencies have made numerous disclosures to the media of highly prejudicial material, in direct violation of controlling statutes, rules, and ethical guidelines. On September 12, 2025, someone on the joint state and federal law enforcement team released to the media an Affidavit of Probable Cause that was posted unredacted on the internet by media outlets, and a substantial portion of the affidavit was then read to a national audience during Governor Cox’s “We GOT HIM” press conference, all in violation of Utah Code § 63G-2-201(3), Utah Code § 63G-2-302(2)(d), Utah Code § 63G-2305(10), (11), and (18), and Utah Rule of Professional Conduct 3.6 (“Prof’l Rule 3.6”). On the same date, someone in law enforcement released Mr. Robinson’s mugshot to the media in direct violation of Utah Code § 63G-2-201(3), Utah Code § 63G-2-302(2)(d), and Utah Code § 63G-2305(10), (11), (18) and (80).<sup>28</sup> On September 15, 2025, Attorney General Bondi, FBI Director Patel, and FBI Deputy Director Bongino made statements to the media in blatant violation of 28 C.F.R. § 50.2(b)(6) and Utah Code § 63G-2-201(3). On September 16, 2025, an even more 

detailed Statement of Probable Cause was read to and then released to the media in its entirety during the County Attorney’s September 16, 2025 press conference, and that reading was 

28 On October 24, 2025, the State informed the Court that “it was not the sheriff’s office that released that to the media. That was a different law enforcement agency that did that…In fact, when that was made public, the …sheriff’s office was very concerned that it had been made public.” Dkt. 268, at 51. Somebody in the Governor’s Office seems to have admitted that it was the Governor who released the mugshot under a shaky rationale. _See_ Daniella Rivera, _Why Gov. Cox’s release of Charlie Kirk murder suspect’s mugshot is unusual in Utah,_ KSLTV.COM, (Sep. 16, 2025, 1:37 PM MT), https://ksltv.com/ksl-investigates/why-gov-coxs-release-of-charlie-kirk- <u>murder-suspects-mugshot-is-unusual-in-utah/819901/.</u> 

92 

accompanied by what was essentially a penalty phase closing argument on victim impact issues, in violation of Utah Code § 63G-2-201(3), Utah Code § 63G-2-302(2)(d), Utah Code § 63G-2305(10), (11), and (18), and Prof’l Rule 3.6. As is further indicated, the sheriff held his own press conference at which he expressed his opinion about the strength of the evidence against Mr. Robinson, and commented on his demeanor, which also violated the same Utah Code and rule provisions. Finally, the Governor, in three nationally televised broadcasts, expressed his opinion about Mr. Robinson’s guilt and character and discussed the forensic evidence in the case, which also violated the same provisions. 

As argued below, the documents, photographs, information and opinions disclosed to the media on these occasions were not properly released, and these disclosures, which reached the public primarily through the electronic media, have severely impaired Mr. Robinson’s opportunity to be tried by a fair and impartial jury that can reach reliable conclusions based on evidence produced and tested in court. To prevent further eroding of that opportunity, the Court should prohibit any further electronic media live streaming or photography. 

The media will no doubt argue that the “bell cannot be unrung” and the fact that the prosecution and law enforcement have already polluted the jury pool argues in favor of allowing the electronic media to continue to do so. But there are at least four compelling answers to this argument. The first two reasons are explored in _U.S. ex rel. Bloeth v. Denno_ : 

It may be argued that since the trial strategy adopted by [the defense] called for emphasis on the number and brutality of Bloeth’s crimes as a basis for the defense of insanity, no harm was done in choosing a jury already familiar with his otherwise inadmissible history. There are at least two insurmountable difficulties with this argument. The publicity, partly sponsored by the prosecution, created opinions of guilt long before trial, far removed from any safeguards against inadmissible matter, and included inadmissible material emanating from the prosecution denigrating the insanity defense and so predisposing the prospective jurors to reject it. A defendant is entitled to be 

93 

tried on the evidence and arguments before the jury in open court, under the guidance of a judge. 

The opinions formed in this case, moreover, were based not solely on accounts of the crime at issue, but on … accounts containing expressions of the [opinion] of the District Attorney…, The interest of the public in the knowledge of the apprehension of the [suspect]… exists, but cannot justify the denial of an impartial jury to the one accused. 

### 313 F.2d at 373.<sup>29</sup> 

In addition, the “can’t unring the bell” argument fails to account for the cumulative 

impact of prejudicial electronic media coverage. As explained in the Declaration of Ruva, 

The volume of coverage in notorious cases can be overwhelming. And studies show that the quantity of pre-trial publicity to which jurors are exposed affects their level of bias. Across studies extending back several decades, researchers have demonstrated that individuals exhibit a greater propensity to pre-judge defendants when they are exposed to more publicity—both as a general conclusion (i.e., showing a general propensity to prejudgment based on an increase in the amount of exposure ), and in response to specific thresholds (i.e., showing that prospective jurors exposed to at least three newspaper articles about a crime were more likely to believe there was ample evidence against a defendant ). This effect has been demonstrated in both naturally occurring and controlled settings, confirming the validity of the proposition. 

Ex. 2, ¶ 9 (footnotes omitted). 

Lastly, the Supreme Court has expressly ruled in the context of protecting the privacy of 

criminal justice records that “the fact that an event is not wholly ‘private’ does not mean that an individual has no interest in limiting disclosure or dissemination of the information.” _U.S. Dep’t of Just. v. Reporters Committee for Freedom of Press_ , 489 U.S. 749, 770 (1989) (internal quotation omitted). _See also Prison Legal News v. Exec. Off. for U.S. Att’ys_ , 628 F.3d 1243, 1249 (10th Cir. 2011) (“That the video and photographs were, at the time of the trials, displayed publicly, may impact the family’s expectation of privacy in those materials but does not negate 

> 29 Unlike in _Denno_ , the defense in this case has in no way attempted to try this case in the press. 

94 

it…. _Reporters Committee_ thus requires an examination whether, as a practical matter, the extent 

of prior public disclosure has eliminated any expectation in privacy.” (citing _Reporters Committee_ , 489 U.S. at 762-63)). Here, although Mr. Robinson’s fair trial and privacy interests 

have been badly damaged, they have not yet been “eliminated”, an outcome that the present motion is seeking to avoid. 

**2. The Affidavit of Probable Cause, the Statement of Probable Cause, and the Mugshots Should Never Have Been Released to the Media, and the Governor’s, the District Attorney’s, the Sheriff’s, and the Department of Justice’s Press Conferences and Other Media Appearances All Violated Controlling Statutes, Rules and Regulations.** 

Numerous statutes, rules, and regulations should have governed the governmental release 

to the media of prejudicial documents, photographs, information and opinions. These are: 

### **a) Utah Code § 63G-2-302(2)(d) and Utah Code § 63G-2-305(80)** 

Utah Code § 63G-2-302(2)(d) provides that: “The following records are private if 

properly classified by a governmental entity:… (d) other records containing data on individuals the disclosure of which constitutes a clearly unwarranted invasion of personal privacy.” 

The United States Supreme Court has described an identically worded exemption under the federal Freedom of Information Act (Exemption 7(C)) as reflecting privacy interests in “avoiding disclosure of personal matters,” _Reporters Committee_ , 489 U.S. at 762; maintaining “the individual’s control of information concerning his or her person,” _id_ . at 763; avoiding “disclosure of records containing personal details about private citizens,” _id_ . at 766; and “keeping personal facts away from the public eye” _Id_ . at 769. 

Embarrassing and humiliating facts—particularly those connecting an individual to 

criminality—qualify for these descriptors. _See, e.g., id_ . at 771 (finding a privacy interest in criminal rap sheets); _Detroit Free Press Inc. v. United States Dep’t of Justice_ , 829 F.3d 478, 480 

95 

(6th Cir. 2016) (en banc) (holding that individuals enjoy a non-trivial privacy interest in their booking photos, for purposes of determining whether to apply the FOIA exemption for information compiled for law enforcement purposes where the disclosure could constitute an unwarranted invasion of personal privacy); _World Publishing Co. v. U.S. Dep’t of Justice_ , 672 F.3d 825, 830-32 (10th Cir. 2012) (same); _Karantsalis v. U.S. Dep’t of Justice_ , 635 F.3d 497, 499 (11th Cir. 2011) (per curiam) (same); _Rimmer v. Holder_ , 700 F.3d 246, 257 (6th Cir. 2012) (the names and identifying information of individuals associated with investigation of a murder); _ACLU v. U.S. Dep’t of Justice_ , 655 F.3d 1, 8 (D.C. Cir. 2011) ( “there is no real dispute that the scope of Exemption 7(C) can extend even to convictions and public pleas”); _Kiraly v. FBI_ , 728 F.2d 273, 277 (6th Cir. 1984) (FBI files identifying individuals suspected of criminal activity but not indicted or tried). _See also Deseret News Publishing Co. v. Salt Lake Cnty_ ., 2008 UT 26, ¶ 33 n. 6, 182 P.3d 372 (“Our interpretation of the phrase “clearly unwarranted invasion of privacy” finds support in the United States Supreme Court’s interpretation of identical language contained in the federal Freedom of Information Act.”). 

The Utah Supreme Court held in _Deseret News Pub. Co_ that under Utah Code § 63G-2302(2)(d), “most investigative reports …could qualify for nonpublic status” but not when (1) names have been redacted from the report and there is no evidence to show that if the report is released that anyone will be able to connect the dots and figure out who the people are or (2) the disclosure of names involves only the person who initiated the complaint or public officials. 2008 UT 26, ¶¶ 34-40. The Court also held that “section 63-2-302(2)(d) necessarily demands an expansive and searching evaluation of the interests that might make an invasion of personal privacy warranted.” _Id._ ¶ 33. 

96 

In this case, the Affidavit of Probable Cause contained Mr. Robinson’s name, date of birth, his parent’s home address, and the details about the ongoing investigation. Dkt. 2. As indicated in the statement of facts, the entire unredacted affidavit began appearing on the internet on the morning of September 12, 2025, shortly after the document was created, so it is highly unlikely that before release of the document anyone conducted “an expansive and searching evaluation of the interests that might make an invasion of personal privacy warranted.” _Deseret News Publishing Co._ , 2008 UT 26, ¶ 33. 

And although the Affidavit used the generic terms “family members” and “roommate”, by disclosing Mr. Robinson’s name, date of birth, and his parent’s home address the actual names of the parents and the roommate were easily discoverable. Indeed, by the early morning of September 12, 2025, the media were at the parent’s home and at Mr. Robinson’s apartment where they interviewed neighbors, published details about Mr. Robinson’s background, and broadcast his mug shot. _See_ CBS NEWS, _FBI searches childhood home of Charlie Kirk killing suspect_ (YouTube, Sep. 12, 2025), https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2QwkFKBBM6M; WSJ News, _What We Know About the Charlie Kirk Shooting Suspect Tyler Robinson_ (YouTube, Sep. 12, 2025), https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=W5aIkefBZWc. On the same date, electronic media outlets were posting Matt and Amber’s Robinson’s name and their pictures on the internet. _See, e.g._ , ONEINDIA NEWS, _Charlie Kirk LATEST: Tyler Robinson’s Father Becomes A National Hero | Shocking Story Behind ARREST_ 

<u>https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4usjW8UgYWw; Jenna Sundel & Joshua Rhett Miller,</u> _Who Is Tyler Robinson’s Father? Matt Robinson Helped Turn in Kirk Suspect_ , NEWSWEEK (Sep. 12, 2025, at 9:57 AM ET), https://www.newsweek.com/matt-robinson-father-tyler-charlie-kirk- <u>suspect-2128904.</u> 

97 

By September 13, 2025, the New York Post had identified “Lance Twiggs” as Mr. 

Robinson’s roommate. _See_ Josh Christenson & Georgia Worrell, _Charlie Kirk’s alleged assassin, Tyler Robinson, lived with transgender partner: source_ , NEW YORK POST (Sep. 13, 2025, at 4:23 PM ET), https://nypost.com/2025/09/13/us-news/charlie-kirk-shooter-tyler-robinson-lived-with- <u>transgender-partner/. And on the same date, the FBI confirmed to the media that this information</u> was correct. _See_ DALLAS EXPRESS, _“Trans” Roommate of Tyler James Robinson Identified As Lance Twiggs, Cooperating With FBI_ (Sep. 12, 2025), https://dallasexpress.com/national/trans- <u>roommate-of-tyler-james-robinson-identified-as-lance-twiggs-cooperating-with-fbi/. (“Senior</u> 

FBI officials told Fox News Digital that Robinson and Twiggs were in a ‘romantic relationship’ and shared an apartment in St. George.”) Shortly thereafter, the media began delving into Luna’s background. _See_ Marni Rose McFall, , _What Lance Twiggs’ Social Media Reveals About Tyler Robinson’s Trans Partner_ , Newsweek (Sep. 16, 2025, at 3:11 AM ET), 

<u>https://www.newsweek.com/lance-twiggs-tyler-robinson-partner-social-media-2129970. The</u> present case, therefore, does not fall within either of the two exceptions to application of Utah Code § 63G-2-302(2)(d) that were recognized in _Deseret News Publishing Co._ , 2008 UT 26, �� 34-40. 

The United States Supreme Court has noted that the privacy interest recognized in the federal counterpart to Utah Code § 63G-2-302(2)(d) “must be understood ... in light of the consequences that would follow” from unlimited disclosure. _Nat’l Archives & Records Admin. v. Favish_ , 541 U.S. 157, 170 (2004). Here, the predictable harm to 

privacy rights caused by the authorities’ release to the media of the Affidavit of Probable Cause (Dkt.2), and later, the more expansive Statement of Probable Cause (Dkt. 1) are briefly 

98 

and Exhibit 4 

summarized in the attached Exhibit 3 

Ex. 3. 



The predictable harm to Tyler Robinson’s privacy and fair trial rights caused by the authorities’ release to the media of the Affidavit of Probable Cause and the more expansive Statement of Probable Cause is more extensive, as fully documented in the statement of facts. As there indicated, release of these documents, which are based on several layers of hearsay that have never been subject to any adversary testing, led directly to the media repeatedly publishing, dramatizing, and having their “experts” make derogatory and speculative comment s about such material, creating in the minds of the jury pool in this case the lasting impression that Mr. Robinson is both guilty and dangerous enough to require execution. 

99 

Particularly damaging has been the display of Mr. Robinson’s mug shot in virtually every one of the electronic media stories about this case, which are often juxtaposed against loving images of Mr. Kirk and his family. As was concluded in _Detroit Free Press_ , in the course of holding that the disclosure of a mug shot violated to federal counterpart to Utah Code § 63G-2302(2)(d), “ a disclosed booking photo casts a long, damaging shadow over the depicted individual”, and “modern technology only heightens the consequences of disclosure—’in today’s society the computer can accumulate and store information that would otherwise have surely been forgotten.’” 829 F.3d at 482 (quoting _Reporters Committee_ , 489 U.S. at 771). The court elaborated: 

Booking photos—snapped “in the vulnerable and embarrassing moments immediately after [an individual is] accused, taken into custody, and deprived of most liberties”—fit squarely within this realm of embarrassing and humiliating information. _Karantsalis_ , 635 F.3d at 503. More than just “vivid symbol[s] of criminal _accusation_ ,” booking photos convey _guilt_ to the viewer. _Id_ . (emphasis in original). Indeed, viewers so uniformly associate booking photos with guilt and criminality that we strongly disfavor showing such photos to criminal juries. _See United States v. Irorere_ , 69 Fed.Appx. 231, 235 (6th Cir. 2003) (“[T]he Sixth Circuit has condemned the practice of showing ‘mug shot’ evidence to a jury ‘as effectively eliminating the presumption of innocence and replacing it with an unmistakable badge of criminality.’ “ [further citation omitted]); _see also United States v. McCoy_ , 848 F.2d 743, 745-46 (6th Cir. 1988) (finding the district court erred in overruling an objection to lineup photos, which “suggest that [the defendant] is a ‘bad guy’ who belongs in jail”). This alone establishes a non-trivial privacy interest in booking photos. 

_Id_ . at 482. 

In light of the predictable damaging consequences that flowed from the release of the Affidavit of Probable Cause, the Statement of Probable Cause, and the mug shots, it should be concluded that a violation of Utah Code § 63G-2-302(2)(d) took place when these materials were released because whatever interest the press or public had in the release of such material, that 

100 

interest was far outweighed by the privacy and fair trial interests put at serious risk by the disclosure. 

Moreover, with regard to the mugshot images, Utah Code § 63G-2-305(80) specifically 

provides: 

The following records are protected if properly classified by a governmental entity:… 

(80) an image taken of an individual during the process of booking the individual into jail, unless: 

(a) the individual is convicted of a criminal offense based upon the conduct for which the individual was incarcerated at the time the image was taken; 

(b) a law enforcement agency releases or disseminates the image: 

- (i) after determining that the individual is a fugitive or an imminent threat to an individual or to public safety and releasing or disseminating the image will assist in apprehending the individual or reducing or eliminating the threat; or 

(ii) to a potential witness or other individual with direct knowledge of events relevant to a criminal investigation or criminal proceeding for the purpose of identifying or locating an individual in connection with the criminal investigation or criminal proceeding; 

(c) a judge orders the release or dissemination of the image based on a finding that the release or dissemination is in furtherance of a legitimate law enforcement interest; or 

(d) the image is displayed to a person who is permitted to view the image under Section 17-22-30 

Here, Mr. Robinson was already in custody when his mug shots were released and none 

of the other exceptions apply. Release of the mug shots thus violated both Utah Code § 63G-2- 

302(2)(d) and Utah Code § 63G-2-305(80). 

### **b) Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10) and Utah Code § 63G-2-201(3)** 

Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10) provides: 

101 

The following records are protected if properly classified by a governmental entity: 

(10) records created or maintained for civil, criminal, or administrative enforcement purposes or audit purposes, or for discipline, licensing, certification, or registration purposes, if release of the records: 

(a) reasonably could be expected to interfere with investigations undertaken for enforcement, discipline, licensing, certification, or registration purposes; 

(b) reasonably could be expected to interfere with audits, disciplinary, or enforcement proceedings; 

(c) would create a danger of depriving a person of a right to a fair trial or impartial hearing; 

(d) reasonably could be expected to disclose the identity of a source who is not generally known outside of government and, in the case of a record compiled in the course of an investigation, disclose information furnished by a source not generally known outside of government if disclosure would compromise the source; or 

(e) reasonably could be expected to disclose investigative or audit techniques, procedures, policies, or orders not generally known outside of government if disclosure would interfere with enforcement or audit efforts. 

With regard to Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10)(a), the Utah Supreme Court held in _Deseret_ 

_News Publishing_ , that disclosure of a “report of an ongoing investigation ‘reasonably could be expected to interfere with investigations undertaken for enforcement, discipline, licensing, certification, or registration purposes’”. 2008 UT 26, ¶ 18. The Court further held that “the County was required to conduct a conscientious and neutral evaluation of the report’s GRAMA status without regard to existing designations or classifications.” _Id_ . ¶ 24. 

In this case, as indicated above, the speed with which the disclosures were made after 

creation of the documents and images suggests strongly that prior to the disclosure, no government official conducted a “a conscientious and neutral evaluation of the report’s GRAMA status without regard to existing designations or classifications.” _Id_ . Instead, the September 15, 

102 

2025, media appearances by FBI Director Patel and Deputy Director Bongino cited above indicate that material was being released pursuant to their blanket policy that it was in the “public interest” to do so, without any regard for weighing the constitutional and statutory interests of Mr. Robinson, his family, or Luna. That is exactly what _Deseret News Publishing_ prohibits. And unlike in _Deseret News Publishing,_ where the investigation was not ongoing when the disclosures were made, here it clearly was. _See, e.g._ , Kash Patel, _Director Patel’s Remarks at the September 12, 2025, Press Conference on the Utah Valley University Shooting_ , FBI, https://www.fbi.gov/news/speeches-and-testimony/director-patel-remarks-september-12- <u>press-conference-utah-valley-university-shooting (“This is very much an ongoing investigation,</u> as the governor said, and we will continue to work with state and local authorities to develop the investigation, to provide them the evidence they need for their ongoing prosecutions. And we will be here to answer every call they absolutely have as long as it takes, for as long as we need to find and apprehend whatever suspects were involved in this crime.”). 

Finally, this is not a case like _Deseret News Publishing_ where competing interests fought to a draw, requiring that the documents be disclosed. _See Deseret News Publishing_ , 2008 UT 26, ¶ 24 (GRAMA “mandate that when competing interests fight to a draw, disclosure wins.”). Instead, it is one involving the constitutional and statutory right to privacy of Mr. Robinson, his parents, and Luna, and Mr. Robinson’s fundamental constitutional right to a fair, impartial, and reliable guilt and penalty trial. For reasons discussed in the Introduction, Mr. Robinson’s fundamental constitutional right to a fair and impartial trial, in combination with the privacy rights and interests at stake, must prevail over any competing interest. The disclosures in this case therefore violated Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10)(a). 

103 

It must also be stressed that the disclosures also violated Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10)(c) and (10)(d). Although no Utah appellate court has construed these provisions, it is clear that the disclosure of Mr. Robinson’s name and birthdate, of his parent’s address, and of detailed 

accounts of statements made to law enforcement by the parents and by Luna, “reasonably could be expected to disclose the identity of a source who is not generally known outside of government and, in the case of a record compiled in the course of an investigation, disclose information furnished by a source not generally known outside of government if disclosure would compromise the source.” _Id_ . Indeed, the media had discovered the identities of these sources shortly after the disclosures of the Affidavit of Probable Cause on September 12, 2025. 

Lastly, and most importantly, Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10)(c), which creates a broad 

category requiring non-disclosure of any record if its release “would create a danger of depriving a person of a right to a fair trial or impartial hearing” seems to contemplate that the Court will utilize the categories of information set forth in Comment 5 to Prof’l Rule 3.6 to determine what information in a record would create such a danger. _See supra_ , n. 4. 

This interpretation is bolstered by Utah Code § 63G-2-201(3), which specifically references “court rule(s)” and “federal regulation[s]”, stating: 

The following records are not public: 

(a) a record that is private, controlled, or protected under Sections 63G-2-302, 63G-2-303, 63G-2-304, and 63G-2-305; and 

(b) a record to which access is restricted pursuant to court rule, another state statute, federal statute, or federal regulation, including records for which access is governed or restricted as a condition of participation in a state or federal program or for receiving state or federal funds. 

This provision incorporates into the structure of Utah Code § 63G-2-201(3) rules like 

Prof’l Rule 3.6 and federal regulations like 28 C.F.R. § 50.2 that if a record is protected under 

104 

either Prof’l Rule 3.6 or 28 C.F.R. § 50.2, it becomes a non-public record by virtue of Utah Code § 63G-2-201(3). Thus, both Prof’l Rule 3.6 and 28 C.F.R. § 50.2 must be taken into account in conducting a comprehensive GRAMA analysis. 

As just one example, Prof’l Rule 3.6 (b)(2) permits a lawyer to state “information contained in a public record”, however, 28 C.F.R. § 50.2(b)(3) contains no such exception. This means that a federal official who attends a press conference in Utah and reads the contents of an affidavit of probable cause to the media which covers subjects covered in Comment 5 of Prof’l Rule 3.6 may, for the reasons stated below, be violating Prof’l Rule 3.6, but he will most certainly be violating 28 C.F.R. § 50.2(b)(6) and therefore will also be violating Utah Code § 63G-2-201(3). 

As discussed above in footnote four and further below, Comment 5 to Prof’l Rule 3.6 specifies certain categories of information that if released are deemed to be “more likely than not to have a material prejudicial effect on a proceeding.” Comment 5 has been interpreted as an essential part of Prof’l Rule 3.6. _See Leavitt_ , 2025 UT 46, ¶ 31 (concluding under Prof’l Rule 3.6 that there was substantial evidence in the record that the former Utah District Attorney knew or reasonably should have known that his statements had a substantial likelihood of materially prejudicing the proceedings because “[t]hese …statements are of the type specifically identified in comment 5 to Prof’l Rule 3.6 as ‘more likely than not to have a material prejudicial effect on a proceeding.’”). In light of _Leavitt_ , it makes sense for the Court to take into account the categories of information set forth in Comment 5 in determining whether release of the Affidavit of Probable Cause, the Statement of Probable Cause, and the mugshots created “a danger of depriving a person of a right to a fair trial or impartial hearing.” _See_ Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10). 

105 

At the same time, the Court should recognize that Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10)(c) is more protective of a criminal defendant’s right to a fair trial than is Prof’l Rule 3.6. For instance, Prof’l Rule 3.6(a) states: 

A lawyer who is participating or has participated in the investigation or litigation of a matter shall not make an extrajudicial statement that the lawyer knows or reasonably should know will be disseminated by means of public communication and will have a substantial likelihood of materially prejudicing an adjudicative proceeding in the matter. 

Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10)(c) creates a more protective standard by providing that release of a record is prohibited even if the release would only “create a danger of depriving a person of a right to a fair trial or impartial hearing.” 

Similarly, Prof’l Rule 3.6 (b) provides in part that “[n]otwithstanding paragraph (a), a lawyer may state: (1) the claim, offense or defense involved and, except when prohibited by law, the identity of the persons involved; (2) information contained in a public record[.]” However, Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10)(c) contains no such exceptions. 

In this case, the release of the Affidavit of Probable Cause, the Statement of Probable Cause, and the mugshots clearly created “a danger of depriving a person of a right to a fair trial or impartial hearing.” Both the Affidavit and the Statement contained information that allowed the media to readily determine “the identity of a witness” (Prof’l Rule 3.6, Comment 5(i)), and both contained commentary on: “the character…of a…suspect” ( _id_ . 5(i)); “the expected testimony of a witness” ( _id_ .); “the existence [and] contents of any confession, admission or statement given by a…suspect” ( _id_ . 5(ii)); the “performance (and) results of any examination or test” and “the identity [and] nature of physical evidence expected to be presented” ( _id_ . 5(iii));”opinion[s] as to the guilt…of a …suspect” ( _id_ . 5(iv)); and “information that the lawyer 

106 

knows or reasonably should know is likely to be inadmissible as evidence in a trial, and that would, if disclosed, create a substantial risk of prejudicing an impartial trial.” ( _id_ . 5(v)). 

In regard to the release of the mugshots, there is no doubt that the release of these images “create[d] a danger of depriving a person of a right to a fair trial or impartial hearing” (Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10)(c)), because these images have long been recognized by courts as inherently prejudicial and thus any prosecutor knew or should have known that their release to and widespread dissemination by the media would have “a substantial likelihood of materially prejudicing an adjudicative proceeding in the matter.” Prof’l Rule 3.6 (a). and would be “information that the lawyer knows or reasonably should know is likely to be inadmissible as evidence in a trial, and that would, if disclosed, create a substantial risk of prejudicing an impartial trial.” Comment 5(v). 

The cases on this point are numerous and in agreement. _See, e.g., Eberhardt v. Bordenkircher_ , 605 F.2d 275, 280 (6th Cir. 1979) (reversing a state court conviction on Sixth and Fourteenth Amendment grounds, in part because the admission into evidence of mugshots was a “clea[r] erro[r] which touch[es] on fundamental rights and affect the overall fairness of the trial Eberhardt received…. The use of mug shots has been strongly condemned in federal trials, as effectively eliminating the presumption of innocence and replacing it with an unmistakable badge of criminality.”(collecting cases)). _See also World Publishing_ , 672 F.3d at 828-29 (“A booking photograph is a vivid symbol of criminal accusation, which, when released to the public, intimates, and is often equated with, guilt. Further, a booking photograph captures the subject in the vulnerable and embarrassing moments immediately after being accused, taken into custody, and deprived of most liberties.” (quotation omitted)); _United States v. Romero-Rojo_ , 67 F. App’x 570, 572 (10th Cir. 2003) (unpublished) (holding that the admission of a booking photo at trial 

107 

can be unduly prejudicial under Fed. R. Evid. 404(b) because “mugshots carry a clear implication of criminal activity that breaches the rule against admitting evidence of the defendant’s bad character or previous brushes with the law.”). 

In sum, release of the Affidavit of Probable Cause, the Statement of Probable Cause, and 

the mugshots was prohibited by Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10) and § 63G-2-201(3). 

### **c) Utah Code § 63G-2-305(11)** 

Utah Code § 63G-2-305(11) provides, “The following records are protected if properly classified by a governmental entity … (11) records the disclosure of which would jeopardize the life or safety of an individual.” 

There is no caselaw on this provision. However, in the unique context of this case, the release of the Affidavit of Probable Cause, the Statement of Probable Cause, and the mugshots jeopardized the life or safety of Mr. Robinson, his parents, and Luna. 

To reiterate the clear and present danger of violence which has existed since the 

inception of this case, on September 10, 2025, President Trump appeared on national television and announced that “[R]adical left political violence has hurt too many innocent people and taken too many lives” and that “the monster who attacked [Kirk] was attacking our whole country.” _See supra,_ FOX NEWS, _BREAKING: Trump releases Oval Office statement._ 

The next day, in a statement to reporters at the White House, Mr. Trump was asked what his message was to “conservative influencers, podcasters, TV hosts that … might be targeted by some of these radical groups” and he answered, “[w]e have radical left lunatics out there, and we just have to beat the hell out of them.” _See supra_ , Donald J. Trump (2nd Term), _Remarks_ . This call-to-arms was widely reported in the media. _See supra_ , D. Sanger. _._ As indicated below, from that point forward, things went downhill even further and the political and violent rhetoric dramatically escalated. 

108 

It is demonstrated above that the release of the Affidavit of Probable Cause and the mug shot on September 12, 2025 allowed the electronic media to quickly identify Mr. Robinson, his parents, and Luna and to use the hearsay statements in the affidavit to create a narrative strongly implicating Mr. Robinson. The release of the Statement of Probable Cause on September 16, 2025, for no apparent reason other than advocacy since the magistrate had already found “substantial evidence” to support the charges (Ex. 1), added further fuel to the fire of incrimination that the electronic media had already set ablaze. The predictable result was even more violent rhetoric from the President and his supporters. And as we know from the extensive record built in _United States v. Trump_ , “[t]he [r]ecord …shows that former President Trump’s words have real-world consequences. Many of those on the receiving end of his attacks …have been subjected to a torrent of threats and intimidation from his supporters.” 88 F.4th at 1011; _see also supra_ at pp. 62-66 (full summary of the record in _Trump_ ). 

In the volatile circumstances of the present case, the Affidavit of Probable Cause, the Statement of Probable Cause, and the mugshots should have been withheld from the media under Utah Code § 63G-2-305(11). 

### **d) Utah Code § 63G-2-305(18)** 

Utah Code § 63G-2-305(18) provides, “The following records are protected if properly classified by a governmental entity…(18) records prepared for or by an attorney, consultant, surety, indemnitor, insurer, employee, or agent of a governmental entity for, or in anticipation of, litigation or a judicial, quasi-judicial, or administrative proceeding.” 

Only one Utah case has considered this provision, _Utah Legal Clinic v. Salt Lake City Corp._ , 2019 UT App 58, 440 P.3d 948. Challenging the denial of disclosure of records related to the criminal prosecution of one of ULC’s clients, the Utah Court of Appeals ultimately held that the public interest advanced by a legal clinic was weak, and that the city’s interest in 

109 

nondisclosure of city prosecutor records outweighed any public interest in access to the records.. _Id_ . ¶¶ 21-22. The court also clarified that “when government records are properly classified into a category of record that is designated by statute as “protected,” the records ‘may be ordered to be disclosed ... only if the person or party seeking disclosure of the record has established, by a preponderance of the evidence, that the public interest favoring access is equal to or greater than the interest favoring restriction of access[.]’” _Id_ . ¶ 15 (quoting § 63G-2-305(17)-(18) and Utah Code § 63G-2-406(1)) The court did not elaborate on the meaning of Utah Code § 63G-2305(18), but its citation to Utah Code § 63G-2-305(17), which protects from disclosure “records that are subject to the attorney client privilege” is helpful in clarifying that Utah Code § 63G-2305(18) is not aimed at attorney work-product. 

On its face, Utah Code § 63G-2-305(18) is broad enough to shield from disclosure the 

Affidavit of Probable Cause, the Statement of Probable Cause, and the mugshots. 

### **e) Utah Rule of Professional Conduct 3.6** 

Prof’l Rule 3.6(a) states the basic prohibition on the release of information by lawyers relating to criminal and civil proceedings. That provision provides: 

(a) A lawyer who is participating or has participated in the investigation or litigation of a matter shall not make an extrajudicial statement that the lawyer knows or reasonably should know will be disseminated by means of public communication and will have a substantial likelihood of materially prejudicing an adjudicative proceeding in the matter. 

As indicated above, Comment 5 to Prof’l Rule 3.6 lists certain subjects that are “more 

likely than not to have a material prejudicial effect on a proceeding, particularly when they refer to a …a criminal matter or any other proceeding that could result in incarceration.” The listed subjects relevant to an analysis of the Affidavit of Probable Cause and the Statement of Probable 

Cause are set forth above. Those are the same subjects that are relevant to the analysis of the statements made by the Governor and the County Attorney with one exception. During his three 

110 

nationally televised media appearances on September 14, 2025, the Governor asserted that Mr. Robinson was not cooperating with law enforcement. Comment 5(ii) prohibits comment on “the existence or contents of any confession, admission or statement given by a defendant or suspect 

_or that person’s refusal or failure to make a statement_ .” The Governor’s statements about Mr. Robinson’s alleged lack of cooperation with law enforcement (he did in fact turn himself in) clearly violated this prohibition because the Governor is a lawyer who in all of his media appearances has represented himself as a member of the investigative and prosecutorial team. 

Similarly, the Utah County Sheriff, acting as an agent of the prosecution, held his own press conference on September 16, 2025 at which he expressed his opinion about the strength of the evidence against Mr. Robinson, and commented on his demeanor, which also violated Prof’l Rule 3.6 (a) and Comment 5. 

One of the remaining issues to be addressed is whether the statements made by the County Attorney at his press conference on September 16, 2025, and the other statements made by the Governor in his “WE GOT HIM” press conference on September 12, 2025 and in his later media appearances also violated Prof’l Rule 3.6(a) and Comment 5. 

It will be recalled that during his “WE GOT HIM” press conference, the Governor read verbatim most of the content of the Affidavit of Probable Cause. Similarly, at his September 16, 2025 press conference the County Attorney read verbatim the entirety of the Statement of Probable Cause attached to the Information, although he also went off-script to deliver what was essentially a penalty phase closing argument on the subject of victim impact. Similarly, in his three November 14, 2025 national media appearances, the Governor went off-script in the sense that he did not appear to be reading from either the Affidavit of Probable cause or the Statement 

111 

of Probable Cause, and he discussed several topics that were nowhere mentioned in the Affidavit 

or the Statement, such as: 

   - “WE GOT HIM” 

   - “We have additional forensic evidence that has been processed. We’ll be sharing that when charges are filed on Tuesday. So everything we know confirms that this is the person.” 

   - “the suspect has not been cooperating so far.” 

   - “We’re getting all this information from family members, again people around the suspect, and then forensic evidence that we have which confirms everything and more than what we were able to share in that initial press conference.” 

   - That there had been a “radicalization that happened in a very short period of time” 

   - “Clearly there was a lot of gaming going on. Friends that have confirmed that there was kind of that deep dark internet, the Reddick culture and these other dark places of the internet where this person was going deep. You saw that on the casings 

   - “there clearly was a leftist ideology with this assassin” 

   - he was “deeply indoctrinated with leftist ideology” 

   - “all we can confirm is that those conversations definitely were happening, and they did not believe it was actually him. It was all joking until he admitted that it actually was him.” 

   - “we’re collecting much more, including forensic evidence.” 

- “This is a direct assault on America, a direct assault against every single one of us.” 

- _See supra_ , Stelloh, et al., _Suspect in Charlie Kirk’s killing._ 

Prof’l Rule 3.6 (b) provides in part: “[n]otwithstanding paragraph (a), a lawyer may state: … (2) information contained in a public record[.]” Unfortunately, there are no Utah cases 

interpreting this provision. The fundamental issue raised with respect to this provision is whether the state may draft an affidavit or statement containing all of the subjects that are prohibited by Comment 5, file it with the Court, and then read it verbatim to the media during a press conference. Mr. Robinson urges that such an interpretation of this exception would swallow the 

112 

rule.<sup>30</sup> Such an interpretation would also conflict with the Utah Supreme Court’s holding in _Deseret News Publishing_ , that the holder of a record is “ required to conduct a conscientious and neutral evaluation of the report’s GRAMA status without regard to existing designations or classifications.” 2008 UT 26, ¶ 24. 

As noted previously, Utah Code § 63G-2-305(10)(c), requires the record holder to assess whether release of a record “would create a danger of depriving a person of a right to a fair trial or impartial hearing.” In this case, as demonstrated above, when that standard is applied to the Affidavit of Probable Cause and the Statement of Probable Cause in light of Comment 5, it is clear that these documents should never have been released. A prosecutor in discharge of his duty under _Deseret News Publishing_ would have to come to the same conclusion with respect to these or similar documents containing Comment 5 violations, which means that a prosecutor would never be in a position to adopt a strategy of loading up a document that violated the principles of Comment 5 and thereafter use the filing of it to excuse reading it verbatim at a press conference. In other words, under _Deseret News Publishing_ , a prosecutor has a duty to properly classify a document and to screen out objectionable material under Utah Code § 63G-2305(10)(c) and other provisions of GRAMA such as Utah Code § 63G-2-302(2)(d) and Utah 

Code § 63G-2-305(10)(a) before a document is filed with a court as a public record. 

A similar duty is imposed by the American Bar Association’s _Fair Trial and Public_ 

_Discourse Standards_ (Conduct by Lawyers Participating in a Criminal Matter), _available at_ 

30 _See Att’y Grievance Comm’n of Maryland v. Gansler_ , 835 A.2d 548, 569 (Md.
