App. Ct. 2003) (“by strictly limiting what is considered a public record, this definition enables all of the components of MRPC 3.6 to filter objectionable publicity, preventing the ‘public record’ exception from swallowing the general rule of restricting prejudicial speech.”). _Gansler_ is cited several times with approval in _Leavitt_ , 2025 UT 46, ¶ 60, as is _In re Brizzi_ , 962 N.E.2d 1240, 1247 (Ind. 2012) (per curiam), discussed below. 

113 

https://www.americanbar.org/groups/criminal_justice/resources/standards/fair-trial-public- 

discourse/. Standard 8-2.1(c) provides: 

This Standard should not be construed as prohibiting a lawyer participating in a criminal matter from releasing or authorizing the release of a record or document that the lawyer is required to release under state open records laws or the federal Freedom of Information Act, upon receipt of a proper request. A lawyer participating in a criminal matter should not place statements or evidence into the court record or into a document that is deemed an open record under applicable law solely for the purpose of circumventing this Standard. If the lawyer has reason to believe that the public release of a record or document would create a substantial probability of harm to the fairness of a trial or other overriding interest, the lawyer should consider seeking a sealing order[.]. 

The Commentary to this Standard provides in part: 

[M]ost open records laws contain exemptions for material that would be prejudicial to an ongoing enforcement proceeding, to the fairness of a trial, or that would constitute an unwarranted intrusion on personal privacy. The purpose of this paragraph is to remind lawyers that they must be familiar with the open records laws in their jurisdiction, including the exceptions pertinent to these Standards. If the lawyer believes that all or part of a document falls within such an exception, the lawyer should withhold such material if its release would violate these standards. In such a situation, the lawyer should promptly provide notice to the party requesting the information so that the lawyer’s decision can be subject to judicial review. 

On the other side of the coin, lawyers should not insert information into a document that is deemed an open record solely to circumvent these Standards. Nor should lawyers make a statement during a judicial proceeding, or include information in a public court filing, solely to circumvent these Standards. If a lawyer is aware that certain information contained in a court filing, or that will be adduced at a hearing, poses a significant danger to the fairness of the trial or other overriding interest that would warrant sealing, the lawyer should consider applying for a sealing order for that filing or proceeding, in whole or in part. 

_Id_ .at 37.<sup>31</sup> 

31 The commentary is available at chrome- 

extension://efaidnbmnnnibpcajpcglclefindmkaj/https://www.americanbar.org/content/dam/aba/p ublications/criminal_justice_standards/fair_trial_commentaries.pdf/. 

114 

Public Discourse 8-2.1 Standard and the Commentary are consistent with the duty 

already imposed on Utah prosecutors by _Deseret News Publishing._ Where that duty was not discharged in Mr. Robinson’s case, neither the Governor nor the County Attorney may take refuge in Prof’l Rule 3.6(b)(2) as an excuse or justification for violating Prof’l Rule 3.6(a). 

Nor does Prof’l Rule 3.6 (b)(2) apply to any statements that are not contained in a filed record, such as the County Attorney’s penalty phase closing argument about victim impact, or the statements of the Governor quoted above. _See Gansler_ , 835 A.2d at 569 (“To receive the protection of the ‘public record’ safe harbor, the lawyer must not provide information beyond quotations from or references to public government records…. The “public record” safe harbor, whether construed narrowly or broadly, could not apply possibly to any statement that introduced information to the public for the first time.”); _In re Brizzi_ , 962 N.E.2d at 1247 (“ _Gansler_ held…and we concur, that to receive the protection of the public record safe harbor, a lawyer may not provide information beyond quotations from or references to the contents of the public record Moreover, we hold that a prosecutor must make clear that what is being disclosed is, in fact, the contents of the probable cause affidavit or other identified public document so the statements cannot be misunderstood to be the prosecutor’s own opinion about the evidence or the suspect’s guilt.”). 

The Governor’s and the County Attorney’s off-script statements, combined with the statement in the Affidavit of Probable Cause that was released to the media in which Attorney General investigator Brian Davis stated his personal opinion that “I believe there is probable cause that Tyler Robinson committed the crimes of aggravated murder by shooting Charlie Kirk in a circumstance that put many around him at grave risk of death, felony discharge of a firearm, and obstruction of justice for moving and hiding the rifle believed to be used in 

115 

the shooting” (Dkt. 2), and combined further with the Statement of Probable Cause (Dkt. 1) which was released to the media and is still on the County Attorney’s website in which Mr. Gray gives his person opinion that “[b]ased upon evidence received from Brian Davis of the Utah State Bureau of Investigations, I have reason to believe the defendant committed the offense(s) as charged herein”, have severely impaired Mr. Robinson’s constitutional right to be tried by a fair and impartial jury. _See Leavitt_ , 2025 UT 46, ¶ 39 (“Leavitt’s position as the County Attorney made the potential for prejudice even higher. His statements likely carried more weight in the eyes of the public.” (citing _Gansler_ , 835 A.2d at 559 (“Comments by prosecuting attorneys, in particular, have the inherent authority of the government and are more likely to influence the public.”); _In re Brizzi_ , 962 N.E.2d at 1246 (“A prosecutor’s opinion of guilt is particularly likely to create prejudice, given that his or her words carry the authority of the government and are especially persuasive in the public’s eye.”)). 

This reasoning applies with even greater force to statements by Presidents and Governors. And because of the prestige of all of these individuals, the prejudice is highly unlikely to diminish even after a lengthy period of time. So, we conclude that the time between these statements and the trial date did not neutralize the substantial likelihood that they would materially prejudice the trial. Moreover, these individuals are all highly invested in this case and will no doubt be offering more of the same right up to the trial. _See id_ . ¶ 40 (“Leavitt contends that the potential for prejudice was lessened because Baum’s trial did not take place until much later. We agree that the statements’ proximity to trial is relevant. However, it is not dispositive…. And we conclude that the time between these statements and the trial date did not neutralize the substantial likelihood that they would materially prejudice the trial.”). 

116 

Prof’l Rule 3.6(a) was repeatedly violated in this case and Prof’l Rule 3.6(b)(2) does not 

provide an escape hatch from these violations. 

**f) 28 C.F.R. § 50.2** 

28 C.F.R. § 50.2(b)(1) and (2) set forth the basic ground rules applicable to the release of 

information by personnel of the Department of Justice relating to criminal proceedings. Those sections provide: 

Guidelines to criminal actions. 

(1) These guidelines shall apply to the release of information to news media from the time a person is the subject of a criminal investigation until any proceeding resulting from such an investigation has been terminated by trial or otherwise. 

(2) At no time shall personnel of the Department of Justice furnish any statement or information for the purpose of influencing the outcome of a defendant’s trial, nor shall personnel of the Department furnish any statement or information, which could reasonably be expected to be disseminated by means of public communication, if such a statement or information may reasonably be expected to influence the outcome of a pending or future trial.<sup>32</sup> 

28 C.F.R. § 50.2(b)(3), like Prof’l Rule 3.6(b) contains exceptions to this general rule, but unlike 

Prof’l Rule 3.6 (b) it does not contain a public record exception. 

28 C.F.R. § 50.2(b)(6) is comparable to Comment 5 and states as follows: 

The release of certain types of information generally tends to create dangers of prejudice without serving a significant law enforcement function. Therefore, personnel of the Department should refrain from making available the following: 

- (i) Observations about a defendant’s character. 

(ii) Statements, admissions, confessions, or alibis attributable to a defendant, or the refusal or failure of the accused to make a statement. 

32 _Available at_ : https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-28/chapter-I/part-50/section-50.2. 

117 

(iii) Reference to investigative procedures such as fingerprints, polygraph examinations, ballistic tests, or laboratory tests, or to the refusal by the defendant to submit to such tests or examinations. 

(iv) Statements concerning the identity, testimony, or credibility of prospective witnesses. 

(v) Statements concerning evidence or argument in the case, whether or not it is anticipated that such evidence or argument will be used at trial. 

(vi) Any opinion as to the accused’s guilt, or the possibility of a plea of guilty to the offense charged, or the possibility of a plea to a lesser offense. 

The statements made to the electronic media by FBI Director Patel, Deputy Director Bongino, 

and Attorney General Bondi are discussed above at pp. 15-17. It is clear without further discussion that all three of these officials violated 28 C.F.R. § 50.2(b)(6). Moreover, by virtue of Utah Code § 63G-2-201(3), these individuals also violated state law because that provision provides that “[t]he following records are not public:…a record to which access is restricted pursuant to …federal regulation.” 

### **g) Conclusion** 

In sum, the multiple violations discussed above underscore the need to take strong 

remedial action to prevent the further erosion of Mr. Robinson’s right to trial by a fair and impartial jury. Mr. Robinson next turns to demonstrating that because the traditional means of controlling prejudicial pretrial publicity are not available or have not been or will not be effective in this particular case, prohibiting further electronic media live streaming and in-court photography is the only available remedy available to the Court. 

### **D. The Traditional Means of Controlling Prejudicial Pretrial Publicity Are Not Available or Have Not Been or Will Not Be Effective in This Case** 

A good and fairly recent summary of the remedies traditionally available to control 

prejudicial pretrial publicity is provided in the ABA Public Discourse Standard 8-5.3 entitled “Mitigating the Effects of Publicity on the Fairness of a Trial.” Standard 8-5.3 provides: 

118 

If a case has been the subject of significant publicity, the court should consider the following options, to the extent available under applicable law in the jurisdiction and subject to the standards elaborated below, as means of mitigating the prejudicial effects of such publicity. The court should select the most effective option or options in light of the circumstances presented that will be the least disruptive to the proceedings and to jurors. The options include: 

(a) ordering a continuance; 

(b) conducting voir dire as to pretrial publicity; 

(c) providing clear cautionary instructions to the jury from the outset of jury selection; 

(d) providing clear cautionary instructions to court personnel, parties, lawyers, and witnesses; 

(e) providing lawyers with additional peremptory challenges; 

(f) impaneling additional alternate jurors; 

(g) importing jurors from another district or locality; 

(h) ordering a severance; 

(i) impaneling an anonymous jury; 

(j) sequestering the jury; and 

(k) ordering a change of venue. 

One of these options (providing clear cautionary instructions to court personnel, parties, lawyers, and witnesses) has been tried in this case in the form of Dkt. 23, the Court’s _Protection Order re: Pre-Trial and Trial Publicity_ (“Pretrial Publicity Order”) issued September 16, 2025. Unfortunately, the order came too late to prevent the initial prejudicial pretrial publicity which stemmed directly from the decisions by prosecutors and state law enforcement to issue detailed statements to the media and to release the Affidavit of Probable Cause (Dkt. 2) and the Statement of Probable Cause (Dkt. 1). This remedy has also been ineffective in stemming the flow of 

119 

subsequent prejudicial pretrial publicity by officials and others seeing themselves as not bound by the Pretrial Publicity Order and by such publicity stemming from the reporting of court proceedings by the electronic and internet media. 

Two of these options (impaneling additional alternate jurors and sequestering the jury) are designed to protect against prejudicial publicity occurring during trial and as noted in the commentary to this Standard “sequestering the jury is considered very burdensome and today is rarely used except in the most highly publicized case because of the fiscal cost and the resentment it can engender among jurors.” _See supra_ , n. 32 (Public Discourse Standards Comments, at 77). 

One of these options (ordering a severance) is irrelevant to this case, one of them (importing jurors from another district or locality) is not permitted by state law per _State v_ . _Richins_ , 2025 UT 10, ¶ 72, 568 P.3d 1046 and one of them (impaneling an anonymous jury) has been overturned on appeal in _NBC v. Stewart_ , 360 F.3d 90 (2d Cir, 2004) and _United States v. Quattrone_ , 402 F.3d 304 (2005), as acknowledged by the Commentary to Standard 8-5.3. _See supra_ , n. 32 (Public Discourse Standards Comments, at 79, n.92). 

Four of these options (conducting voir dire as to pretrial publicity, providing clear cautionary instructions to the jury from the outset of jury selection, providing lawyers with additional peremptory challenges, and ordering a change of venue) are all designed to address prejudicial pretrial publicity after it has already taken place. These remedies do not address _Sheppard‘s_ central concern that “we must remember that reversals are but palliatives; the cure lies in those remedial measures that will prevent the prejudice _at its inception_ .” _Sheppard_ , 384 U.S at 363 (emphasis added). 

120 

Given the nature and extent of the pretrial publicity that has already occurred in this 

capital case, and that is likely to continue up to and including the yet unknown date of the trial, allowing the electronic media to continue to live stream all proceedings in this case until that date based on the unrealistic hope that the kind of indelible prejudicial pretrial publicity that the electronic media has produced and will continue to produce can be fully remedied through a combination of a change of venue, voir dire, and jury instructions is both unrealistic and untenable. 

As Chief Justice Rehnquist declared for the majority of the Court in _Gentile v. State Bar of Nevada_ : 

Few, if any, interests under the Constitution are more fundamental than the right to a fair trial by “impartial” jurors, and an outcome affected by extrajudicial statements would violate that fundamental right. See, e.g., _Sheppard_ , [384 U.S. at 350-351]; _Turner v. Louisiana_ , [379 U.S. 466, 473 (1965)] (evidence in criminal trial must come solely from witness stand in public courtroom with full evidentiary protections). Even if a fair trial can ultimately be ensured through voir dire, change of venue, or some other device, these measures entail serious costs to the system. Extensive voir dire may not be able to filter out all of the effects of pretrial publicity, and with increasingly widespread media coverage of criminal trials, a change of venue may not suffice to undo the effects of statements such as those made by petitioner. 

501 U.S. 1030, 1075 (1991). 

The Chief Justice’s observations ring especially true in this case where the prejudicial publicity has been worldwide, and has relentlessly focused on every recognized form of prejudicial publicity, including: character assassination; alleged confessions to Mr. Robinson’s family, partner, and even his own attorney; mug shots; shackles; jail clothes; incriminating demeanor; an alleged refusal to cooperate with law enforcement; Mr. Robinson’s perceived similarity to Luigi Mangione; Mr. Robinson’s alleged romantic relationship with a transgender person; the unqualified and repeated assertion by the Governor that he has been “deeply 

121 

indoctrinated with leftist ideology”; and, perhaps most pernicious of all, the theme repeatedly stressed by President Trump and those that support him that Mr. Robinson and the “radical left” have joined together to murder Charlie Kirk. 

In the indelible words of the Fifth Circuit, “if you throw a skunk into the jury box, you can’t instruct the jury not to smell it.” _Dunn v. United States_ , 307 F.2d 883, 886 (5th Cir. 1962). _See also Irvin,_ 366 U.S. at 727 (“With [a prejudicial pretrial] opinion permeating their minds, it would be difficult to say that each could exclude this preconception of guilt from his deliberations. The influence that lurks in an opinion once formed is so persistent that it unconsciously fights detachment from the mental processes of the average man.”); _Id_ . at 728 (“No doubt each juror was sincere when he said that he would be fair and impartial to petitioner, but the psychological impact requiring such a declaration before one’s fellows is often its father…. As one of the jurors put it, ‘You can’t forget what you hear and see.’”); _Levine v. U.S. Dist. Ct. for Cent. Dist. of California_ , 764 F.2d 590, 600 (9th Cir. 1985)(“We recognize…that jury instructions are often an ineffective remedy.”)); _United States v. Concepcion Cueto_ , 515 F.2d 160, 164 (1st Cir. 1975) (“But a juror’s impressions [of impartiality], however honest, cannot be dispositive where the improper information is as damning and material as this.”); _Bloeth_ , 313 F.2d at 372 (“[M]erely going through the form of obtaining jurors’ assurances of impartiality is insufficient” where “the extent and nature of the publicity has caused such a buildup of prejudice that excluding the preconception of guilt from the deliberations would be too difficult for the jury[.]”). 

Here, as in _Delaney v. United States_ , “[o]ne cannot assume that the average juror is so endowed with a sense of detachment, so clear in his introspective perception of his own mental processes, that he may confidently exclude even the unconscious influence of his preconceptions 

122 

as to probable guilt, engendered by a pervasive pre-trial publicity.” 199 F.2d 107, 112-13 (1st Cir. 1952); _accord_ , _Murphy v. Florida_ , 421 U.S. 794, 802 (1975) (“Even [a juror’s assurance] of impartiality might be disregarded in a case where the general atmosphere in the community or courtroom is sufficiently inflammatory.”); _State v. Lafferty_ , 749 P.2d 1239, 1251 (Utah 1988) (same). 

What these cases have found as a matter of common sense have been backed up by numerous social science studies. As explained by Judge Gertner in _United States v. Houlihan_ : 

Studies by social scientists suggest that pretrial publicity affects potential jurors in ways of which they may not be aware, which they may not be able to articulate. Publicity, even if it is not strictly speaking, inflammatory, which suggests culpability, or which links the defendant to the criminal activities of others, as here, directly affects the readers’ perception of guilt, how likely they are to give weight to negative facts about the defendant at trial, to resolve conflicts in credibility against the defendants. Publicity unrelated to the crime can even have an impact; to be sure, the more similar the publicized cases to the case on trial, as here, the greater the effect.” 

926 F. Supp. 14, 16-17 (D. Mass. 1996) (citing studies). _Accord_ , _United States v. Footman_ , 33 F. 

Supp. 2d 60, 62 (D. Mass. 1998) (Gertner, J.) (barring evidence of a prior conviction because “[a]necdotal evidence and precedent, not to mention social science studies, make it quite clear that the jury cannot perform the mental acrobatics the Government would require of them, and further, that a limiting instruction would be useless”). _See also_ Ex. 2, ¶¶ 33-36 (Dr. Ruva reviewing and updating social science research demonstrating that biased jurors may not 

recognize their biases—undermining voir dire and the effectiveness of limiting instructions). 

What was said about the ability of Oklahoma prospective jurors to set aside the 

overwhelming prejudicial pretrial publicity in the Oklahoma City bombing trial is equally true here: 

Properly motivated and carefully instructed jurors can and have exercised the discipline to disregard that kind of prior awareness. Trust in their ability to do 

123 

so diminishes when the prior exposure is such that it evokes strong emotional responses or such an identification with those directly affected by the conduct at issue that the jurors feel a personal stake in the outcome. That is also true when there is such identification with a community point of view that jurors feel a sense of obligation to reach a result which will find general acceptance in the relevant audience. 

_McVeigh_ , 918 F. Supp. at1473. 

In addition, it is not at all clear in light of _State v. Richins_ whether the defense will be afforded any sort of meaningful in-person individual voir dire, even though, like _Richins_ , this is a high-profile aggravated murder case involving a vast amount of prejudicial pretrial publicity. 2025 UT 10, ¶¶ 37-49. Tellingly, the reason given in _Richins_ for the denial of such questioning was that it would “‘compromis[e] ‘the] privacy of the prospective jurors’” and would not be conducive to creating “ ‘the safe environment needed to answer personal questions truthfully and free from any pressure to conform to perceived judicial or community standards.’” _Id._ ¶ 47 (quoting the Presiding Judge). Televising Mr. Robinson’s proceedings certainly risks compromising the same values. 

Like voir dire and jury instructions, contemplating an eventual moving of the trial to another location in Utah does nothing to stem the current flow of electronic prejudicial publicity, and in all likelihood will not be a feasible remedy in this particular high profile and emotionally charged case, especially if the Court allows that flow to continue unchecked. As Chief Justice Rehnquist stressed in _Gentile v. State Bar of Nevada_ , , “with increasingly widespread media coverage of criminal trials, a change of venue may not suffice to undo the effects of [prejudicial pretrial publicity].” 501 U.S. at 1075; _see also Trump_ , 88 F.4th at 1017 (“Moving the trial to a different location would also be ineffective. Mr. Trump’s rhetoric has national reach. See Special Counsel Mot. Reply 9 (noting that Mr. Trump has more than 6 million followers on the platform Truth Social.”); _Levine_ , 764 F.2d at 600 (“A change of venue would be appropriate if the 

124 

publicity surrounding a trial is centered on a specific geographical location. A postponement would be appropriate if the publicity is temporary. This case presents neither situation.”). 

Regarding the wishful thinking that a continuance will somehow diminish the electronic media’s thirst for the sensationalistic and biased coverage of this case, this Court has already found that “[a]s with many high-profile cases, public interest may both wax and wane, but it is likely to persist.” Dkt. 137, at 6. In any event, it cannot be denied that “[a]llowing prejudicial [electronic media coverage] to go unchecked for an even longer pre-trial period would simply compound the problem”, further, “[d]elays also ‘entail serious costs to the [judicial] system,” and “frustrate the public’s interest in the swift resolution of criminal charges.” _Trump_ , 88 F.4th at 1018 (quoting _Gentile_ , 501 U.S. 1075). _See also Bloeth_ , 313 F.2d at 373 (“While time elapsing between the original publicity and the trial might in many cases blunt the effect of publicity and obviate the need for change of venue, it did not in this case, in view of the saturation nature of the publicity in the non-metropolitan area covered.”). 

Finally, one option not listed in Public Discourse Standard 8-5.3 that has already been tried by the Court is to simply warn violators of the Court’s decorum order that violations will be taken seriously. A similar approach was tried, and failed, in _United States v. Trump_ , where the court noted: 

The Supreme Court has been clear that the First Amendment permits, and Article III and due process principles require, courts to do more to protect the integrity of the criminal justice process than to shake their finger at a [violator] and tell him not to do what the law already forbids. _See Sheppard_ , 384 U.S. at 362-363[.] 

_Trump_ , 88 F.4th at 1008-09. _See also_ Wright, 38 F.R.D. at 438-39 (“I realize, of course, that the conduct of the press in the past with reference to the protection of the rights of the accused has not been exemplary. Moreover, even after the press has erred on the side of prejudicial publicity 

125 

in landmark cases, …its acts of contrition have consisted of little more than the utterance of pious platitudes.”).<sup>33</sup> 

### **E. Conclusion** 

For all of the reasons stated above, the Court should conclude that it has carefully 

considered all available options to terminating electronic media coverage and that none of the available options would remedy the compelling fair trial, security, privacy, and other factors discussed herein that singly or in combination compel the conclusion that termination of 

electronic media coverage is constitutionally required in this case. 

### **III. THE TERMINATION OR RESTRICTION OF FURTHER ELECTRONIC MEDIA LIVE STREAMING AND PHOTOGRAPHIC COVERAGE OF ANY PROCEEDINGS IS ALSO WARRANTED UNDER RULE 4-401.01.** 

### **A. Introduction** 

Rule 4-401.01 was adopted by the Utah Supreme Court’s Rules Committee in 2013 based 

on the recommendation of the _Final Report of the Judicial Council Study Committee on_ 

_Technology Brought into The Courtroom_ (April 10, 2012) (“Final Report”).<sup>34</sup> The Final Report is a mere 18-pages in length, only 7-pages of which are devoted to the issue of cameras-in-the- 

courtroom. Attached to the Final Report are the following Tab numbered documents: (1) an 

33 That is certainly true in this case where media counsel’s response to the decorum order violation on December 11, 2025 was to claim, based on no presented evidence, that the violation was “inadvertent”, and that: 

They understand their obligations. They take it very seriously. They’re not perfect; mistakes happen. We’re trying to correct as much as we can. 

Dkt. 256, at 9. As described above, as counsel was making this argument his cameraman was violating the decorum order by filming court staff. _See supra_ , p 34. 

34 Available at: 

<u>https://legacy.utcourts.gov/committees/Tech_in_Courtrooms/2012.03.23%20Final%20Report.pd f.</u> 

126 

undated document entitled _Cameras In The Courtroom: A State-By State Guide_ , by the Radio Television Digital News Association (RTDNA), an advocacy organization for journalists and news organizations; (2) a 1996 law review article by California media lawyer Kelli Sanger; (3) a 1994 Federal Judicial Center report entitled _Electronic Media Coverage of Federal Civil Proceedings: An Evaluation of the Pilot Program In Six District Court and Two Courts of Appeals_ “(“1994 Federal Report”); (4) a 2010 law review article by then Ninth Circuit Judge Alex Kozinski, _Of Cameras and Courtrooms_ , 20 FORDHAM INTELL. PROP. MEDIA & ENT. L.J. 1107 (2010); (5) opinion in _Estes v. Texas_ ; (6) opinion in _Chandler v. Florida_ ; (7) proposed Rule 4-401, which is more pro-media than the version eventually adopted;<sup>35</sup> (8) existing Rule 4-401; (9) a seven page 2011 Utah State Courts Publication Entitled _Social Media Subcommitte of the Judicial Outreach Committee_ , which does not address cameras-in-the-courtroom; (10) a 10-page undated report by five judges entitled _Electronic Media Report_ ; and (11) a two page undated document with no author listed, that does not address cameras in the courtroom. 

35 Jeff Hunt, one of the counsel for the media in this case was one of the authors of the Final Report. _See Journalists Now Allowed to Tweet, Live Blog from Utah Courtroom,_ Rep. Committee For Freedom Press (Nov. 20, 2012) https://www.rcfp.org/journalists-now-allowedtweet-live-blog-utah-courtrooms/. (“The Utah Supreme Court in February 2011 established a committee to study the impact of expanding media coverage in courtrooms. The committee was made up of judges, courtroom administrators and media attorneys, including Hunt.”). Mr. Hunt’s stance is reflected in his quote that “[t]he language in the rule defining a news reporter is quite broad, and we drafted it that way intentionally, …It includes any person who is gathering information for dissemination to the public. You don’t have to be connected to or employed by a traditional media outlet.”) Although the Judicial Council initially accepted this broad definition, it narrowed the Rule in 2014 to its present form. _See_ Utah Court Rules – Published for Comment (Posted: Feb. 24, 2015) https://legacy.utcourts.gov/utc/rules-comment/category/cja04-0401-01/. The Rule was narrowed again in 2015 to provide that the presumption of openness applied only “where the predominant purpose of the coverage is journalism or dissemination of news to the public.” _Id_ . 

127 

Relying solely on the 1994 Federal Report, the 1996 law review article by the media 

lawyer, which surveyed some pre-1996 state survey data, and Judge Kozinski’s 2010 law review article (which focused exclusively on his own qualified pro-camera-in-the-courtroom views and a brief summary of some of the results of the 1994 Federal Report), the authors of the Final 

Report opted to swing for the fence with this sweeping generalization: 

The concern most often raised about electronic media coverage is that such coverage may harm the decorum of the proceedings and negatively impact trial participants. The extensive empirical research and broad-based experience of other states, however, suggest that these concerns are unfounded. For example, several states, including Arizona, California, Florida, Hawaii, Kansas, Louisiana, Maine, Massachusetts, Minnesota, New Jersey, New York, Ohio, Virginia, and Washington, have studied the impact of electronic media coverage on courtroom proceedings, focusing particularly on the effect that cameras have upon courtroom decorum and upon witnesses, jurors, attorneys, and judges. See _Kelli L. Sager & Karen N. Fredericksen, Televising the Judicial Branch: In Furtherance of the Public’s First Amendment Rights,_ 69 S. CAL. L. REV. 1519, 1543 (1996) (“Televising the Judicial Branch”). 

The results from the state studies were unanimous: electronic media coverage of courtroom proceedings—whether civil or criminal—has no detrimental impact on the parties, jurors, counsel, or courtroom decorum. _Televising the Judicial Branch_ , 69 S. CAL. L. REV. at 1544, 1547. For example, the state studies revealed that fears about witness distraction, nervousness, distortion, fear of harm, and reluctance to testify were unfounded. Id. at 154344. A three-year pilot program permitting electronic media coverage of civil proceedings in six federal district courts and two federal circuit courts reported similar results, as well a favorable view of such coverage by the judges who participated in the program. _See_ Federal Judicial Center, ELECTRONIC MEDIA COVERAGE OF FEDERAL CIVIL PROCEEDINGS: AN EVALUATION OF THE PILOT PROGRAM IN SIX DISTRICT COURTS AND TWO COURTS OF APPEALS (1994); see also Televising the Judicial Branch, 69 S. CAL. L. REV. at 1545; Alex Kozinski & Robert Johnson, _Of Cameras and Courtrooms_ , 20 FORDHAM INTELL. PROP. MEDIA & ENT. L.J. 1107, 1114 (2010) (summarizing state and federal studies on electronic media coverage of judicial proceedings and concluding that the empirical research demonstrates no detrimental impact on trial participants or on courtroom decorum). 

Final Report, at 8-9. 

128 

Because of the age of the Final Report and the fact that it was relying solely on pre-1996 state survey studies, Mr. Robinson retained Dr. Christine L. Ruva, PH.D., who has conducted many of the modern empirical studies on the effects of pretrial publicity, to “review, evaluate, and update the empirical research discussed in the Final Report by the Utah Judicial Council Study Committee on Technology (April 10, 2012)[.]” Ex. 2 ¶ 2. After an exhaustive review of the vast amount of relevant empirical studies that are now available, Dr. Ruva turned her attention to the 2012 Final Report and after careful review ultimately concluded that “the empirical research discussed in the 2012 Judicial Council Final Report is limited and dated and is based on unreliable survey research.” _Id_ . at Conclusion. In paragraphs 39-62, Dr. Ruva explains in detail the bases for her expert opinion, carefully walking through all the available literature that was not considered in the Final Report and examining the fundamental flaws in the pre-1996 state survey data relied upon in the Final Report. 

From a lawyer’s viewpoint, some of Dr. Ruva’ most striking findings relate to the Final Report’s failure to even mention _Hollingsworth v. Perr_ y, 558 U.S. 183 (2010) _,_ which, as explained _supra_ at p. 81, establishes that two years before the Final Report at least five members of the present Court believed that Justice Harlan’s concurring opinion in _Estes_ was speaking for “the Court”, and that these same five members share the view of the _Estes_ majority that televising of a notorious case is improper. 

Dr. Ruva’s declaration states: 

50) The 2012 Final Report’s description of the outcome of the 1994 federal civil trial pilot program is as follows: “The pilot program was in effect from 1991 through 1994. Notwithstanding the Federal Judicial Center’s ultimate recommendation that federal trial courts allow cameras in civil proceedings, the federal judiciary declined to continue the program when the study period expired.” Final Report at 10. However, that description does not tell the whole story. As explained by the United States Supreme Court in _Hollingsworth v. Perry_ , 558 U.S. 183, 193 (2010): 

129 

In 1996, the Judicial Conference of the United States adopted a policy opposing the public broadcast of court proceedings. This policy was adopted after a multiyear study of the issue by the Federal Judicial Center which drew on data from six district and two appellate courts, as well as state-court data. _In light of the study’s findings, the Judicial Conference concluded that “the intimidating effect of cameras on some witnesses and jurors [is] cause for concern.”_ Administrative Office of United States Courts, Report of Proceedings of Judicial Conference of the United States 47 (Sept. 20, 1994). 

In more than a decade since its adoption the Judicial Conference has continued to adhere to its position on the broadcast of court proceedings. 

(emphasis added). 

51) _Hollingsworth v. Perry_ itself recognizes that “[t]his Court has recognized that witness testimony may be chilled if broadcast.” Id. at 195, citing _Estes v. Texas_ , 381 U.S. 532, 547 (1965); id., at 591 (Harlan, J., concurring). The 2012 Final Report does not discuss _Hollingsworth._ 

Several other key points made by Dr. Ruva are: (1) “the Sager and Kozinski articles cited 

in the report are law review articles and not empirical research articles. They cite state surveys and one federal survey regarding civil court proceedings…. Survey data cannot be used to infer 

causation, and this is not its only limitation - e.g., response bias, recall failure or misinformation effects (may not accurately recall past event and information after the event affects recall.)” _id_ . ¶ 40; (2) “the three-year pilot federal study published in 1994 that found judges have a favorable opinion of electronic media coverage of court in civil cases tells us nothing about the possible prejudicial effect such coverage could have on courtroom proceedings, witness testimony and memory, and juror bias, behavior, and decision making.” _id_ . ¶ 41; (3) “survey research is widely used, but it comes with several limitations and pitfalls that researchers need to consider” _id_ . ¶ 42; (4) “[i]n fact, five peer-reviewed journal articles have critiqued the survey studies relied upon in the 2012 Final Report, and three of these studies were published before the 2012 Report and are nowhere discussed in it.” _id_ . ¶ 43; (5) “[i]n addition to not acknowledging these critiques, the 

130 

2012 Final Report’s description of the existing studies is flawed” _id._ ¶ 49; (6) “None of these surveys assessed, or could assess, whether live streaming of all aspects of a high profile criminal case in the age of social media could cause irreparable conscious and unconscious bias and 

trauma to prospective jurors or caused untoward effects in other trial participants[.]” _id_ . ¶ 52; (7) “[f]inally, the 2012 Final Report does not address the pre-2012 empirical research discussed 

above and could not have considered the substantial empirical research that has taken place since 2012.” _id_ . ¶ 53. 

The bottom line here is that in deciding the present motion, this Court should place little or no weight on the unsupported, dated, and slanted conclusions of the 2012 Final Report. 

Instead, the Court should carefully consider “the substantial empirical research that has taken place since 2012” as reviewed in Dr. Ruva’s declaration. Ex. 2, ¶ 53. 

### **B. The “Predominant Purpose” of the Electronic Media Coverage in This Case Is Not “Journalism or Dissemination of News to the Public.”** 

Judge Kozinski’s law review article, indicates that while generally an advocate for court- 

controlled filming and later release of some portions of a _trial_ (he said nothing about pretrial proceedings and also noted that “nobody seriously believes that cameras should be allowed for every moment of every trial”), he was of the view that: 

Trials would be more open and transparent if they were held in Madison Square Garden, and that would certainly be technologically feasible. Yet the Supreme Court has said that a defendant “is entitled to his day in court, not in a stadium.” [ _Estes_ , 381 U.S. at 549 (1965)] if cameras in the courtroom rob criminal defendants and civil litigants of their dignity, and promote a public perception of trials as more about sensational entertainment than a sober search for truth, courts may be justified in parting ways with other public institutions, and public expectations, to exclude cameras in favor of forms of reporting that better advance respect for the rule of law and the guarantee of a fair trial. 

Kozinski, at 1122. 

131 

Consistent with Judge Kozinski’s view, Rule 4-401.01(2)(A) since 2015 has provided that: “There is a presumption that electronic media coverage by a news reporter shall be permitted in public proceedings _where the predominant purpose of the electronic media coverage request is journalism or dissemination of news to the public.”_ (emphasis added). No Utah court has construed any aspect of Rule 4-401.01, and the emphasized language is not a feature of any other state or federal court rule or statute. The Court is therefore left with little guidance as to how to apply this aspect of the Rule.<sup>36</sup> 

In his article, Judge Kozinski provided this example of when courts should prohibit 

camera coverage on the ground that such coverage “rob[s] criminal defendants and civil litigants of their dignity, and promote[s] a public perception of trials as more about sensational entertainment than a sober search for truth”: 

Unlike concern with the effect of cameras inside the courtroom, this argument retains real bite after the O.J. experience. Consider footage of the verdict (available now on YouTube). It’s high drama: As the courtroom waited, the camera zoomed in for an intense close-up of O.J.’s face, and remained there for the agonizing moments before-and during-the verdict. Because the camera was positioned above the jury, O.J. appeared to gaze ominously into the camera’s eye. Ron Goldman’s sister began to cry, and the camera pivoted for a close-up of her face. From there, to the stunned faces of the prosecution. And back to Ron Goldman’s sister. As the Supreme Court has said, “[t]he television camera is a powerful weapon” and “inevitable close-ups of [the accused’s] gestures and expressions during the ordeal of his trial might well transgress his personal sensibilities [and] his dignity.” [ _Estes_ , 381 U.S. at 549]. That’s to say nothing of the impact on the victim’s family, or the public perception of a trial depicted in such a manner. 

36 _But see_ United States House of Representatives, _Rules of the House of Representative_ , Rule XI(4) (Jan. 10, 2023) (permitting committee hearings or committee meetings to be covered by audio and visual means “for the education, enlightenment, and information of the general public, on the basis of _accurate and impartial news coverage_ ”, but specifically providing that “it is the intent of this clause that audio and video recordings of any coverage…may not be used for any partisan political campaign purpose or be made available for such use” and that “[i]f audio or visual coverage of the hearing or meeting is to be presented to the public as live coverage, that coverage shall be conducted and presented without commercial sponsorship.”). 

132 

Kozinski _,_ at 1122. 

Judge Kozinski describes exactly the kind of sensationalism and dramatization that has so far characterized the prejudicial electronic pretrial publicity in this case with its almost exclusive close-up focus on Mr. Robinson’s and the Court’s demeanor, Mr. Robinson’s alleged confessions, his mugshot and shackles, and the uninformed, speculative, and sensationalistic musings of media talking heads. 

The extent to which advertising profit and sensationalism are at the root of most of the publicity in this case is underscored by the sensationalistic content of the numerous representative postings referenced above, by the fact that they all feature product advertisements, and by the further undeniable fact that “[j]ust hours after the tragic news of Charlie Kirk’s assassination was confirmed, a flurry of ads featuring memorial merchandise exploded across social media platforms like Facebook and Instagram.” Foreign Brands Profit From Memorial Merchandise After Tragedy, HERE SHREVEPORT (Sep. 26, 2025); _see also_ Keenan Chen, _Merch sellers cash in on Kirk’s killing with flood of social media ads_ , THE GUARDIAN, (Sep. 25, 2025, at 11:26 ET) 

<u>https://www.theguardian.com/technology/2025/sep/25/charlie-kirk-merch-social-media-ads</u> (itself featuring advertisements). “In a viral post with more than 8,000 shares and hundreds of thousands of likes, the brand See Jesus In You promoted an ‘Echo of Freedom’ shirt with an American flag and Kirk’s signature, calling him a symbol of ‘faith, liberty, and America’. The simple shirt, which Kirk was wearing when he was assassinated, has become a hot commodity among his conservative supporters. Another clothing brand, Liberty Faith Gear, struck a much more combative tone in marketing its version of the freedom tee, as it urged ‘patriots [to] rise when freedom is attacked. We do not hide. We do not bow’”. Chen, _Merch sellers cash in_ . 

133 

Some courts have attempted to prevent such dramatization and sensationalism by 

prohibiting the media from focusing on the defendant’s demeanor, or, as Judge Kozinski suggested, by taking control of the camera away from the media and releasing a court video only after a delay to allow time for the parties to lodge objections to release. _See_ Kozinski, at 1127-28 (noting that “it’s no longer the case that the courtroom camera must be operated by the media, as it was during the O.J. trial” and that “[c]ombined with a delay before posting”, a court-created video “can be presented in as boring and straightforward a fashion as you please: no close-ups, no moving cameras and no filming of the defense table or the gallery.”). 

In the highly publicized case of _State v. Bryan Kohberger_ , the trial court allowed the 

media to retain control of all cameras, but insisted that the media stop zooming in on 

Kohberger’s demeanor, a measure that this Court has so far not imposed. This solution, however, failed to work because the media could not control itself. The trial judge therefore ruled: 

As demonstrated by some of the examples presented in the defense’s Motion to Remove Cameras from Courtroom, media cameras, both still and video, have and continue to zoom in on Kohberger despite the Court’s order that images should capture the courtroom in its entirety. At least some of the images are of Kohberger entering and exiting the courtroom. This is in direct violation of the Court’s previous directives not to film or take photographs until the Court is on the record and to immediately stop recording and taking photographs when the hearing concludes. It is the intense focus on Kohberger and his every move, along with adverse headlines and news articles, that leads the Court to conclude that continued photograph and video coverage inside the courtroom by the media should no longer be permitted. This step should help ensure Kohberger’s right to a fair trial by an impartial jury and achieve a proper administration of justice. 

Additionally, defense counsel has raised continued concerns with video footage and photographs capturing private documents on counsel table. This is again in violation of the Court’s directives. The State has also raised issues of vulnerable victims and witnesses being filmed during testimony. At this juncture, the Court has no confidence that directives to stop photographing or videoing during such testimony would be adhered to. Further, the duty on the bailiffs within the courtroom to ensure a safe and secure environment is being 

134 

eclipsed by the burden to monitor media and the public who may be audio recording, videoing, or photographing the proceedings. 

_State v. Bryan Kohberger_ , Case No. CR29-22-2805 (D.C. Latah County Idaho, Nov. 17, 2023) 

( _Order Denying Second Motion to Intervene and Granting Defendant’s Motion to Remove Media Cameras From the Courtroom_ , attached as Exhibit 5). 

A majority of the justices in _Estes_ unequivocally condemned the kind of sensationalistic 

and profit-driven electronic media coverage that has occurred in this case, where sensationalism, advertising, and zooming in on Mr. Robinson for the purpose of commenting on his demeanor, shackles, and attire is clearly the predominant purpose of the electronic coverage. Justice Clark’s lead opinion in _Estes_ states: 

The television camera is a powerful weapon. Intentionally or inadvertently it can destroy an accused and his case in the eyes of the public. While our telecasters are honorable men, they too are human. The necessity for sponsorship weighs heavily in favor of the televising of only notorious cases, such as this one, and invariably focuses the lens upon the unpopular or infamous accused. Such a selection is necessary in order to obtain a sponsor willing to pay a sufficient fee to cover the costs and return a profit. We have already examined the ways in which public sentiment can affect the trial participants. To the extent that television shapes that sentiment, it can strip the accused of a fair trial. 

381 U.S. at 549-50. 

Chief Justice Warren, CJ, joined by Justices Douglas and Goldberg, agreed with and elaborated upon Justice Clark’s analysis, writing that: 

The entire hearing on September 24 was televised live by station KLTV of Tyler, Texas, and station WFAA—TV of Dallas, Texas. Commercials were inserted when there was a pause in the proceedings. On the evening of Monday, September 24, both stations ran an edited tape of the day’s proceedings and interrupted the tape to play the commercials ordinarily seen in the particular time slot. 

_Id_ . at 554; _see also id_ . at 571 (“The televising of trials would cause the public to equate the trial 

process with the forms of entertainment regularly seen on television and with the commercial 

135 

objectives of the television industry. In the present case, tapes of the September 24 hearing were run in place of the ‘Tonight Show’ by one station and in place of the late night movie by another. Commercials for soft drinks, soups, eyedrops and seatcovers were inserted when there was a pause in the proceedings. In addition, if trials were televised there would be a natural tendency on the part of broadcasters to develop the personalities of the trial participants, so as to give the proceedings more of an element of drama. This tendency was noticeable in the present case.”). 

Perhaps most significantly, Justice Harlan, who supplied the crucial fifth vote in the case, also agreed with Justice Clark’s analysis: 

It is said that the pictorial broadcasting of trials will serve to educate the public as to the nature of the judicial process. Whatever force such arguments might have in run-of-the-mill cases, they carry little weight in cases of the sort before us, where the public’s interest in viewing the trial is likely to be engendered more by curiosity about the personality of the well-known figure who is the defendant (as here), or about famous witnesses or lawyers who will appear on the television screen, or about the details of the particular crime involved, than by innate curiosity to learn about the workings of the judicial process itself. Indeed it would be naive not to suppose that it would be largely such factors that would qualify a trial for commercial television ‘billing,’ and it is precisely that kind of case where the risks of permitting television coverage of the proceedings are at their greatest. 

_Id_ . at 594-95 (Harlan, J., concurring). 

Consistent with the various opinions on _Estes_ , the Utah Supreme Court concluded in 

_Sinclair v. Turner_ , : 

We appreciate that the facts of life must be taken into account. _The public is intrigued by the sensational, and thus by crime and the facts surrounding it. Consequently, it is but natural and perhaps necessary that the information media cater to that appetite_ . Yet this should also be considered: the service they perform so vitally affects the public welfare that in consideration of the guarantees of freedom of expression the law assures to them, they should be regarded as having corresponding responsibility to perform their duties in an awareness that in our system, they and the public whom they serve, all have an essential interest in, and in a sense are all involved in law enforcement. There should be a minimizing of sensationalism by the exercise of reasonable restraint and good judgment in reporting the news in as fair and objective a 

136 

manner as possible, in an awareness of the desirability and indeed of the necessity of according those suspected or accused of crime fair treatment and a trial by an impartial court and jury. 

434 P.2d at 307 (emphasis added). 

In other words, according to _Estes_ and _Sinclair_ , the goal of televising court proceedings is to make money and to cater to the appetite of the public for the “sensational.” “Suggestions that televised trials educate the public are disingenuous. The goal is not education but sensationalism and profit. To be sure, what we must be most concerned about is the defendant’s right to a fair trial, not entertainment.” _Nevers v. Killinger_ , 990 F. Supp. 844, 849, n. 2 (E.D. Mich. 1997), _rev’d on other grounds_ , 169 F.3d 352 (6th Cir. 1999); _Moussaoui_ , 205 F.R.D. at 188 (“contrary to what intervenors have argued, the purpose of this trial is not to provide catharsis to the victims or to educate the world about the American legal system. Instead, the purpose is to determine the innocence or guilt of this defendant for the specific crimes charged in the Indictment.”). 

Given the sensationalistic and profit-driven nature and the extraordinary extent of the electronic media coverage of Mr. Robinson’s case as outlined above, and for the reasons stated by the Justices in _Estes_ and by the Utah Supreme Court in _Sinclair_ , it should be concluded that because the “predominant purpose” of the electronic media coverage in this case has not been and is unlikely ever to be “journalism or dissemination of news to the public[.]”, The presumption in favor of electronic media coverage does not apply to Mr. Robinson’s case under the express terms of Rule 4-401.01(2)(A). 

- **C. Any Presumption in Favor of Electronic Media Coverage Has Been Overcome.** 

The 2012 Final Report expressly acknowledges that 

[E]lectronic media coverage of trial court proceedings raises concerns about fair trial rights, personal privacy, safety, security interests, and other legitimate interests. Accordingly, the proposed revision of Rule 4-401 balances those interests by permitting electronic media coverage of open judicial proceedings 

137 

while allowing a judge to prohibit or restrict such coverage to protect fair trial rights, privacy, security, and other important interests. 

Final Report, at 11. 

Rule 4-401.01(2)(B) requires that when determining whether the presumption of 

electronic media coverage has been overcome and whether such coverage should be prohibited or restricted beyond the limitations in the Rule, a judge “shall” consider “some or all” of nine specific factors. In this capital case, all of these factors weigh heavily in favor of prohibiting electronic live streaming and photographic coverage, and their combined weight clearly outweighs any factor that favors a contrary result. The factors are: 

### **(i) whether there is a reasonable likelihood that electronic media coverage will prejudice the right of the parties to a fair proceeding;** 

This is the first and clearly the weightiest factor under the Rule because it is based on Mr. Robinson’s state and federal constitutional right to fair, impartial, and reliable proceedings, as well as on the state’s independent due process right to fair proceedings. Mr. Robinson cannot speak for the State, but he does note that in a previous filing the State and the Sheriff urged the Court to “limi[t] media coverage for all in-person and remote hearings.” Dkt. 109, at 18-19 (classified as private).<sup>37</sup> What Mr. Robinson can speak to is the fact that the Supreme Court has “always held that the atmosphere essential to the preservation of a fair trial—the most fundamental of all freedoms—must be maintained at all costs.” _Estes_ , 381 U.S. at 540. At the same time, the Court has consistently held that there is no First Amendment right to live stream or photograph court proceedings. 

37 Although in Dkt. 137, this Court granted defendant’s motion to strike this pleading, it did so with the understanding that “as agreed to by the Defendant, the court has considered the _substance_ of the State and Sheriff’s opposing memorandum.” _Id_ . at 3 (emphasis in original). 

138 

As also indicated above, in _State v. James_ , the Utah Supreme Court applied a reasonable likelihood standard in a change of venue context, which is the same standard used in Rule 4- 401.01(2)(B). 767 P.2d at 555 (Utah 1989). _James_ thus provides essential guidance in applying this particular factor. 

The _James_ Court cited and followed the California’s Supreme Court’s decision in _Maine v. Superior Court_ , 438 P.2d 372 (Cal. 1968), in holding that regardless of language used in a Utah statute, the constitutionally correct standard to apply when assessing the effects of pretrial publicity is the reasonable likelihood standard. _James_ , 767 P.2d at 552. It also cited and followed _Frazier v. Superior Court of Santa Cruz County_ , 486 P.2d 694 (Cal. 1971) in holding that “a reasonable likelihood of prejudice does not mean that the prejudice must be more probable than not.” _Id_ . Finally, it cited and followed _Martinez v. Superior Court of Placer County_ , 629 P.2d 502 (Cal. 1981) in holding that “the factors which have been considered criteria of the potential for prejudice from pretrial publicity…include (1) the standing of the victim and the accused in the community; (2) the size of the community; (3) the nature and gravity of the offense; and (4) the nature and extent of publicity.” _Id_ . Each of these factors support granting the present motion. 

### **(1) the standing of the victim and the accused in the community** 

Neither Mr. Kirk nor Mr. Robinson is a resident of Utah County. However, Mr. Kirk has consistently been portrayed in the electronic media as a Christian, a devoted father, a national hero, a martyr, and a potential Republican candidate for the Presidency, while Mr. Robinson has consistently been portrayed as a “monster” (President Trump), a “depraved assassin” (President Trump), a person who is “everything that the far left wants from our young men [who] rejected the conservatism and the spirituality, the values of a small-town family [and]…moved into a small apartment, he became addicted to porn, he became addicted to hate, and he ended up 

139 

sleeping with somebody who doesn’t know whether they’re a man or a woman” (Vice President J.D. Vance), a “horrible human being” (Attorney General Bondi), a “evil human being” (Governor Cox), a person “deeply indoctrinated by leftist ideology” (Governor Cox), ); a “true sociopath” (Retired FBI Agent Parker); and a “evil, evil human being” (Megyn Kelly). 

Given these starkly contrasting portrayals, which will no doubt continue in the future, this factor weighs strongly in favor of finding a reasonable likelihood that further electronic media live streaming and photographic coverage will prejudice the right of Mr. Robinson to fair proceedings. _See, e.g._ , _Martinez_ , 629 P.2d at 507-08 (this factor weighed in favor of a venue change where although “ the victim was ‘no public figure,’ his status nonetheless appears to be significant” and where “[o]n the other hand, the press described petitioner in terms unlikely to evoke the sympathy or concern of the community.”). 

### **(2) the size of the community** 

In the 2024 Presidential Election, Utah County listed 368,875 registered voters. _See supra_ , _Countywide Summary Results for Utah County_ , 2024 Gen. Election **.** This Court has already found: that “the media interest in Mr. Robinson’s trial is arguably unprecedented in the State of Utah”; that “[p]hotographs and visual recordings of Mr. Robinson in jail clothing will likely be shared and disseminated in various public platforms”; that “[t]here is a strong likelihood that a substantial number of potential jurors in Utah County will view these images and may associate Mr. Robinson’s jail clothing with indicia of guilt”; that “[t]he danger of prejudice is compounded by technological advancements that allow the images to be manipulated in ways that do not accurately reflect the truth of the court proceedings”; that “[t]his case has generated an immense amount of pre-trial publicity”; that “[a]s with many highprofile cases, public interest may both wax and wane, but it is likely to persist”; that “[v]isual 

140 

recordings and photographs of Mr. Robinson shackled may give the impression that he is dangerous and may undermine his presumption of innocence”; that “[t]he potential jury pool in Utah County, based on their proximity, is likely to be even more exposed to the images of these proceedings and the messages those images send”; and that “[t]his case generates extraordinary ongoing national and international attention, some of which is threatening in nature to the parties, their representatives, and non-party participants (e.g. witnesses and court personnel).” Dkt. 137, at 5-6; Dkt. 266, at 2. 

As stated in _Maine v. Superior Ct. of Mendocino Cnty._ , even “a large city may …become so hostile to a defendant as to make a fair trial unlikely.” 438 P.2d at 380, n. 13. And relatively speaking, Utah County cannot be considered a “large” venue, especially when one contemplates how many prospective jurors would need to be removed from service because of the death penalty qualification process, and how many of the prospective pool voted for Mr. Trump in 2024 Presidential Election. _See, e.g._ , _Casellas-Toro_ , 807 F.3d at 386-87 (1st Cir. 2015) (finding a presumption of prejudice warranted even though “more than 3 million people live in [the venue]”); _Powell v. Superior Ct_ ., 283 Cal. Rptr. 777, 783, 788 (Cal. Ct. App. 1991) (even though the potential jury pool in Los Angeles County was 6.526 million, a change of venue of the case involving the beating of Rodney King was required because “there is a substantial probability Los Angeles County is so saturated with knowledge of the incident, so influenced by the political controversy surrounding the matter and so permeated with preconceived opinions that potential jurors cannot try the case solely upon the evidence presented in the courtroom.”). 

Given the Court’s findings, and considering the nature and extent of the prejudicial publicity in this capital case and the relatively small size of the eligible jury pool in Utah County, 

141 

this factor weighs in favor of a finding that there is a reasonable likelihood that further electronic 

media live streaming will prejudice the right of the parties to a fair proceeding. 

### **(3) the nature and gravity of the offense** 

As indicated above _,_ in _State v. James_ , the Utah Supreme Court held that the fact that the State is seeking the death penalty is an important factor weighing in favor of a finding that there is a reasonable likelihood that media coverage will prejudice the right to a fair trial. As instructed by _James_ , in a capital case, “[t]he judgment should be made in an atmosphere as free from any taint of bias or prejudice as is reasonably possible.” 767 P.2d at 555. As explained in _Martinez_ , “[t]he peculiar facts or aspects of a crime which make it sensational, or otherwise bring it to the consciousness of the community, define its ‘nature’; the term ‘gravity’ of a crime refers to its seriousness in the law and to the possible consequences to an accused in the event of a guilty verdict.” 629 P.2d at 506. 

Here, the peculiar facts or aspects of the crime which make it sensational are obvious. From the beginning, the crime has been characterized by the President and his supporters, including the Governor, as a cold-blooded “political assassination” that has had a profound impact on the entire nation. The electronic media has dutifully broadcast that sensational 

characterization across the nation on an almost daily basis. And as in _Martinez_ , 

The seriousness of the charged crime stands out clearly. Murder is a crime of utmost gravity; inasmuch as the state is seeking the death penalty, it is a crime of the gravest consequences to petitioner. Because it carries such grave consequences, a death penalty case inherently attracts press coverage; in such a case the factor of gravity must weigh heavily in a determination regarding [remedial measures]. 

_Id_ . at 507. 

142 

### **(4) the nature and extent of publicity** . 

The sensationalistic and prejudicial nature and overwhelming extent of the prejudicial electronic and other pretrial publicity in Mr. Robinson’s case is set forth above at length. That publicity features every one of the several categories of publicity that courts and other authorities have recognized as inherently prejudicial. _See, e.g._ , _Skilling_ , 561 U.S. at 382 (a presumption of juror prejudice is warranted where the pretrial publicity references a “confession or other blatantly prejudicial information of the type readers or viewers could not reasonably be expected to shut from sight.”); _Gentile_ , 501 U.S. at 1046 (noting that public statements that are “obvious bases for a finding of prejudice “ include mention of a confession or of “evidence from searches or test results.”). _See also supra_ , fn. 4 (setting forth the categories of prejudicial publicity that are considered inherently prejudicial under governing standards). 

In addition to featuring every category of prejudicial pretrial publicity recognized in _Skilling_ , _Gentile_ , and the relevant standards, the prejudicial electronic media publicity in this case has also heavily stressed several additional sensationalistic themes that courts have recognized as necessitating strong remedial measures. 

For example, in _Maine_ , the court stressed that “[p]olitical factors have no place in a criminal proceeding, and when they are likely to appear, as here, they constitute an independent reason for [requiring remedial action].” 438 P.2d at 380. _See also People v. Tidwell_ 473 P.2d 748, 754 (Cal. 1970) (en banc) (news items indicating there was “considerable political debate concerning the fiscal impact of the trial,” was one of the factors supporting a change of venue). 

Similarly, in _Powell,_ political factors required a change of venue from Los Angeles County, the court concluding that “[w]hile we recognize that the incident and some of its ramifications have received widespread publicity elsewhere, the impact on residents of Los 

143 

Angeles is unquestionably much greater because of the unabated and acrimonious total involvement of city officials and local community leaders. There is no doubt that these political biases would invade the jury box if the case were tried in Los Angeles County.” 283 Cal. Rptr. at 787. 

As in _Tidwell_ , Mr. Robinson’s case is also one where there has been extensive debate in the electronic media about the cost of the trial.<sup>38</sup> However, the prejudicial political rhetoric here has gone far beyond a concern for the cost of the trial and has featured a relentless drumbeat of electronic media interviews of a Republican President, Vice President, Attorney General, FBI Director, White House staff, and the Governor, all pushing the narrative that Mr. Robinson is somehow part of a “radical left” conspiracy that must be stamped out to protect the republic. As in _Powell_ , the impact of this inflammatory political rhetoric in the predominantly Republican Utah County is “unquestionably much greater because of the unabated and acrimonious total involvement of city officials and local [state, and federal] community leaders.” 283 Cal. Rptr, at 787. 

38 _See, e.g._ , KSL NEWS UTAH, _Utah County grapples with looming legal costs of accused Charlie Kirk killer prosecution, defense_ , (YouTube, Sep. 25, 2025), <u>https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=O1ltB2zhd_U; Amelia Hobson,</u> _Utah County Commission discuss cost of Tyler Robinson’s defense_ , ABC 4 (Sep.26, 2025 at 10:18 PM MT), <u>https://www.abc4.com/news/charlie-kirk-shooting/tyler-robinson-defense-cost/; Daniel Gura,</u> _EXCLUSIVE: Charlie Kirk ‘Assassin’ Tyler Robinson’s $750K High-Priced Public Defense Team Expected to ‘Impact’ Utah Budget as Outrage Grows over Public Footing the Bill_ , AOL (Sep. 30, 2025), https://www.aol.com/entertainment/exclusive-charlie-kirk-assassin-tyler- <u>194500999.html; Stephanie Price,</u> _Taxpayer cost for suspected Charlie Kirk assassin’s death penalty case predicted by Utah commissioner,_ FOX NEWS (Nov. 2, 2025 _,_ at 2:00 PM ET) 

<u>https://www.foxnews.com/us/taxpayer-cost-suspected-charlie-kirk-assassins-death-penalty-casepredicted-utah-commissioner.</u> 

144 

Another dominant theme in the electronic media coverage has been an almost 

pathological focus on Mr. Robinson’s mug shots, shackles, jail clothes, alleged incriminating demeanor, body language, and lip movements, and his alleged refusal to cooperate with law enforcement. Here, as long as the cameras are allowed to remain, Mr. Robinson is in a no-win situation. If he sits in court without any facial expression or verbal communication with his attorneys he will be labelled by the media as a stone-faced, remorseless sociopath. In fact, that was exactly the commentary during the Sheriff’s September 16, 2025 press conference. On the other hand, if he attempts to engage with his lawyers in a normal human interaction and happens to smile at something that has nothing to do with his case, then he will be labelled as a smirking remorseless sociopath. 

This Court has correctly found that “[p]hotographs and visual recordings of Mr. Robinson in jail clothing will likely be shared and disseminated in various public platforms”, that there is “a strong likelihood that a substantial number of potential jurors in Utah County will view these images and may associate Mr. Robinson’s jail clothing with indicia of guilt”, that “[t]he danger of prejudice is compounded by technological advancements that allow the images to be manipulated in ways that do not accurately reflect the truth of the court proceedings”, and that “[v]isual recordings and photographs of Mr. Robinson shackled may give the impression that he is dangerous and may undermine his presumption of innocence”, and that “the potential jury pool in Utah County, based on their proximity, is likely to be even more exposed to the images of these proceedings and the messages those images send.” These findings are consistent with the conclusions reached by the United States Supreme Court in _Deck_ , 544 U.S. at 633, and should lead the Court to conclude that in light of how the electronic media has chosen to cover the 

145 

proceedings, and the likelihood that such sensationalistic coverage will continue, that the Court will not allow any further live streaming or photographing of any of the proceedings. 

The electronic media, guided by commentators like the Vice President and the Governor, also seem to be unusually fixated on Mr. Robinson’s alleged romantic relationship with his roommate and with the fact that the roommate is apparently transitioning from a male to a female. And this fixation has continued even though the Governor in his September 14, 2025 CNN interview could not come up with any explanation as to the legal relevance of these facts. The Governor mentioned these themes in all three of his national broadcasts on September 14, 2025, and the Vice President, against the backdrop of a large fireworks display and huge applause, announced at a recent Turning Point event on December 21, 2015 that Mr. Robinson was a person who is “everything that the far left wants from our young men [who] rejected the conservatism and the spirituality, the values of a small-town family [and]…moved into a small apartment, he became addicted to porn, he became addicted to hate, and he ended up sleeping with somebody who doesn’t know whether they’re a man or a woman”. Faced with a similar situation, the court in _Frazier v. Superior Ct_ ., concluded: 

We recognize that in some criminal trials there is at least the possibility, regrettably, that the defendant may be judged on his appearance and life style rather than on his proven acts. In the circumstances here shown, however, that consequence is more probable than possible…. There may not be, as the trial court found, hostility toward this particular defendant as an individual; but so long as there is hostility towards the group or subculture with which he is identified, he runs the very real risk of being judged not for what he has done but for what he is, or what he appears to be. 

486 P.2d at 699. 

The community’s involvement in the hunt for the killer and the electronic media’s outpouring of support for Mr. Kirk and his family are also important factors to consider when assessing the nature and extent of prejudicial publicity. Here, as reported by FBI Director Kash 

146 

Patel on September 12, 2025, “[l]ast night, we had a total of approximately 7,000 interviews— excuse me, 7,000 leads. As of this morning, thanks to your great work, we have over 11,000 leads that were called in to the FBI, and we are running out every single lead that we can. Every one of those leads will be run out.” _See supra_ , Kash Patel, _Director Patel’s Remarks._ Consistent with this widespread local community sentiment, the electronic media has produced lengthy tributes to Mr. Kirk and have closely followed all aspects of his family’s grieving process, where the event has been attended by thousands and online viewings of such events are in the millions. As was concluded in _Maine_ , “[w]e do not hold it to be an invariable rule that sympathy for a victim demonstrates antipathy to the alleged perpetrators of an offense. But such pervasive civic involvement in the fate of a victim, particularly when the events all transpire in a relatively small community, is a strong indication that [remedial measures] should be [employed].” 438 P.2d at 379 (footnotes omitted). 

A final factor to consider when assessing the nature and extent of prejudicial publicity in this case is the fact, discussed more fully below, that such publicity, especially in the electronic media, has focused on violent rhetoric, which in turn has already led to threats against and harassment of trial participants, including counsel and Mr. Robinson’s parents. _See_ Exs. 3, 4. As was the case in _Powell_ , “[s]uch unacceptable attempts to influence the judicial proceedings at this early stage add another impermissible factor into the boiling cauldron surrounding this case, making it imperative to take every step possible to ensure that an impartial unbiased jury be seated.” 283 Cal. Rptr. at 787. 

In sum, for all of the foregoing reasons, there is clearly a reasonable likelihood that continuing to allow the electronic media to live-stream all proceedings in this case will prejudice 

147 

Mr. Robinson’s fundamental State and Federal constitutional rights to fair, impartial, and reliable proceedings. 

### **(ii) whether there is a reasonable likelihood that electronic media coverage will jeopardize the safety or well-being of any individual;** 

This Court has already found that “[t]his case generates ongoing national and 

international attention, some of which is threatening in nature to the parties, their representatives, and non-party participants (e.g. witnesses and court personal).” Dkt. 265, at 2. 

Indeed, a prominent feature of the prejudicial publicity in this case, coming from the 

highest sources of government, is the unfounded accusation that Mr. Robinson killed Mr. Kirk at the behest of or in direct response to the rhetoric of the “radical left”, and that such rhetoric and Mr. Robinson’s alleged actions have put the nation on the brink of a civil war that will be averted only if there is “crackdown” on Mr. Robinson and all of this alleged supporters. 

As indicated above, on the evening of the killing, the President spoke to the nation in a televised statement in which he said that “Charlie was the best of America, and the monster who attacked him was attacking our whole country”, that the rhetoric of the “radical left” was 

“directly responsible for the terrorism that we’re seeing in our country today, and it must stop right now”, and that his administration “will find each and every one of those who contributed to this atrocity and to other political violence, including the organizations that fund it and support it, as well as those who go after our judges, law enforcement officials, and everyone else who 

brings order to our country.” Chad DeGuzman, _”A Dark Moment for America”: Trump Responds to Charlie Kirk’s Death_ , TIME (Sep. 10, 2025, at 7:55 PM MT) 

<u>https://time.com/7316299/charlie-kirk-shot-death-donald-trump-speech-transcript-politicalviolence/ =</u> (publicizing the full text of the President’s statement). 

148 

On the same day, the President’s supporters took to social media platforms to repeat and expand upon the President’s remarks in statements that were widely reported. _See, e.g.,_ Sheera Frenkel & Stuart A. Thompson, _‘Civil War’ Mentions Surge Online After Kirk Assassination_ , NY TIMES, (Sep. 12, 2025), https://www.nytimes.com/2025/09/12/technology/charlie-kirk- <u>shooting-civil-war.html (“Minutes after the shooting of the right-wing influencer Charlie Kirk in</u> Utah on Wednesday, calls for civil war began appearing online. The term ‘civil war’ was mentioned on Wednesday more than 129,000 times on the social platform X, according to an analysis by The New York Times. On Thursday, it was mentioned at least 210,000 times…. Elon Musk, X’s owner and the most-followed person on the platform, had one of the most widely shared and liked posts evoking war after Mr. Kirk’s death. ‘If they won’t leave us in peace, then our choice is fight or die,’ he wrote. His post was viewed more than 1.7 million times and liked over 37,000 times.’”); Chad DeGuzman, _”This Is War” Trump and Others on Right Call for Crackdown on Left After Charlie Kirk’s Murder_ , TIME, (Sep. 11, 2025, at 12:15 AM MT) <u>https://time.com/7316315/republicans-far-right-reacts-charlie-kirk-death-blame-left-</u> 

<u>crackdown/?utm_source=chatgpt.com (documenting numerous other similar statements).</u> 

Those remarks included statements such as: 

- “It’s a real treat to see all these Liberals condemn political violence now. You called us Hitler. You called us Nazis. You called us Racists. You have blood on your hands.” (Katie Miller, the wife of White House deputy chief of staff Stephen Miller); 

- “The Left is the party of murder” (Elon Musk); 

- “The Left lectured us for the last decade about the dangers of violence from the Right … The danger was actually on the Left” (Shaun Maguire); 

- “The Left are terrorists… You could be next…We must shut these lunatic leftists down. Once and for all. The Left is a national security threat” (Laura Loomer); 

- the Democratic Party is “a domestic terrorist organization whose most fanatics will not hesitate to murder their political opponents” (Sean Davis). 

149 

_Id_ . 

Some of this inflamed rhetoric expressly warns of the imminent threat of a new “civil 

war” and the urgent need to take action: 

- “The left and their policies are leading America into a civil war. And they want it. Just like the democrat party wanted our 1st civil war. The gloves are off …May the mighty wrath of vengeance fall upon the Brown Shirts who are responsible for this” (Republican Rep. Derrick Van Orden); “THIS IS WAR” (Chaya Raichik); 

- “Civil war” (Andrew Tate); 

- “This is a new civil war” (Kristan Hawkins); 

- “I’m ready for civil war…You want a fight and you’re going to get it.” (Brian Eastwood); 

- “Whether we want to accept it or not, they are at war with us. And what are we going to do about it? How much political violence are we going to tolerate?” (Jesse Watters); 

- “I’m praying that [Kirk] survives. And I’m praying that this country rises up and ends this.” (Marjorie Taylor Greene); 

- “The Left will not stop until they are forced to. The Right must gain power, keep power, and wield power righteously” (Joel Webbon);
