- “It’s time to bring the hammer down on the left” (Patrick Casey); 

- “It is time for a complete crackdown on the left. Every Democratic politician must be arrested and the party banned under RICO [Racketeer Influenced and Corrupt Organizations Act]. Every libtard commentator must be shut down. Stochastic terrorism. They caused this.” (Matt Forney). 

_Id_ . This call-to-arms in this and other highly publicized cases has had a definite impact on the 

attitudes of the public. _See_ Shannon Bond, _Kirk shooting videos spread online, even to viewers who didn’t want to see them_ , NPR, (Sep. 18, 2025, at 5:00 AM ET) 

<u>https://www.npr.org/2025/09/18/nx-s1-5542231/kirk-shooting-violence-social-media (reporting</u> 

on recent public opinion surveys that have found that “up to 60% of Americans say violence may be justified if people from the other political party commit a violent act first.”) 

150 

Given this widespread and frightening attitude and the vehemence of the fire and 

brimstone rhetoric in this case, it would be naïve to think that Mr. Robinson, his attorneys, the prosecution team, the witnesses, the jurors, and the Court itself and its staff would not be at serious risk of injury if the Court were to permit their images and conduct to be broadcast to the world on a prolonged and eventually daily basis in this highly emotionally charged case.<sup>39</sup> 

As Judge Kozinski wrote in his article, “When the public sees a trial for itself, or through the lens of the camera, there’s always a risk of misunderstanding: The public may mistake zealous advocacy for obstruction of justice, or vice versa.” Kozinski, at 1123. 

All it would take to lead to violence would be one unhinged and violent person who was influenced by the inflammatory rhetoric in this case, was offended by Mr. Robinson, or the performance of counsel on either side, or the Court, or other trial participants, and felt it was his or her patriotic duty in this new “civil war” to engage in violence against the perceived “enemy.” Indeed, one of the Court’s justification for restraining Mr. Robinson in the courtroom was for his own safety, with the Court observing that “though these proceedings will focus on legal matters, both Mr. Robinson and court patrons are likely to feel strong emotions.” Dkt. 137, at 6. But restraints on Mr. Robinson, and armed guards in combat gear in the courtroom, while they will 

go a long way in projecting the prejudicial image of Mr. Robinson as guilty and dangerous and therefore deserving of death, will do nothing to stop the threat of violence outside the courtroom. 

39 As will be demonstrated at the hearing of this motion, Ms. Nester has already received death and other threats of violence or harassment, as have Mr. Robinson’s parents. _See_ Ex. 3, 4. It must also be noted that although the court’s three decorum orders all contain the provision that “[m]edia representatives shall use their best efforts to avoid visually recording or photographing court employees-except for the judge-without prior approval”, and that “[t]his includes court security staff, clerks, and judicial assistants”, this aspect of the order has already been violated. _See_ NEW YORK POST, _Charlie Kirk Murder Suspect Said He Thinks About the Shooting ‘Everyday’: Lipreader_ referenced above, which contains prolonged shots of the Court’s security staff. 

151 

That threat cannot be entirely eliminated even if electronic media coverage is not permitted, but the presence of electronic media will certainly put the permanent visual spotlight on all trial participants and will therefore increase the likelihood of violence to each and every one of the participants. 

As stated in _Moussaoui_ : 

The traditional public spectator or media representative who attends a federal criminal trial leaves the courtroom with his or her memory of the proceedings and any notes he or she may have taken. These spectators do not leave with a permanent photograph. However, once a witness’ testimony has been televised, the witness’ face has not just been publicly observed, it has also become eligible for preservation by VCR or DVD recording, digitizing by the new generation of cameras or permanent placement on Internet web sites and chat rooms. Today, it is not so much the small, discrete cameras or microphones in the courtroom that are likely to intimidate witnesses, rather, it is the witness’ knowledge that his or her face or voice may be forever publicly known and available to anyone in the world…. Even if every witness’ face were obscured, the faces of the attorneys, court staff, and security officers would be exposed, subjecting them to the possibility of long-term security problems. 

205 F.R.D. at 186-87. _See also Perry v. City & Cnty. of San Francisco_ , No. 10-16696, 2011 WL 

2419868, at *78 (9th Cir. Apr. 27, 2011) (“The presence of cameras in the courtroom is likely to heighten the level and the potential of threats to judges. The number of threats against judges has escalated over the years, and widespread media exposure could exacerbate the problem. Additionally, all witnesses, jurors, and United States Marshals Service personnel may be put at risk because they would no longer have a low public profile.”(Statement of Chief Judge Becker before the United States Senate)). 

There is also a legitimate concern in this case that because the threats of violence and 

“civil war” are originating from President Trump and his supporters, that the trial participants will be so distracted by security concerns that they will be hindered in the performance of their duties. As concluded in _United States v. Trump_ , 

152 

Messages designed to generate alarm and dread, and to trigger extraordinary safety precautions, will necessarily hinder the trial process and slow the administration of justice. For example, trial personnel and participants will be distracted or delayed by objectively reasonable concerns about their safety and that of their family members, as well as by having to devote time and resources to adopting safety measures or working with investigators. 

88 F.4th at 1014. 

As most recently observed by the New York Times, “[t]oday’s algorithmically drivenattention hungry information ecosystem does more than distort reality. It also stokes anger that divides society along political or social lines…For those caught in the vortex of an internet storm-fairly or not- the experience can be traumatic (and dangerous).” Steven Meyers, _Top Leaders Fuel a Surge In Falsehoods: A Traditional Restraint on Rumors Fades_ , at A2, A12, NY TIMES (Dec. 27, 2025). _See also_ , Ex. 2, ¶ 17 (Dr. Ruva: “Conventional, and social media, and especially live stream publicity of or about traumatic events such as assassinations, terrorism, and other mass acts of violence can cause trauma and emotional responses such as anger, disgust, and fear that distort decision-making and lead to bias.”). 

There is also another aspect to the issue of whether there is a reasonable likelihood that electronic media coverage will jeopardize the safety or well-being of any individual. As aptly summarized: 

The tragedy also highlights risks that will shape America’s trajectory in the coming years. In the short term, there is a heightened danger of copycat actions or retaliatory violence, as ideologically motivated individuals draw inspiration from either the act itself or the reactions to it. Security requirements for public figures—already significant—will only intensify. In the medium term, the event is likely to deepen polarization, as each side interprets the killing through its own tribal lens. This will provide fertile ground for online radicalization and recruitment, further eroding social cohesion. In the long term, unless these dynamics are checked, the United States faces the prospect of democratic backsliding. Trust, empathy, and tolerance could continue to diminish, while political violence becomes normalized as a form of expression rather than condemned as an aberration. 

153 

_A Nation Divided: What the Charlie Kirk Shooting Reveals About America_ , THE SOUFAN 

CENTER (Sep. 12, 2025), https://thesoufancenter.org/intelbrief-2025-september-12, 

Many of the media outlets in this case have compared this case to the Kennedy 

assassination. Whether that comparison is accurate or not has itself been the subject of fierce debate.<sup>40</sup> But one aspect of the Kennedy assassination and its aftermath does have relevance to the issue considered here. As was found in the Warren Commission Report, the media’s clamor for unrestrained access to Mr. Oswald combined with the media’s overreliance on statements made by law enforcement presented serious obstacles to the obtaining of a fair trial for Oswald 

and contributed to his murder before he ever had the opportunity for a fair trial: 

Newspaper, radio, and television reporters were allowed _uninhibited access_ to the area through which Oswald had to pass when he was moved from his cell to the interrogation room and other section of the building, thereby subjecting Oswald to harassment and creating chaotic conditions which were not conductive to orderly interrogation or the protection of the rights of the prisoner. 

40 Perfectly illustrating the type of falsehoods that can get generated in today’s “algorithmically driven-attention hungry information ecosystem”, when ChatGPT is queried about reactions to the controversy about this case’s comparison to the JFK assassination, it responds: 



<!-- Start of picture text -->
Before summarizing reactions, | need to correct a key point to avoid spreading misinformation:<br>Charlie Kirk has not been assassinated.<br>He is alive, and there has been no real event comparable to the Kennedy assassination involving him.<br><!-- End of picture text -->



<!-- Start of picture text -->
What did happen is that some commentators and social-media users used exaggerated or hypothetical<br>thetoric, comparing imagined harm, threats, or political controversy around Charlie Kirk to the JFK<br>assassination. Those comparisons themselves became the subject of media criticism and debate.<br>With that clarified, here’s how supporters vs. critics reacted to the comparison itself ©<br><!-- End of picture text -->

Someone subscribing to the view that Mr. Kirk is alive could well take violent offense at the prosecution team’s attempt to prosecute Mr. Robinson. _See Estes_ , 381 U.S. at 547 (“[I]nquisitive strangers and ‘cranks’ might approach witnesses on the street with jibes, advice or demands for explanation of testimony. There is little wonder that the defendant cannot ‘prove’ the existence of such factors. Yet we all know from experience that they exist.”) 

154 

The numerous statements, sometimes erroneous, made to the press by various law enforcement officials, during this period of confusion and disorder in the police station, _would have presented serious obstacles to the obtaining of a fair trial for Oswald_ . To the extent that the information was erroneous or misleading, it helped to create doubts, speculations, and fears in the mind of the public which might otherwise not have arisen. 

The Dallas Police Department’s decision to transfer Oswald to the county jail in full public view was unsound. The arrangements made by the police department on Sunday morning, only a few hours before the attempted transfer, were inadequate. _Of critical importance was the fact that news media representatives and others were not excluded from the basement even after the police were notified of threats to Oswald’s life. These deficiencies contributed to the death of Lee Harvey Oswald._ 

_Report of the President’s Commission on the Assassination of President Kennedy_ , at 20-21, (Sep. 

24, 1964) (“Warren Report”), _available at_ https://www.archives.gov/research/jfk/warrencommission-report/toc. 

The danger of violence in the Oswald case, created by “uninhibited access” by the media, coupled with the media’s reliance on law enforcement sources to create “doubts, speculations, and fears in the mind of the public” is exponentially greater in this case where gavel to gavel 

electronic media coverage would inevitably get processed though today’s algorithmically drivenattention hungry information ecosystem that in this case is being fueled by the President and his supporters. 

As was concluded in _United States v. Trump_ , 

Given the record in this case, the court ha[s] a duty to act proactively to prevent the creation of an atmosphere of fear or intimidation aimed at preventing trial participants and staff from performing their functions within the trial process. Just as a court is duty-bound to prevent a trial from devolving into a carnival, see _Sheppard_ , 384 U.S. at 357-358…, so too can it prevent trial participants and staff from having to operate under siege. 

88 F.4th at 1014. 

155 

For this and the other reasons discussed above, this factor not only weighs heavily in favor of prohibiting further electronic media live streaming or photographing in this particular capital case, but is alone sufficient to justify termination of such coverage, which the Court can do under Rule 4-401.01(5) without making any detailed factual findings since the media has already violated the decorum order on more than one occasion in a manner that directly threatens both fair trial and security interests. 

### **(iii) whether there is a reasonable likelihood that electronic media coverage will jeopardize the interests or well-being of a minor;** 

In his appearance on Meet the Press on September 14, 2025, Governor Cox stated that: 

I can’t emphasize enough the damage that social media and the internet is doing to all of us. Those dopamine hits. These companies, trillion-dollar market caps, the most powerful companies in the history of the world, have figured out how to hack our brains, get us addicted to outrage, which is the same type of dopamine, the same chemical that you get from taking fentanyl, get us addicted to outrage and get us to hate each other. I’m seeing it in real time since the tragic death of Charlie Kirk. I’m seeing it in every corner of our society. The conflict entrepreneurs are taking advantage of us and we are losing our agency. And we have to take that back…. 

I believe that social media has played a direct role in every single assassination and assassination attempt that we have seen over the last five, six years…There is no question in my mind. Cancer is probably not a strong enough word. What we have done especially to our kids. It took us a decade to realize how evil these algorithms are and we’re doing everything in Utah. First state in the nation a couple of years ago to pass comprehensive reform. Sadly, these most powerful companies on earth are suing us to prevent us from implementing these things. 

_See supra_ , Stelloh, et al., _Suspect in Charlie Kirk’s killing,_ at 4:55. at 4:55. 

As alluded to by the Governor, the federal courts have so far blocked pioneering efforts 

by the Utah Legislature to control the flow of harmful social media content to minors. _See_ Utah Minor Protection in Social Media Act, Utah Code §§ 13-71-101 to 401. Two legislative findings in support of that legislation were that “the state has a compelling interest in safeguarding the 

156 

well-being and privacy of minors in the state”, and that “prolonged and unregulated social media use has been linked to adverse effects on the mental health of minors, including increased rates of anxiety, depression, and social isolation.” Utah Code § 13-71-102. 

A federal district court has issued a preliminary injunction barring the enforcement of the Act on the ground that the plaintiff social media association was likely to succeed on the merits of its claim that the Act violated its members’ First Amendment rights. _NetChoice, LLC v. Reyes_ , 748 F. Supp. 3d 1105 (D. Utah 2024). That decision is currently on appeal to the Tenth Circuit. _See NetChoice v. Brown_ , _et al_ , Case No. 24-4100 (10th Cir., Oct. 11, 2024). Because of the pendency of this appeal, there are currently in force no effective controls in Utah on the use of social media by minors. This means that in this case, unless this Court cuts off the steady stream of hateful, violent, fabricated imagery and narratives, and downright false conspiracy and political rhetoric that has flowed directly from the electronic media coverage of this case, through the amplifying and distorting filter of social media, to the eyes and ears of all Utah citizens, then all minors in Utah who use social media will be exposed to such toxic coverage.<sup>41</sup> 

Even if there was a First Amendment right to record and broadcast court proceedings, which there clearly is not, the State’s “compelling interest in safeguarding the well-being …of minors in the state” would clearly outweigh the existence of such a hypothetical right in this case, given the false and incendiary nature of the electronic media coverage that has pervaded this case. _See Globe Newspaper_ , 457 U.S. at 607 (finding that “safeguarding the physical and 

41 _See Social Media and Youth Mental Health: The U.S. Surgeon General’s Advisory_ , at 4 (2023), _available at_ 

<u>https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK594761/pdf/Bookshelf_NBK594761.pdf (reporting</u> that “[u]p to 95% of youth ages 13-17 report using a social media platform, with more than a third saying they use social media ‘almost constantly.’”). 

157 

psychological well-being of a minor” in the context of a sex-crimes case was a compelling 

interest that can on a case-by-case-basis override any First Amendment right of access.). 

### **(iv) whether there is a reasonable likelihood that electronic media coverage will constitute an unwarranted invasion of personal privacy of any person;** 

In _Lauro v. Charles_ , after arresting and charging Lauro as a criminal defendant, the 

police handcuffed Lauro, walked him outside the station house, drove him around the block in an unmarked police car, and walked Lauro back to the police station so that a television news crew could film him in a staged “perp walk.” 219 F.3d 202, 203 (2d Cir.2000). Lauro’s civil suit against the police department and police officers asserted that the perp walk was an unreasonable seizure that violated his privacy rights under the Fourth Amendment. _Id_ . at 208. 

_Lauro_ stated that Fourth Amendment privacy protection applies to a person not only during an initial search and seizure by police, but also to persons lawfully in police custody. _Id._ at 212. Lauro was lawfully under arrest. But with regard to the “perp walk” occurring after arrest, viewing that “seizure” either as (1) a separate seizure occurring when Lauro was forcibly removed from the police station, paraded before news cameras, and then returned to the station, or (2) as “a continuation and aggravation of the seizure that occurred when he was arrested,” the Fourth Amendment required that seizure to be reasonable. _Id_ .; _see also Lauro v. City of New York,_ 39 F. Supp. 2d 351, 363, fn. 8 (S.D.N.Y.1999), _reversed and remanded by Lauro_ , 219 F.3d 202. The Second Circuit concluded that the staged perp walk was unrelated to the object of the arrest, had no legitimate law enforcement justification, and invaded Lauro’s privacy to no purpose, and therefore violated the Fourth Amendment. 219 F.3d at 213. 

In a crucial passage of the opinion, the Court ruled: 

Despite its adverse effects on Lauro’s dignity and privacy, the perp walk might nevertheless have been reasonable under the Fourth Amendment, had it been sufficiently closely related to a legitimate governmental objective… In 

158 

this respect, Charles argues that “the importance of the press in informing the general public about the administration of criminal justice has long been recognized by the Supreme Court,” …., and that this interest suffices to justify the perp walk before us. 

The Supreme Court, however, in [ _Wilson v. Layne_ , 526 U.S. 603 (1999)] explicitly rejected an identical argument when it was proffered in support of the media ride-along in that case. The defendants had asserted three justifications for the presence of the media: (1) that officers should have discretion to decide when it would further the mission of law enforcement to allow media presence; (2) that accurate reporting on law enforcement activities was a significant interest; and (3) that the presence of media could deter law enforcement abuses. _See_ [ _id_ . at 612-13]. The Court found that none of these justifications was sufficient to overcome the Wilsons’ Fourth Amendment rights, noting, “[w]ere such generalized ‘law enforcement objectives’ themselves sufficient to trump the Fourth Amendment, the protections guaranteed by that Amendment’s text would be significantly watered down.” _Id_ . at 612 …. Moreover, with respect to the argument that the ride-along served to inform the public about the administration of law enforcement, the Court commented: 

There is certainly language in our opinions interpreting the First Amendment which points to the importance of “the press” in informing the general public about the administration of criminal justice.... But the Fourth Amendment also protects a very important right, and in the present case it is in terms of that right that the media ride-alongs must be judged... 

[T]he need for accurate reporting on police issues in general bears no direct relation to the constitutional justification for the police intrusion into a home in order to execute a felony arrest warrant. _Id_ . at 612-13[.]. 

_Lauro_ , 219 F.3d at 212-13. 

_Wilson_ , along with _Ayeni v. Mottola_ , 35 F.3d 680 (2d Cir.1994), both involved media 

videos of a suspect taken in the suspect’s home. But _Lauro_ concluded that: 

It is not the case, however, that the holdings in _Ayeni_ and _Wilson_ turned solely on the special status of the home, or that the Fourth Amendment’s privacy protections end at the door of one’s house. Our court recognized, rather, that the privacy interests threatened in _Ayeni_ went beyond those that attach to the inviolability of the home, and concluded that _obtaining and broadcasting images of the Ayenis in a humiliating situation itself infringed on their Fourth Amendment privacy rights…_ . In doing so, _Ayeni_ was consistent with longstanding Fourth Amendment jurisprudence, which has declined to set spatial boundaries on the rights protected by that amendment. For, as the Supreme 

159 

Court has famously stated, “the Fourth Amendment protects people, not places.” _Katz v. United States_ , 389 U.S. 347, 351 [(1967)]. 

_Lauro_ , 219 F.3d at 211 (emphasis added). 

In the district court, the defendant in _Lauro_ argued that infringement on the plaintiff’s 

privacy rights was justified by the importance of the press in informing the general public about the administration of criminal justice. The district court disagreed, holding that “this Court is convinced beyond doubt that the perp walk procedure is not designed nor intended for the purpose of information dissemination, but rather for the purposes of incident dramatization and arrestee humiliation _.” Lauro v. City of New York,_ 39 F. Supp. 2d at 364, n. 9. 

On appeal, the defendant again raised this argument, and the Second Circuit also rejected it, but on the ground that _Wilson_ established the important Fourth Amendment principle that “the reasonableness of the police’s actions in conducting a search or seizure must be judged, in part, through an assessment of the degree to which those actions further _the legitimate law enforcement purposes behind the search or seizure_ .” _Lauro_ , 219 F.3d at 211 (emphasis added). The capture of the suspect’s image in _Lauro_ served no such interest because “in staging the perp walk, Detective Charles engaged in conduct that was unrelated to the object of the arrest, that had no legitimate law enforcement justification, and that invaded Lauro’s privacy to no purpose. By exacerbating Lauro’s seizure in an unreasonable manner, Charles violated the Fourth Amendment.” _Id._ at 213. _See also State v. Tiedemann_ , 2007 UT 49, ¶ 34, 162 P.3d 1106 (“Utah’s search and seizure provisions (which are identical to those in the federal constitution) provide ‘a greater expectation of privacy than the Fourth Amendment as interpreted by the United States Supreme Court.’”) (quoting _Brigham City v. Stuart,_ 2005 UT 13, ¶ 11, 122 P.3d 506 (“article I, section 14 of the Utah Constitution provides a greater expectation of privacy than the Fourth Amendment as interpreted by the United States Supreme Court.”)). 

160 

Similarly, in this case, allowing the media to film and broadcast to the world close-up images of Mr. Robinson without his consent and while he is being held without bail pursuant to his initial arrest violates his Fourth Amendment and article I, section 14 right to privacy because 

“obtaining and broadcasting images of [him] in a humiliating situation itself infringe[s] on [his] Fourth Amendment privacy rights” and such conduct is unrelated to the object of the arrest and served no legitimate law enforcement justification. _See also Houchins v. KQED, Inc.,_ 438 U.S. 1, 5 n.2 (1978) (plurality opinion) (“Inmates in jails, prisons, or mental institutions retain certain fundamental rights of privacy; they are not like animals in a zoo to be filmed and photographed at will by the public or by media reporters, however ‘educational’ the process may be for others.”); _Demery v. Arpaio_ , 378 F.3d 1020, 1028-29, 1031 (9th Cir. 2004) (holding that streaming live images of pretrial detainees to internet was not reasonably related to a nonpunitive purpose, and thus, violated the Fourteenth Amendment) (“[W]e fail to see how turning pretrial detainees into the unwilling objects of the latest reality show serves any …legitimate goals…. [T]the otherwise valid governmental interest in assuring accountability and public scrutiny cannot justify broad public exposure of pretrial detainees’ intimate circumstances, as none of the purposes justifying pretrial detention are served thereby. The Sheriff’s policy is all the more troubling because displaying images of the County’s pretrial detainees to internet users from around the world is not rationally connected to goals associated with educating the citizenry 

of Maricopa County.”). 

In words of Justice Clark in _Estes_ , 

[W]e cannot ignore the impact of courtroom television on the defendant. Its presence is a form of mental—if not physical—harassment, resembling a police line-up or the third degree. The inevitable close-ups of his gestures and expressions during the ordeal of his trial might well transgress his personal sensibilities, his dignity, and his ability to concentrate on the proceedings before him—sometimes the difference between life and death— 

161 

dispassionately, freely and without the distraction of wide public surveillance. A defendant on trial for a specific crime is entitled to his day in court, not in a stadium, or a city or nationwide arena. The heightened public clamor resulting from radio and television coverage will inevitably result in prejudice. 

381 U.S. at 549. 

Finally, just as Mr. Robinson retains a Fourth Amendment and article I, section 14 

privacy right not to be photographed or filmed without his consent so too does he and other trial participants retain such a right under the state and federal constitutional right to privacy and under general principles of law establishing the right to privacy for all persons. _See generally_ , _Deseret News Publishing_ , 2008 UT 26, ¶ 13 (“[T]the right of individual privacy concerning personal information acquired by governmental entities [is] entitled to constitutional protection.”]; _Stien v. Marriott Ownership Resorts, Inc.,_ 944 P.2d 374, 377-78 (Utah Ct. App. 1997) (recognizing a common law right to privacy which is violated by “(1) intrusion upon the plaintiff’s seclusion or solitude, or into plaintiff’s private affairs, (2) appropriation, for the defendant’s advantage, of the plaintiff’s name or likeness, (3) public disclosure of embarrassing private facts about the plaintiff, and (4) publicity which places the plaintiff in a false light in the public eye.”); _Shattuck-Owen v. Snowbird Corp.,_ 2000 UT 94, ¶ 11, 16 P.3d 555 (“We adopt the standard set forth in _Stien_ .”); _Mau v. Rio Grande Oil_ , 28 F. Supp. 845, 846 (N.D. Cal. 1939) (“The right of privacy, conceding it to exist, is a purely personal one, that is, it is a right of each individual to be let alone, or not to be dragged into publicity.”(internal quotation omitted)). 

In this case where the prosecution is seeking the death penalty, Mr. Robinson’s parents and many other persons, including young students, will be witnesses, either in the guilt phase of the case, or, if there is a conviction of aggravated murder, at a penalty phase. Penalty phase trials inevitably involve the disclosure of intimate and sometimes embarrassing details about the family background and history of the accused and his family, and about the victim’s family. As 

162 

### indicated 

Doubtless many other witnesses will also object to being filmed, but unfortunately Rule 4-401.01 does not provide a notice provision or other mechanism for bringing those objections to the 

attention of the Court. 

As a general matter, however, what United States Court of Appeal Chief Judge Edward 

Becker said about this issue in explaining to the Senate in September 2000 why the Judicial Conference of the United States continues to oppose cameras in the courtroom rings true: 

Broadcasting of trials presents many of the same concerns about privacy as does the indiscriminate dissemination of information on the Internet that was once only available at the courthouse. Witnesses and counsel frequently discuss very sensitive information during the course of a trial. Often this information relates to individuals who are not even parties to the case, but about whom personal information may be revealed. Also, in many criminal and civil trials, which the media would most likely be interested in televising, much of the evidence introduced may be of an extremely private nature, revealing family relationships and personal facts, including medical and financial information. This type of information provided in open court, is already available to the public through the media. Televising these matters sensationalizes these details for no apparent good reason. 

Involvement in a federal case can have a deep and long-lasting impact on all its participants, most of whom have neither asked for nor sought publicity. In this adversarial setting, reputations can be compromised and relationships can be damaged. In fact, according to the [1994] FJC study on live courtroom media coverage, 56% of the participating judges felt that electronic media coverage violates a witness’s privacy. This is not to say that the Conference advocates closed trials; far from it. Nevertheless, there is a common-sense distinction between a public trial in a public courtroom—typically filled with individuals with a real interest in the case—and its elevation to an event that allows and encourages thousands to become involved intimately in a case that essentially concerns a small group of private people or entities. 

The issue of privacy rights is one that has not been adequately considered or addressed by those who would advocate the broadcasting of trials. This heightened awareness of and concern for privacy rights is a relatively new and important development that further supports the position of the Judicial Conference to prohibit the use of cameras in the courtroom. 

163 

_Statement of Judge Becker on behalf of the Judicial Conference of the United States_ , Hrg. 1061029 ( _Allowing Cameras And Electronic Media In The Courtroom_ ) (Sep. 6, 2000), reprinted in _Perry v. City & Cnty. of San Francisco,_ No. 10-16696, 2011 WL 2419868, at *78-79 (9th Cir. Apr. 27, 2011). 

Because Mr. Robinson and other trial participants have important privacy rights that would be violated by filming them without their consent this factor weighs heavily against permitting continued electronic media coverage in the circumstances of this case. 

- **(v) whether electronic media coverage will create adverse effects greater than those caused by media coverage without recording or transmitting images or sound;** 

This issue has been empirically addressed in several studies which are reviewed in the 

attached declaration of Dr. Christine L. Ruva, PH.D. _See_ Ex.2 ¶¶ 20-24. As she concludes in 

paragraph 24: 

Collectively, these studies on modality effect of information presentation demonstrate that visual and multimedia evidence pose a substantially greater risk of prejudicing jurors than auditory evidence or print formats. Visual formats—especially graphic images, video re-creations, and televised coverage—amplify emotional responses, increase perceptions of guilt, and alter liability assessments, while auditory evidence exerts minimal biasing effect. Moreover, jurors often fail to recognize their susceptibility to these influences, and judicial instructions have proven largely ineffective in mitigating such biases. These findings strongly support the need for careful judicial scrutiny of highly visual or emotionally charged evidence and extensive pretrial publicity to preserve the defendant’s constitutional right to a fair trial. 

_Accord_ , Mary Angela Bock, _Framing the Accused: The Perp Walk as Media Ritual_ , 22 VISUAL 

COMM. QUARTERLY 2016 206, 218 (2015), https://doi.org/10.1080/15551393.2015.1105104 

(“Experimental research has shown that when in conflict, visual messages trump the linguistic, 

which means that when coupled with video of a perp walk, the word ‘alleged’ may be 

meaningless.”). 

164 

As this Court found, “[p]hotographs and visual recordings of Mr. Robinson in jail 

clothing will likely be shared and disseminated in various public platforms” and “there is a strong likelihood that a substantial number of potential jurors in Utah County will view these images and may associate Mr. Robinson’s jail clothing with indicia of guilt.” Dkt. 137, at 5. The Court also found that “[v]isual recordings and photographs of Mr. Robinson shackled may give the impression that he is dangerous and may undermine his presumption of innocence.” _Id_ . at 6. Critically, in reference to the present factor, the Court also found that the “danger of prejudice is compounded by technological advancements that allow the images to be manipulated in ways that do not accurately reflect the truth of the court proceedings.” _Id_ . at 5. Although the Court has resolved the issued of jail attire, the problem of shackles remains, and as the Court correctly perceived, if the Court allows capturing of images in the courtroom there is nothing to prevent the creation of false images such as the following:

_Unraveled Crime, Kirk Shooting: Street Clothes or Shackles? Shocking Courtroom Debate in Tyler Robinson’s Case_ (YouTube, Oct. 28, 2025), (https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AL- <u>zTcldsYQ)</u> 

The problem is not confined to real or fake images of Mr. Robinson in shackles, but also exists with respect to phony lip reader, body language, or any other type of junk science “analysis” of Mr. Robinson’s in-court demeanor, appearance, lip movement, or gestures, and also extends to manipulation of courtroom scenes to create the impression of “court chaos”, or “shocking” developments. And as Judge Brinkema pointed out in _United States v. Moussaoui_ , the worst part of the problem is that once these fake images are created, they “become eligible for preservation by …the new generation of cameras or permanent placement on Internet web sites and chat rooms (and) may be forever publicly known and available to anyone in the world.” 205 F.R.D. at 187. This means that a prospective juror browsing the internet six months from now, or even a year from now, could easily come across the lip reader confession video which was filmed in blatant violation of the Court’s decorum order, a result that would be devastating to Mr. Robinson’s right to a fair trial. This factor, when considered in light of the extremely damaging material that the electronic media has already created in violation of the decorum order, and the greater likelihood of prejudice visual images create weighs heavily in favor of prohibiting any further live streaming or court photography. 

166 

### **(vi) the adequacy of the court’s physical facilities for electronic media coverage;** 

The court’s physical facilities do not seem to be large enough to accommodate electronic media coverage without jeopardizing other interests protected by the court’s decorum orders. 

All three of the court’s decorum orders provide: “Media representatives shall use their best efforts to avoid visually recording or photographing court employees-except for the judgewithout prior approval. This includes court security staff, clerks, and judicial assistants.” Dkt. 28, at 5, ¶ 40; Dkt. 138, at 5, ¶ 40; Dkt. 265, at 5, ¶ 41. They also provide that “No visual recording is permitted of a person’s lips, so as to be decipherable by a lip reader, during conferences involving counsel at counsel tables or bench conferences with the judge.” Dkt. 28, at 5, ¶ 45; Dkt. 138, at 5-6, ¶ 47; Dkt. 265, at 6, ¶ 48. 

The broadcast of the court appearance on September 29, 2025, which is still online today, shows multiple clear images of court security staff, clerks, and judicial assistants. _See_ THE SALT LAKE TRIBUNE, _Tyler Robinson, accused of killing Charlie Kirk, has second court hearing_ (YouTube, Sep. 29, 2025), https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fX5LxStO_yI (3.1 million views as of January 11, 2026). 

At the appearance on December 11, 2025, the camera was originally set up in the jury box, which would of course not be feasible during the trial itself. The jury box was in close proximity to the defense table and allowed the camera to zoom in on Mr. Robinson’s face, lips, and handcuffs, and to read documents at the defense team table, resulting in the “lip reader” and “body language” broadcasts referenced above. The close proximity of the camera to the defense table also made it difficult for the lawyers to speak in confidence to Mr. Robinson. After it was discovered that the media was violating the decorum order, the camera was relocated, but as indicated above at least one person reported that Mr. Robinson’s restraints were still visible. 

167 

At both camera angles, the courtroom is small enough that the camera person could have and did pick up clear images of the court’s staff, an obvious violation of the decorum order that creates an irreversible security risk once the live stream is posted on the internet. 

Also, at both camera angles, and indeed at any location in the small courtroom, the camera person will still have the capability to zoom in on Mr. Robinson’s face and lips, which means that if electronic media coverage is allowed to continue into the trial under the court’s current decorum order, both Mr. Robinson and his lawyers will only be able to communicate orally if they cup their hands over their lips. This will create yet another prejudicial image for the media to zoom in on and broadcast during pretrial proceedings, which will no doubt be used by pundits and “experts” as further evidence of Mr. Robinson’s guilt, the same conclusion that a jury will likely draw if such conversation takes place in their presence, and this inference will be all the more compelling to those who have viewed the New York Post’s online video of the lip reader “confession”. 

Most importantly, for the defense the alternative to creating such a prejudicial image is to cease having oral conversations with Mr. Robinson in the courtroom, which is both impractical and detrimental to his Sixth Amendment right to the effective assistance of counsel. 

The court could consider eliminating the problem by modifying the decorum order to prohibit zooming in on Mr. Robinson. As indicated above, this was attempted and then abandoned in the _Kohberger_ case because the media continued to zoom in on the defendant even after being ordered not to do so. _See_ Ex. 5. The reason that this solution did not work in _Kohberger_ and will not work here lies in the competitive “ethics” practiced by media video and photo journalists. In a study of those “ethics”, it was reported based on interviews with video and photo journalists engaged in covering criminal cases that there was a “make or break” pressure 

168 

on such journalists to bring back to their editors photographs or videos that could be spun as incriminating, such a photographs of “perp walks”. _See supra_ Bock, at 209. 

Bock notes that a guiding ethical principle “widespread among journalism scholars” is 

that “news _constructs_ rather than records reality.” _Id_ . at 206-07 (emphasis in original). Reflecting the cynicism of such “ethics”, she writes that “[s]hooting video of a perp walk is a work routine for many photo- and television journalists in the U.S.”, and that “it is important to note that the term ‘perpetrator’ automatically implies that this is the person who committed the crime; it’s not a ‘defendant walk’” or ‘alleged perpetrator walk.’ The very name implies that a subject is guilty.” _Id_ . at 207. The study queried photo and video journalists about what would happen if 

they came to their editors without the incriminating shot, and a couple of the photographers on a court stakeout had this to say: 

Q: What would happen if he went back without the shot? 

His immediate response: “Aww, Christ Almighty . . . condemned to death.” Later, “N” continues about missing the shot: “They’re very condemning at our place. . . . If everybody else got it and you don’t and you were here . . . .” He doesn’t finish the thought with words, more of a head shake and sigh. 

Photographer #2: “Eh, what can they do? They can be a little mad. . . . It depends on how important it is.” 

_Id_ . at 209-10. 

Bock also reports that prior journalism studies on the topic had found that “[t]he pressure 

seems as much a matter of professional pride as externally imposed by management”, and that photo and video journalists are “so determined to do their job that they would meet the demands of a shoot even it meant contradicting their personal ethical beliefs.” _Id_ . at 210. 

These journalism studies help to explain why the prohibition on zooming in on the defendant did not work in _Kohberger_ and why the same solution would not work here. They also 

169 

help to explain why the overwhelming thrust of the electronic media coverage in this case has been a prosecutorial-like attempt to incriminate Mr. Robinson through visual images of his restraints, clothing, gestures, and demeanor, aided and abetted by the commentary of biased lawyers, and other pundits and pseudo-experts. 

In sum, even this factor weighs in favor of granting the present motion because the size of the courtroom, coupled with the electronic media’s track record of violating decorum orders in this and other cases, based on a competitive “ethics” code, raises logistical and other problems that are simply unsolvable. 

### **(vii) the public interest in and newsworthiness of the proceeding;** 

The public interest in the proceeding in this case is fully protected by allowing the public and all media personal to attend and report on court proceedings in any case. _See Estes_ , 381 U.S. at 541 (“It is true that the public has a right to be informed as to what occurs in its courts, but reporters of all media, including television, are always present if they wish to be and are plainly free to report whatever occurs in open court through their respective media.”) Discontinuing electronic media live streaming would not prevent either the electronic media or the print media from attending and reporting on the court proceedings in this case. 

According to the courts, a criminal case is newsworthy if it is either “notorious” or “sensational.” _Id_ . at 545 (“[W]e must remember that realistically it is only the notorious trial which will be broadcast, because of the necessity for paid sponsorship.”); _Sinclair_ 434 P.2d at 307 (“The public is intrigued by the sensational, and thus by crime and the facts surrounding it. Consequently, it is but natural and perhaps necessary that the information media cater to that appetite.”). The fallacy of the argument that sensational or notorious cases are newsworthy and 

170 

therefore should be televised was pointed out by Chief Justice Warren in his concurring opinion 

in _Estes_ : 

For the most part, however, the most important factor that would draw television to the courtroom would be the nature of the case. The alleged perpetrator of the sensational murder, the fallen idol, or some other person who, like petitioner, has attracted the public interest would find his trial turned into a vehicle for television. Yet, these are the very persons who encounter the greatest difficulty in securing an impartial trial, even without the presence of television. This Court would no longer be able to point to the dignity and calmness of the courtroom as a protection from outside influences. For the television camera penetrates this protection and brings into the courtroom tangible evidence of the widespread interest in a case—an interest which has often been fanned by exhaustive reports in the newspapers, television and radio for weeks before trial. The present case presents a clear example of this danger. 

381 U.S. at 576-77 (Warren, C.J., concurring). 

As demonstrated above, five members of the Court determined in _Estes_ and 

_Hollingsworth_ that televising a notorious trial such as the prevent one violates the due process clause of the Fourteenth Amendment. It would turn the logic of these cases on their head and would violate the Supremacy Clause to hold in this case that because the case is either notorious 

or sensational it is thus newsworthy and should therefore be televised under a state rule. 

### **(viii) potentially beneficial effects of allowing public observation of the proceeding through electronic media coverage;** 

This factor is specifically addressed by five members of the Court in _Estes_ . Justice 

Clark’s lead opinion reminds us that “[c]ourt proceedings are held for the solemn purpose of endeavoring to ascertain the truth which is the sine qua non of a fair trial.” _Estes_ , 381 U.S. at 540. “As has been said, the chief function of our judicial machinery is to ascertain the truth. The use of television, however, cannot be said to contribute materially to this objective. Rather its use amounts to the injection of an irrelevant factor into court proceedings.” _Id_ . at 544. 

“Embarrassment may impede the search for the truth, as may a natural tendency toward 

171 

overdramatization…. Indeed, the mere fact that the trial is to be televised might render witnesses reluctant to appear and thereby impede the trial as well as the discovery of the truth.” _Id_ . at 547. “The judge decided that the trial proceedings would be telecast. He announced no restrictions at the time. This emphasized the notorious nature of the coming trial, increased the intensity of the publicity on the petitioner and together with the subsequent televising of the trial beginning 30 days later inherently prevented a sober search for the truth.” _Id_ . at 550-51. 

Chief Justice Warren, joined by Justices Douglas and Goldberg, agreed that “the purpose 

of trial [is to serve] as a vehicle for discovering the truth.” They further agreed that 

The present record provides ample support for scholars who have claimed that awareness that a trial is being televised to a vast, but unseen audience, is bound to increase nervousness and tension, cause an increased concern about appearances, and bring to the surface latent opportunism that the traditional dignity of the courtroom would discourage. Whether they do so consciously or subconsciously, all trial participants act differently in the presence of television cameras. And, even if all participants make a conscientious and studied effort to be unaffected by the presence of television, this effort in itself prevents them from giving their full attention to their proper functions at trial. Thus, the evil of televised trials, as demonstrated by this case, lies not in the noise and appearance of the cameras, but in the trial participants’ awareness that they are being televised. To the extent that television has such an inevitable impact it undercuts the reliability of the trial process. 

_Id_ . at 569-70 (footnote omitted). 

They also agreed with Justice Clark’s basic point that far from serving some educational 

or other legitimate purpose, the use of television in a courtroom “amounts to the injection of an 

irrelevant factor into court proceedings.” Chief Justice Warren wrote: 

It is argued that television not only entertains but also educates the public. But the function of a trial is not to provide an educational experience; and there is a serious danger that any attempt to use a trial as an educational tool will both divert it from its proper purpose and lead to suspicions concerning the integrity of the trial process. The Soviet Union’s trial of Francis Gary Powers provides an example in point. The integrity of the trial was suspect because it was concerned not only with determining the guilt of the individual on trial but also with providing an object lesson to the public. This divided effort undercut 

172 

confidence in the guilt-determining aspect of the procedure and by so doing rendered the educational aspect self-defeating. 

_Id_ . at 575. 

In Justice Harlan’s controlling concurring opinion in _Este_ s, he was even more emphatic 

on this point: 

The arguments advanced against the constitutional banning of televised trials seem to me peculiarly unpersuasive. It is said that the pictorial broadcasting of trials will serve to educate the public as to the nature of the judicial process. Whatever force such arguments might have in run-of-the-mill cases, they carry little weight in cases of the sort before us, where the public’s interest in viewing the trial is likely to be engendered more by curiosity about the personality of the well-known figure who is the defendant (as here), or about famous witnesses or lawyers who will appear on the television screen, or about the details of the particular crime involved, than by innate curiosity to learn about the workings of the judicial process itself. Indeed it would be naive not to suppose that it would be largely such factors that would qualify a trial for commercial television ‘billing,’ and it is precisely that kind of case where the risks of permitting television coverage of the proceedings are at their greatest. 

It is also asserted that televised trials will cause witnesses to be more truthful, and jurors, judges, and lawyers more diligent. To say the least this argument is sophistic, for it is impossible to believe that the reliability of a trial as a method of finding facts and determining guilt or innocence increases in relation to the size of the crowd which is watching it. Attendance by interested spectators in the courtroom will fully satisfy the safeguards of ‘public trial.’ Once openness is thus assured, the addition of masses of spectators would, I venture to say, detract rather than add to the reliability of the process. See _Cox v. State of Louisiana_ , 379 U.S. 559, 562 [(1965)]. A trial in Yankee Stadium, even if the crowd sat in stony silence, would be a substantially different affair from a trial in a traditional courtroom under traditional conditions, and the difference would not, I think, be that the witnesses, lawyers, judges, and jurors in the stadium would be more truthful, diligent, and capable of reliably finding facts and determining guilt or innocence. There will be no disagreement, I am sure, among those competent to judge that precisely the opposite would likely be the case. 

381 U.S. at 594-95 (Harlan, J., concurring). 

Five members of the Supreme Court could not have been clearer that the function of a 

trial is to ascertain the truth, “not to provide an educational experience”, and that at best 

173 

televising the proceedings introduces an “irrelevant factor” into court proceedings and at worst it impedes the search for truth, especially in a notorious case. Given the clarity and sweeping language used by the Court it is disappointing, yet perhaps understandable that a “committee …made up of judges, courtroom administrators _and media attorneys_ ”<sup>42</sup> would write a Final Report that contained a solitary and greatly watered-down description of the significance of 

_Estes_ : 

The United States Supreme Court weighed in on the debate in 1965 and again in 1981, ultimately recognizing the right of states to allow such coverage. In _Estes v.Texas_ , 381 U.S. 532 (1965), the Supreme Court overturned the conviction of Billy Sol Estes, holding that coverage of the trial, which included some use of cameras, violated Estes’ due process rights. Four justices of the five member majority found that televising trials, at least under then-existing technology, was inherently unconstitutional. The fifth justice took a narrower view based on the specific circumstances of the _Estes_ case and suggested that technological advancements might one day lead to a different result. 

Final Report, at 9-10. 

What about the significance of the Court’s agreement on the principles quoted above? 

The Report never says. What about the significance of _Hollingsworth_ ? The Report never mentions it. 

Instead, the Report declares as an _ipse dixit_ that “[p]ermitting electronic media coverage will allow the public to actually see and hear what transpires in the courtroom, and to become better educated and informed about the work of the courts.” Final Report, at 11. However, even putting aside the fact that this statement is directly contrary to _Estes_ , the premise of this assertion is that the public would actually view lengthy live stream coverage to get “better educated about the work of the courts.” Whatever the situation in the pre-social media days of 2012, the reality today is that people in every state, including Utah, get their news from social media and other 

> 42 _See supra_ , n. 35. 

174 

online media outlets, not by watching gavel-to-gavel coverage on television. _See, e.g._ , Art Raymond, _Here’s where, and how, Utahns are getting their news_ , DESERET NEWS (Dec. 13, 2022, at 7:44 PM MT), https://www.deseret.com/utah/2022/12/13/23496469/how-do-utahns-get- 

<u>news-digital-devices-trusted-sources (statewide pool demonstrated that “[w]hen it comes to how</u> Utahns are consuming their news, a vast majority [91 %] say digital devices are their No. 1 go-to and more than half report they rarely or never get news from television, radio or print publications.”); _2024 Policy Issues Survey February 2024 Likely Voter Poll_ , SUTHERLAND INSTITUTE (2024) https://utahnewsdispatch.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/05/Election-Trust- <u>Forum-VBM-survey-presentation.pdf (statewide poll of Utah voters indicating that only 19% of</u> those polled listed television as their most preferred media type for receiving news). What these numbers mean is that most Utahns have been and will continue to be receiving their diet of “news” about this case not from television live streaming of the proceedings, but by a stew of online _interpretations_ and _repackaging_ of unrepresentative samples of that live streaming, mixed in with a large dose of unverified and often unreliable hearsay, served up for consumption by what the State has accurately described in its pleadings as “pundits and other attention seekers with varying levels of mental stability”(Dkt. 109, at 9 (Classified Private)) and by what the Governor has accurately described in his Meet the Press appearance as internet and social media “conflict entrepreneurs.” 

That reality helps to explain the results of the study cited above that found that 100% of a sample of social media postings in this case involved conspiracy theories or political agendas. It is those false theories, not the workings of our courts, that the public is being “educated” about. 

That reality also helps to explain a recent poll conducted by KUTV. _See_ Wendy Hollaran, _Gov. Cox questioned on accusations of prejudicing accused assassin Tyler Robinson’s case_ , 

175 

KUTV (Nov. 25, 2025 at 8:27 PM), https://kutv.com/news/2news-investigates/do-you-think- 

<u>tyler-robinson-can-receive-an-impartial-trial-in-utah.</u> 

The poll results were as follows:

Where could 42% of those polled have come up with the idea that it would be “hard to 

avoid bias after public comments”, and another 11% come up with the idea that Mr. Robinson could not receive a fair trial in Utah County? It could not have been through watching the few live streamed proceedings televised to date, where this Court has stressed from the outset that it is committed to assuring Mr. Robinson a fair trial. _See_ Dkt. 239, at 8-9. The only explanation for such numbers is that a substantial number of the public is getting “educated” (i.e., biased) from other sources. 

176 

It is also significant that the 2012 Final Report conveniently ignored an important part of 

the 1994 Federal Report, although the 2012 Final Report claimed was supportive of its conclusions. The 1994 Federal Report contained a content analysis study of broadcasted stories in the pilot program that revealed that the stories did _“a poor job of providing viewers with detail about the legal process.”_ 2012 Final Report, Tab 3 at 119 (emphasis added). 

Chief Judge Becker, speaking on behalf of the Federal Judicial Conference, described this aspect of the study in his statement and testimony before a Senate Hearing on allowing cameras in the courtroom on September 6, 2000: 

V. The Putative Educational Benefit of Cameras in the Courtroom 

The proponents of cameras in the courtroom rely, of course, on the putative benefits of public education and understanding of court processes. The Judicial Conference supports that goal but does not agree that cameras in courtrooms will significantly further it. The FJC study analyzed the results achieved during the pilot project. The main approach to the issue lay in a content analysis of evening news broadcast using footage obtained during the pilot program. The content analysis is disquieting. The ninety stories analyzed presented a total of one hour and twenty-five minutes of courtroom footage, with an average of fifty-six seconds of courtroom footage per story. There is not too much educational content in 56 seconds. Moreover, most of the courtroom footage was voiced over by a reporter’s narration. On average, reporters narrated 63% of all courtroom footage. Thus, the witnesses, parties, and attorneys spoke on camera for just over one-third of the total air time. In at least one-half of the cases photographed, information on the nature of the case was provided by reporters or anchors without relying on the participants. 

The FJC report also sought to determine specifically the extent to which the stories provided basic educational information about the legal system, examining whether five pieces of information were conveyed to the viewer: (1) identification of the case as a civil matter; (2) identification of the type or proceeding, such as a hearing or trial; (3) statements about whether a jury was present; (4) descriptions of the proceedings on a given day; and (5) discussion of the next step in the legal process. The report concluded as follows: 

The vast majority of stories (95% of non-first day stories) did not identify the proceeding covered as a civil matter. In addition, 77% of the stories failed to identify the type of proceeding involved. Almost three-quarters (74%) of all 

177 

stories did not provide information about whether a jury was present, including half of the stories that identified the covered proceedings as a trial. 

Most stories (74%) did explain what transpired in court on a particular day, such as who testified or what evidence was presented. In multiple-day cases, 90% of the stories explained the daily proceedings, compared to 63% in singleday stories. Seventy-six percent of the daily proceedings in a story were explained by a combination of reporter narration and participant discussion. Only 29% of stories mentioned the next step in the litigation process in the case. 

Thus, the stories did not provide a high level of detail about the legal process in the cases covered. In addition, the analysis revealed that increasing the proportion of courtroom footage used in a story did not significantly increase the information given about the legal process. 

In view of the foregoing, we suggest that the benefits of televised coverage of courtroom proceedings are overrated (and are certainly far outweighed by the detriments described above). Television news coverage oftentimes appears simply to use the courtroom for a backdrop or a visual image for the news story which, like many of such stories on television, are delivered in short sound bites and not in depth. 

The FJC study also reported that Court TV covered 28 cases under the program and that C-SPAN covered 7 cases. However, it does not appear from records available to us that these proceedings were broadcast either in their entirety or continuously. The paucity of cases selected by C-SPAN—seven in two years—suggests that the tediousness, technicality, and sheer length of trials are obstacles to comprehensive media transmission, except in the sensational kinds of cases where the harms described previously are the greatest. 

_Perry_ , 2011 WL 2419868, at *82-83.<sup>43</sup> 

The findings of the FJC study severely undermine the “educational benefit” justification 

normally given for allowing such technology in courts. Additionally, the study found that 

43 The hearing transcript at which Chief Judge Becker and many others testified on September 6, 2000 was readily available in 2012 both through the _Perry_ opinion cited above and on the internet _. See Allowing Cameras And Electronic Media In The Courtroom: Hearing on S.721 Before the S. Comm. On the Judiciary_ , 106th Cong.1029 (2000), 

<u>https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CHRG-106shrg73484/html/CHRG-106shrg73484.htm. It</u> is curious that neither this 2000 hearing transcript nor the FJC study is mentioned anywhere in the 2012 Final Report, yet the report attaches as an exhibit a 1996 article by a media lawyer. 

178 

“plaintiffs and their attorneys received more air time than defendants and their attorneys.” 2012 Final Report, Tab 3, at 119. The lack of balance in covering the parties of litigation necessarily presented a biased, incomplete account of the trial. 

It is therefore not true that “[p]ermitting electronic media coverage will allow the public to actually see and hear what transpires in the courtroom, and to become better educated and informed about the work of the courts.” _Id_ . at 11. That result has no chance of happening unless the Court closes the spigot being used by pundits and the internet and social media “conflict entrepreneurs” to generate their false content. _Cf_ . _Sheppard_ , 384 U.S. at 361-62 (explaining that as a means of addressing and averting harm to the criminal justice process inflicted by prejudicial pretrial publicity, the trial court should have “proscribed extrajudicial statements by any lawyer, party, witness, or court official which divulged prejudicial matters” and that had the trial court done so, “the news media would have soon learned to be content with the task of reporting the case as it unfolded in the courtroom—not pieced together from extrajudicial statements.”). 

### **(ix) any other factor affecting the fair administration of justice** . 

There are a number of considerations that the Court should consider under the fair 

administration of justice factor that all weigh in favor of granting the instant motion. 

First, as referenced above, it appears that the State agrees with the defense that the Court should not permit further electronic media live streaming or photographic coverage. The positive or negative effects of cameras depend on the facts and circumstances of each case. The parties, who are familiar with the witnesses who will testify, the evidence that will be offered, and other facts that might indicate the potential for prejudice, are in the best position to determine the appropriateness of cameras. It is also true, as the judge stated in the _Kohberger_ case, that 

179 

“[a]greement between the State and Defense on any issue in a capital case is rare, [which should] further confirm[ ] to the Court the legitimacy and level of concern counsel have raised.” Ex. 5 at 6 (quotation omitted). 

Second, as was concluded in _United States v. Trump_ , 

Messages designed to generate alarm and dread, and to trigger extraordinary safety precautions, will necessarily hinder the trial process and slow the administration of justice. For example, trial personnel and participants will be distracted or delayed by objectively reasonable concerns about their safety and that of their family members, as well as by having to devote time and resources to adopting safety measures or working with investigators. 

88 F.4th at 1014. 

This no mere general concern in this case where the inflammatory rhetoric of the 

President and his supporters were specifically designed to and have had the effect of “generat[ing] alarm and dread, and [of] trigger[ing] extraordinary safety precautions.” _See also_ Ex. 5, at 7 (“Further, the duty on the bailiffs within the courtroom to ensure a safe and secure environment is being eclipsed by the burden to monitor media and the public who may be audio recording, videoing, or photographing the proceedings.”). 

A related concern is that the Court and both the defense and prosecution teams have already had to devote a significant amount of their time and resources to litigating issues related to the electronic media’s insistence on not only attendings court proceedings, but on live streaming them. Given the number of lawyers who have appeared in this case on behalf of the media, the constant need to be on the lookout for decorum order violations, and the reality that there will be future developments that will no doubt necessitate revisiting any order allowing electronic media coverage, there is no end in sight as to the need to pursue what is essentially collateral litigation far removed from what should be the focus of the Court and both teams in their representation of their clients in this death penalty case. _See Trump_ , 88 F.4th at 1014 

180 

(“requiring a court to conduct a mini-trial on that inquiry while readying a high-profile case for 

trial would itself divert and delay the criminal justice process.”). 

### **CONCLUSION** 

Based upon the foregoing, this Court should enter an order pursuant to Utah Rule of 

Judicial Administration Rule 4-401.01 and the constitutional provisions cited above that 

prohibits or restricts electronic media coverage in this cases because: (1) such action is necessary 

to preserve and protect the constitutional provisions set forth above; (2) under Rule 4-401.01, further electronic media coverage should be prohibited or restricted because (a) the electronic media coverage in this case has not been and will not in the future be for the predominant purpose of journalism or dissemination of news to the public (Rule 4-401.01(2)(A)); (b) the reasons for doing so are sufficiently compelling to outweigh any presumption that electronic media coverage by a news reporter shall be permitted (Rule 4-401.01(2)(A); (c) a judge may terminate or suspend electronic media coverage at any time without prior notice if the judge finds that continued electronic media coverage is no longer appropriate based upon consideration of one or more of the factors in Paragraph (2)(B) (Rule 4-401.01(3)(B) ; and (d) the electronic 

media has violated this Court’s decorum order so prohibition or restrict of electronic media 

coverage is warranted under Rule 4-401.01(5).

### **DECLARATION OF CHRISTINE L. RUVA, PH.D.** 

I, Christine L. Ruva, declare as follows: 

1) I am a Professor of Psychology and Sarasota-Manatee campus chair of 

psychology at the University of South Florida. I earned my Ph.D. in Psychology from the University of South Florida in the area of Cognitive and Neural Sciences, where I focused on the application of cognitive psychology to the legal system. For over 25 years I have explored the effects of pre-trial publicity on jurors’ perceptions, emotions, memories, interpretation of trial evidence, deliberation behavior, and verdicts. My research also explores how individual differences of jurors and defendants influence jurors’ perceptions, deliberation behaviors, and verdicts. My research also explores the effectiveness of remedies available to the courts for reducing juror bias (e.g., deliberation, expert testimony, jury instructions, voir dire, and implicit bias remedies). I utilize both quantitative and qualitative (i.e., content analysis of jury deliberations) methods. My research has been published in a variety of high-impact psychology and criminology journals including the following: _Criminal Justice and Behavior; Journal of Experimental Psychology-Applied; Law and Human Behavior; Psychology, Crime and Law; and Psychology, Public Policy, and Law_ . My full Curriculum Vitae is attached as Appendix 1. 

2) I have been asked by counsel for Mr. Robinson to review, evaluate, and update the empirical research discussed in the Final Report by the Utah Judicial Council Study Committee on Technology (April 10, 2012), 

<u>https://legacy.utcourts.gov/committees/Tech_in_Courtrooms/2012.03.23%20Final%20Report.pdf</u> which recommended adoption of what is now UT R JUD ADMIN Rule 4-401.01 (Electronic Media Coverage of Court Proceedings). I have also been asked to summarize any empirical research which bears on the various factors cited in that Rule, which requires that when 

Page 2 of 36 

determining whether the presumption of electronic media coverage has been overcome and whether such coverage should be prohibited or restricted beyond the limitations in this rule, a judge shall consider some or all of the following factors: 

- (i) whether there is a reasonable likelihood that electronic media coverage will prejudice the right of the parties to a fair proceeding; 

- (ii) whether there is a reasonable likelihood that electronic media coverage will jeopardize the safety or well-being of any individual; 

(iii) whether there is a reasonable likelihood that electronic media coverage will jeopardize the interests or well-being of a minor; 

- (iv) whether there is a reasonable likelihood that electronic media coverage will constitute an unwarranted invasion of personal privacy of any person; 

- (v) whether electronic media coverage will create adverse effects greater than those caused by media coverage without recording or transmitting images or sound; 

- (vi) the adequacy of the court's physical facilities for electronic media coverage; 

(vii) the public interest in and newsworthiness of the proceeding; 

(viii) potentially beneficial effects of allowing public observation of the proceeding 

through electronic media coverage; and 

(ix) any other factor affecting the fair administration of justice. 

(Rule 4-401.01(2)(B).) 

### **<u>I. SUMMARY OF RESEARCH</u>** 

3) Decades of research by cognitive psychologists into the causes and impacts of 

bias, as well as individuals’ ability to self-identify bias, establish several empirical findings that 

Page 3 of 36 

bear on the factors listed in Rule 4-401.01(2)(B) in cases where pervasive and inflammatory pretrial publicity (PTP) has occurred. 

4) _First_ , pervasive and inflammatory pre-trial publicity, especially live streaming of all proceedings, can lead to deep-seated bias. This bias is not merely an affinity for the victim or aversion to the defendant. It is cognitive, and as such, it fundamentally affects how jurors will process evidence during the trial and deliberate in the jury room. 

5) _Second_ , these effects are heightened in cases involving notorious crimes such as alleged assassinations, terrorism, or violence against children. And, in today’s world of pervasive social media and live streaming of all proceedings, where information is ubiquitous, is pinpointtailored to the fears and beliefs of the reader and viewer, and may well be inaccurate, the impact of pre-trial publicity is especially acute. 

6) _Third_ , cognitive bias can be unconscious, making it difficult even for the most self-aware and well-intentioned potential juror to identify and self-report in response to general questions about bias. In fact, individuals with unconscious bias may be unduly confident in their ability to disregard pre-trial publicity and judge impartially. Once seated, it can be difficult or impossible for jurors to set this bias aside. 

7) In short, scientific research into bias confirms what logic and good sense suggest: merely questioning potential jurors about their exposure to and unconscious bias created by pervasive and inflammatory pre-trial publicity will not be sufficient to eliminate such bias. While extended individual questioning of jurors to elicit bias in high publicity cases is certainly 

Page 4 of 36 

preferable to group questioning, the fact remains that unconscious bias caused by such publicity remains largely intractable.<sup>1</sup> 

### **<u>II. LIVE STREAMING OF ALL PROCEEDINGS IN A NOTORIOUS CRIMINAL CASE CAN CAUSE BIAS</u>** 

### **A. Live Streaming of Notorious Crimes Can Be Pervasive, Slanted, Sensational, and Emotional—All of Which Can Lead to Bias.** 

- 8) Media coverage of notorious crimes is different. The news media, and today, 

social media, cover crimes every day, and a steady drumbeat of even serious crimes makes most criminal coverage routine. But some crimes receive sensational coverage because they involve public figures, are especially heinous, extraordinarily violent, or threaten the community at large. And sensational coverage, by its sheer volume and by its content, has impacts that the everyday crime beat does not, especially when it is live streamed and then filtered through social media and other internet outlets. 

9) The volume of coverage in notorious cases can be overwhelming.<sup>2</sup> And studies show that the quantity of pre-trial publicity to which jurors are exposed affects their level of bias. Across studies extending back several decades, researchers have demonstrated that individuals exhibit a greater propensity to pre-judge defendants when they are exposed to more publicity— 

both as a general conclusion (i.e., showing a general propensity to prejudgment based on an

> 2 Dorothy J. Imrich et al., _Measuring the Extent of Prejudicial Pretrial Publicity in Major American Newspapers: A Content Analysis_ , 45 J. increase in the amount of exposure<sup>3</sup> ), and in response to specific thresholds (i.e., showing that prospective jurors exposed to at least three newspaper articles about a crime were more likely to believe there was ample evidence against a defendant<sup>4</sup> ). This effect has been demonstrated in 

both naturally occurring and controlled settings, confirming the validity of the proposition.<sup>5</sup>

influences juror bias, concluding that its effects are cumulative.<sup>6</sup> DeLuca (1979) found that mock jurors exposed to three pieces of anti-defendant information were more likely to convict than

O’Connor, and Wallace (2014) reported that jurors who read a high amount of anti-defendant 

PTP (ten news stories) were significantly more likely to render guilty verdicts than those exposed

to a lower amount (five stories). Conversely, greater exposure to pro-defendant PTP reduced the likelihood of guilty verdicts compared to lower exposure.<sup>8</sup> 

11) Survey research corroborates these findings: as potential jurors’ reported 

knowledge of a case increases, so does bias against the defendant (Costantini & King, 1980/1981<sup>9</sup> ; Moran & Cutler, 1991<sup>10</sup> ). Notably, Moran and Cutler found that increased case knowledge did not reduce jurors’ self-reported confidence in impartiality, suggesting bias operates outside conscious awareness. Shaffer (1986) further demonstrated that both the number of PTP articles and respondents’ estimated exposure predicted attributions of guilt in murder cases.<sup>11</sup> 

12) Collectively, these studies indicate that greater PTP exposure amplifies juror bias, increasing the likelihood of prejudicial verdicts. This supports the Supreme Court’s recognition of pre-trial publicity’s biasing influence and the American Bar Association’s ethical rules addressing pervasive publicity. 

13) Media coverage of notorious crimes is likely to be sensational and slanted against the defendant. A 2018 content analysis of over 1,800 pre-trial newspaper articles regarding 20 California capital cases from 1979 to 2005 found that certain highly prejudicial elements recur repeatedly in the coverage. Seventy-five percent of the articles contained some form of negative

17) Similarly, a 2003 study analyzed 50 mock jurors’ recall of factual versus 

“affective” pre-trial publicity. The 50 mock jurors were naturally exposed to “extensive and prolonged” pre-trial publicity about a fraud case and then interviewed to determine what they remembered about the case from that publicity. Their recall was coded as either factual or affective/ evaluative, with factual recall referring to neutral data points about the crime (e.g., 

“The defendants are accused of fraud.”) and affective recall meaning memories reported with an emotion or opinion (e.g., “They were all in on it . . . ruthless con-artists.”). The mock jurors then watched a highly realistic depiction of the actual trial and were interviewed throughout about their reasoning processes, whether they thought the defendants were guilty, and how confident they were in their verdict. The study found that those mock jurors who had affective recall of publicity were more likely to be pro-prosecution and were more confident in finding the defendant guilty after watching a mock trial.<sup>22</sup> Mock jurors who demonstrated that they recalled 

purely factual information from pre-trial publicity, as opposed to more affective or emotional information, did not show the same tendencies. Therefore, the study found that “an affective

individuals experiencing anger do not readily accept new information or process all information systematically.<sup>26</sup>

twelve days between exposure to emotional publicity and trial did not reduce the biasing effects of that publicity, while the wait did reduce the effect of factual publicity.
