Participants were randomly assigned to receive pre-trial publicity through newspaper articles, televised excerpts of testimony, both media types, or control condition. The study found that exposure to television coverage produced significantly stronger emotional reactions, greater belief in the credibility of victims’ testimony, higher perceptions of the defendant’s guilt, and harsher sentencing recommendations than exposure to print media alone. Combined exposure to both television and print media had the most pronounced biasing effect. Importantly, despite these differences, participants across all conditions reported similar confidence in their ability to remain impartial, indicating that jurors may be unaware of their own bias. These findings underscore that vivid, visual television coverage exerts a more powerful prejudicial influence than print media, and that cumulative exposure amplifies this effect. The authors note that such bias likely stems from emotional arousal rather than factual differences, raising concerns about the fairness of trials in cases involving extensive televised coverage (Ogloff & Vidmar, 1994). 

21) Edwards and Mottarella (2014) examined the prejudicial impact of visual versus auditory evidence on mock-juror emotion and verdict decisions in a murder case.<sup>31</sup> Visual evidence conditions included graphic images, neutral images, or no images; auditory evidence consisted of a 911 call recording; and jury instructions were either standard or revised to warn against emotional bias. Results indicated that graphic visual evidence significantly increased conviction rates (31.3%) compared to neutral (26.3%) or no images (19.8%), whereas auditory evidence had no significant effect on verdicts or emotional responses. Emotional state changes were modest and did not mediate the relationship between evidence type and verdict, suggesting visual evidence influences decisions independently of emotional arousal. Revised jury 

> 31 Edwards, E. R., & Mottarella, K. E. (2014). Preserving the right to a fair trial: An examination of prejudicial value of visual and auditory evidence. _North American Journal of Psychology, 16_ (2), 397–414. 

Page 14 of 36 

instructions aimed at reducing emotional bias were ineffective and, in fact, increased conviction rates. According to Edwards and Mottarella (2014), and consistent with Ogloff and Vidmar (1994), this research suggests that when case-related photos circulate before trial (e.g., in news sites or social feeds), the same mechanism can prime community members toward guilt judgments, with this bias persisting independent of their conscious emotional reports. Applied to trial recordings: when courts permit video that includes still frames or inserts of graphic visuals (crime-scene images displayed during recorded proceedings or streamed hearings), those visuals can create distinctive, durable memory traces that affect perceived credibility and culpability, thereby biasing adjudicative assessments even if jurors believe they can remain impartial (Edwards & Mottarella, 2014). 

22) Fishfader and colleagues (1996) examined whether the mode of evidence presentation (print transcripts, videotaped testimony, or videotaped testimony plus a video recreation) affects juror retention, emotional state, liability assessments, and damage awards in a wrongful death case.<sup>32</sup> Videotaped formats significantly increased emotional reactions versus print. Changes in mood were inversely correlated with liability assigned to the plaintiff (i.e., heightened emotion reduced perceived plaintiff responsibility), particularly in the video re-creation condition. Yet retention scores and damage awards did not significantly differ by format (Fishfader, Howells, Katz, & Teresi, 1996). 

23) Basic research in psychology has explored why visual stimuli are more 

memorable and impactful than auditory or text-based stimuli **.** A recent experiment by Ensor, Bancroft, and Hockley (2019) investigated whether the picture-superiority effect—the robust 

> 32 Fishfader, V. L., Howells, G. N., Katz, R. C., & Teresi, P. S. (1996). Evidential and extralegal factors in juror decisions: Presentation mode, retention, and level of emotionality. _Law and Human Behavior, 20_ (5), 565–572. 

Page 15 of 36 

finding that pictures are better remembered than words—extends to auditory stimuli and whether this effect is driven by dual coding, physical distinctiveness, or conceptual distinctiveness.<sup>33</sup> Across four experiments using recognition tasks, participants studied lists of sounds and words under various conditions, including directed-forgetting cues, cue-duration manipulations, and labelability variations. Results consistently showed no auditory analog of the picture-superiority effect in recognition: sounds were not better recognized than words, even when sounds were easy to label or when additional encoding time was provided. While free-recall studies have demonstrated an auditory picture-superiority effect, these findings suggest that recognition memory benefits for pictures arise from conceptual distinctiveness—the deeper processing required to identify diagnostic visual features—rather than dual coding or physical variability. The authors conclude that conceptual distinctiveness explains the picture-superiority effect in recognition, as auditory stimuli lack the feature-rich processing demands of pictures. These findings imply that visual evidence may exert a stronger biasing influence on jurors than auditory evidence because of its inherent conceptual distinctiveness (Ensor, Bancroft, & Hockley, 2019). Applied to trial recordings or live streaming: audio-only recordings of hearings or trials should, on average, exert less prejudicial influence on community listeners than video streams, because they lack the diagnostic visual features that create stronger, biasing memory traces (Ensor et al., 2019; Edwards & Mottarella, 2014). 

24) Collectively, these studies on modality effect of information presentation 

demonstrate that visual and multimedia evidence pose a substantially greater risk of prejudicing jurors than auditory evidence or print formats. Visual formats—especially graphic images, video 

33 Ensor, T. M., Bancroft, T. D., & Hockley, W. E. (2019). Listening to the picture-superiority effect: Evidence for the conceptual-distinctiveness account of picture superiority in recognition. _Experimental Psychology_ . https://doi.org/10.1027/1618-3169/a000437. 

Page 16 of 36 

re-creations, and televised coverage—amplify emotional responses, increase perceptions of guilt, and alter liability assessments, while auditory evidence exerts minimal biasing effect. Moreover, jurors often fail to recognize their susceptibility to these influences, and judicial instructions have proven largely ineffective in mitigating such biases. These findings strongly support the need for careful judicial scrutiny of highly visual or emotionally charged evidence and extensive pretrial publicity to preserve the defendant’s constitutional right to a fair trial. 

### **B. Publicity of Alleged Assassinations, Terrorist Attacks, or Other Mass Acts of Violence May Cause Trauma and Significant Anger, Disgust, Fear, and Related Bias.** 

25) Conventional, social media, and especially live stream publicity of or about 

traumatic events such as assassinations, terrorism, and other mass acts of violence can cause 

trauma and emotional responses such as anger, disgust, and fear that distort decision-making and lead to bias. Frequent viewers of stories relating to such acts risk the “abnormal consolidation of fear conditioning that is associated with development of acute and [post-traumatic stress] responses” and can contribute to trauma-related disorders.<sup>34</sup> Psychological effects in the 

aftermath of such attack are “virtually certain” even if individuals are not aware they are experiencing them.<sup>35</sup> 

26) This effect has been documented with respect to acts such as the Boston Marathon 

Bombing. A nationwide study found that media exposure and graphic images of the bombing affected the cognitive function of exposed individuals. One survey even showed that event- 

> 34E. Alison Holman et al., _Media’s Role in Broadcasting Acute Stress Following the Boston Marathon Bombings_ , 111 PROC. NAT’L ACAD. SCI. U.S. 93, 93 (2014), www.pnas.org/cgi/doi/10. 1073/pnas.1316265110. 

35 U.S. INSTITUTE OF MEDICINE COMMITTEE ON RESPONDING TO THE PSYCHOLOGICAL CONSEQUENCES OF TERRORISM, PREPARING FOR THE PSYCHOLOGICAL CONSEQUENCES OF TERRORISM 34, 62 (Adrienne Stith Butler et al. eds., 2003), https://www.ncbi.nlm. nih.gov/books/NB K221638/. 

Page 17 of 36 

related media exposure was associated with a higher likelihood of acute stress than direct exposure to the bombing.<sup>36</sup> 

27) Furthermore, exposure to traumatic evidence through electronic media can trigger unexpected, negative reactions from prospective jurors suffering from trauma-related disorders, affecting their decision-making. Triggers vary depending on the type of trauma experienced and may be directly or indirectly related to the origin event itself. For example, people could be triggered by sounds, smells, tastes, colors, places and specific objects, as well as evidence related to the traumatic event. In this mental state, individuals may be unable to precisely articulate what they are thinking and feeling, and, of particular significance here, may even be unaware that the traumatic event is the source of their anxiety.<sup>37</sup> Of significance to prospective jurors, traumatized individuals may have a cognitive bias toward emotionally charged memories; and exposure to evidence may cause flashbacks, trigger overwhelming feelings, and cause threat arousal—all of which can impact cognition and information processing.<sup>38</sup> These studies bear directly on three of the factors the Court is called upon to address under Rule: whether there is a reasonable likelihood that electronic media coverage will jeopardize the safety or well-being of any individual; whether there is a reasonable likelihood that electronic media coverage will 

jeopardize the interests or well-being of a minor; and whether electronic media coverage will

### **<u>III. PRE-TRIAL PUBLICITY BIAS IS DIFFICULT TO RECOGNIZE AND SET ASIDE AND CAN DISTORT JURY DELIBERATIONS</u>** 

### **A. Biased Jurors Interpret Information Differently Than Unbiased Jurors.** 

28) The impact of bias on juror decision-making is immense and difficult to 

overcome. This is because jurors exposed to pre-trial publicity interpret new evidence at trial in a different way than jurors who have not been exposed. In fact, bias from pre-trial publicity may persist even when squarely contradicted by trial evidence.<sup>39</sup> 

29) Research into the “story model theory” and the “primacy effect” help explain why pre-trial publicity bias is “sticky” and difficult to overcome. The story model theory posits that the “central cognitive process in juror decision-making is story construction.” This means that jurors turn information gleaned at trial into a narrative, and “the story the juror constructs determines the juror’s decision.”<sup>40</sup> 

30) To test this theory, its originators showed study participants a mock trial and asked them to reach a verdict while discussing their rationale out loud. Each participant’s reasoning was mapped to determine whether the jurors processed the evidence presented chronologically, in the structure of the legal argument, in terms of character sketches, or in a story narrative. The results demonstrated that jurors analyze evidence in the structure of a story, 

> 39 Daftary-Kapur et al., _supra_ note 5, at 474. 

> 40 Nancy Pennington and Reid Hastie, _The Story Model for Juror Decision Making_ , _in_ INSIDE THE JUROR: THE PSYCHOLOGY OF JUROR DECISION MAKING 193, 193 (Reid Hastie ed., 1993), https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511752896.010. 

Page 19 of 36 

not in terms of legal arguments or any other alternative, and—significantly—that jurors fill in gaps in the story with information not presented at trial.<sup>41</sup> 

31) Research into the primacy effect demonstrates that it is difficult for individuals to overcome their first impressions once they form initial opinions, and they therefore interpret later information to be consistent with those first opinions.<sup>42</sup> Individuals remember early information better than later information, and thus, early information holds a bigger influence on decisionmaking and impressions.<sup>43</sup> When the primacy effect is combined with the story-model understanding of juror decision-making, the juror who has a media-formed narrative of events due to pre-trial publicity is at risk of prioritizing this narrative over subsequent, contradictory evidence presented at trial, even when the juror endeavors to consider only the evidence from trial. 

32) Media-exposed jurors also may discount information presented at trial due to 

“belief persistence,” which is the tendency to maintain a belief even after subsequent facts show it to be incorrect,<sup>44</sup> or “confirmation bias,” which is the tendency to form a belief early in the decision-making process and then evaluate new information in a manner favorable to that

PSYCHOL. 175, 186–87 (1970), https://doi.org/10.1037/h0029832; Lee Ross et al., _Perseverance in Self-Perception and Social Perception: Biased Attributional Processes in the Debriefing Paradigm_ , 32 J. PERSONALITY & SOC. PSYCHOL. 880, 889 (1975), https://doi.org/10.1037/0022-3514.32.5.880.

overcoming those biases.<sup>46</sup> 

### **B. Biased Jurors May Not Recognize Their Biases—Undermining Voir Dire and the Effectiveness of Limiting Instructions.** 

33) Prospective jurors may find it particularly difficult to recognize their own biases, 

or may overestimate their capacity to set biases aside, in cases involving traumatic events.<sup>47</sup> 

Moreover, decades of studies have shown that neither jury instructions nor deliberation reduce the impact of factual and emotional publicity.<sup>48</sup>

34) Biased jurors may not answer voir dire questions accurately because their bias is subconscious and they do not know or understand the impact that pre-trial publicity has had. In a quintessential study, researchers exposed approximately 150 mock jurors to either neutral or negative pre-trial publicity, asked them to complete typical voir dire questionnaires, excused those jurors who said they could not be impartial, and then asked the remaining jurors for their verdict.<sup>49</sup> Among those jurors—all of whom reported that they could be impartial—those who were exposed to negative pre-trial publicity returned guilty verdicts at more than twice the rate of those who had been exposed to neutral pre-trial publicity.<sup>50</sup> 

35) Even if jurors are given instructions not to use information learned outside of trial in making their decisions, they may be unable to comply, despite best efforts. Supporting this conclusion, death- 

qualified jurors who showed pre-trial publicity bias in their verdicts overwhelmingly selfreported an ability to be impartial.<sup>54</sup>

### **C. Biased Jurors Distort Jury Deliberations.** 

37) The adverse effects of pre-trial publicity do not stop with the biased juror. Studies show that pre-trial publicity bias can spread among the jury and even increase through a process called “group polarization,” which occurs when group decision-making leads to a more extreme result than the original views of the group’s members. Through this process, pre-trial publicity bias can spread to others not originally affected.<sup>55</sup> 

38) A 2022 study analyzing mock jury deliberations involving some mock jurors who had been exposed to slanted pre-trial publicity and others who had not found that (a) when mock jurors exposed to slanted pre-trial publicity introduced facts into the deliberation which came from pre-trial publicity but were not in the trial record, they were corrected less than 50 percent of the time; (b) mock juries that included some members exposed to pre-trial publicity slanted against the defendant and others exposed to no pre-trial publicity were more likely to find the defendant guilty than juries with no exposed members; and (c) juries comprising some members exposed to pre-trial publicity slanted against the victim and others who had been exposed to no pre-trial publicity were more likely to find the defendant not guilty than juries with no exposed 

Excluding those who self-identified bias or were unsure about their level of bias, the study found the mock jurors exposed to prejudicial pre-trial publicity significantly more likely to find the defendant guilty than those exposed to irrelevant articles. 

> 55 Ruva, C. L., Diaz Ortega, S.E., & O’Grady, K.A **.** (2022). What drives a Jury’s Deliberations? members. Together, these findings suggest that rather than correcting for the bias introduced by 

pre-trial publicity, jury deliberations in fact spread individual jurors’ biases to others.<sup>56</sup> 

### **IV. The Empirical Research Discussed in the 2012 Judicial Council Final Report Is Limited and Dated and Is Based on Unreliable Survey Research.** 

39) The Final Report by the Utah Judicial Council Study Committee on Technology 

(April 10, 2012) states in part as follows: 

The concern most often raised about electronic media coverage is that such coverage may harm the decorum of the proceedings and negatively impact trial participants. The extensive empirical research and broad-based experience of other states, however, suggest that these concerns are unfounded. For example, several states, including Arizona, California, Florida, Hawaii, Kansas, Louisiana, Maine, Massachusetts, Minnesota, New Jersey, New York, Ohio, Virginia, and Washington, have studied the impact of electronic media coverage on courtroom proceedings, focusing particularly on the effect that cameras have upon courtroom decorum and upon witnesses, jurors, attorneys, and judges. See Kelli L. Sager & Karen N. Fredericksen, _Televising the Judicial Branch: In Furtherance of the Public’s First Amendment Rights_ , 69 S. CAL. L. REV. 1519, 1543 (1996) (“Televising the Judicial Branch”). 

The results from the state studies were unanimous:  electronic media coverage of courtroom proceedings—whether civil or criminal—has no detrimental impact on the parties, jurors, counsel, or courtroom decorum. Televising the Judicial Branch, 69 S. CAL. L. REV. at 1544, 1547. For example, the state studies revealed that fears about witness distraction, nervousness, distortion, fear of harm, and reluctance to testify were unfounded. Id. at 1543-44. A three-year pilot program permitting electronic media coverage of civil proceedings in six federal district courts and two federal circuit courts reported similar results, as well a favorable view of such coverage by the judges who participated in the program. See Federal Judicial Center, ELECTRONIC MEDIA COVERAGE OF FEDERAL CIVIL PROCEEDINGS: AN EVALUATION OF THE PILOT PROGRAM IN SIX DISTRICT COURTS AND TWO COURTS OF APPEALS (1994); see also _Televising the Judicial Branch_ , 69 S. CAL. L. REV. at 1545; Alex Kozinski & Robert Johnson, _Of Cameras and Courtrooms_ , 20 FORDHAM INTELL. PROP. MEDIA & ENT. L.J. 1107, 1114 (2010) (summarizing state and federal studies on electronic media coverage of judicial proceedings and concluding that the empirical research demonstrates no detrimental impact on trial participants or on courtroom decorum). 

(Report at pp. 8-9). 

> 56 Ruva et al. (2022), _supra_ note 55. 

Page 25 of 36 

40) Several observations can be made about this aspect of the report. First, the Sager and Kozinski articles cited in the report are law review articles and not empirical research articles. They cite state surveys and one federal survey regarding civil court proceedings. The state surveys asked for the opinions of witnesses, jurors, attorneys, and judges after a trial is completed or after the witness testifies. Survey data cannot be used to infer causation, and this is not its only limitation – e.g., response bias, recall failure or misinformation effects (may not accurately recall past event and information after the event affects recall). The one state that used additional methodology was California which also used interviews and systematic observation. Although this does improve upon survey data alone it does not allow one to infer causation. Each trial, witness, set of jurors, judges, attorneys are different. There is no control over any variable and hence there is no way to conclude that cameras do or do not affect trial players’ behaviors, memory, or decision-making. 

41) Also, the three-year pilot federal study published in 1994 that found judges have a favorable opinion of electronic media coverage of court in civil cases tells us nothing about the possible prejudicial effect such coverage could have on courtroom proceedings, witness testimony and memory, and juror bias, behavior, and decision making. The study itself noted its inherent limitations, stating: 

Several potentially relevant issues were not examined in the evaluation and therefore cannot be addressed in this report. First, the evaluation design as approved by the Ad Hoc Committee on Cameras in the Courtroom did not include a measure of the _actual_ (as opposed to _perceived_ ) effects of electronic media on jurors, witnesses, counsel, and judges. The only way to measure objectively the actual effects of electronic media on jurors and witnesses would be to compare the behavior and perceptions of jurors and witnesses in two different groups of cases: those covered by electronic media and those not covered. The Federal Judicial Center suggested—and the Ad Hoc Committee on Cameras in the Courtroom concurred—that this approach was not feasible because, among other reasons, there would be too few cases in the pilot courts with high media interest to support such an evaluation. 

Page 26 of 36 

Second, we did not directly measure the attitudes of jurors, witnesses, and parties because most have had little courtroom experience and could not, we believed, make judgments (as judges and attorneys could) about the effects of electronic media on themselves. (A witness who has never been in a court room might be nervous for many reasons but might attribute that state—in appropriately—to the presence of cameras.) We did obtain some information on these issues through other methods, such as judge and attorney questionnaires. Also, we reviewed results from state court studies exploring these questions. 

Finally, because the pilot program limited coverage to civil proceedings, the impact of electronic media coverage on federal criminal proceedings was not addressable in this evaluation. Opinions on the issue of criminal coverage were obtained through questionnaires and interviews. 

Another consideration relevant to interpreting the findings in this report is that the pilot courts were chosen from among courts that had volunteered to participate, and most of the analyses in our study focused on judges who actually had experience with electronic media coverage. Thus, it could be expected that judges whose responses we report would on average be more favorable toward electronic media coverage than would a randomly-selected sample of judges throughout the country. 

2012 Study at 8. 

42) As this passage suggests, survey research is widely used, but it comes with 

several limitations and pitfalls that researchers need to consider: 

- a. Sampling Bias 

- If the sample isn’t representative of the population, results can be skewed. 

- Common issues include nonresponse bias and convenience sampling. 

- b. Response Bias 

- Participants may give socially desirable answers rather than truthful ones. 

- Wording of questions can lead to leading or loaded responses. 

- c. Low Response Rates 

- Declining participation rates can reduce reliability and increase bias. 

- d. Limited Depth 

- Surveys often capture surface-level attitudes or behaviors without exploring underlying reasons. 

- Complex topics may require qualitative methods for deeper insights. 

- e. Measurement Error 

- Poorly designed questions can lead to misinterpretation. 

- Ambiguous or double-barreled questions confuse respondents. 

- f. Recall Issues 

- Respondents may not accurately remember past behaviors or experiences. 

- g. Mode Effects 

Page 27 of 36 

- Online, phone, and face-to-face surveys can produce different results due to context and interviewer presence. 

- h. Difficulty in Establishing Causality 

- Surveys are typically correlational; they rarely prove cause-and-effect relationships. 

- i. Data Quality Concerns 

- Inattentive respondents or straight-lining can compromise validity. 

- j. Cultural and Language Barriers 

- Misinterpretation of questions across different cultural contexts can distort findings. 

43) In fact, five peer-reviewed journal articles have critiqued the survey studies relied 

upon in the 2012 Final Report, and three of these studies were published before the 2012 Report 

and are nowhere discussed in it. _See_ Borgida, E., DeBono, K. G., & Buckman, L. A., _Cameras in the courtroom: The effects of media coverage on witness testimony and juror perceptions_ , 14 _Law and Human Behavior_ 489–509 (1990), https://doi.org/10.1007/BF01044224; Slater, D., & Hans, V. P., Methodological issues in the evaluation of experiments with cameras in the courts, 30 _Communication Quarterly_ 346-380 (1982); Christo Lassiter, _TV or not TV—That is the_ 

_Question_ , 86 J. CRIM. L. & CRIMINOLOGY 928, 966–67 (1996) (explaining that surveys are flawed because respondents are inherently biased in favor of believing that they participated objectively in a trial, and because of the small sample size involved in most such studies); Cristina Carmody Tilley, _I Am A Camera: Scrutinizing The Assumption That Cameras In The Courtroom Furnish Public Value By Operating As A Proxy For The Public_ , 16 Journal of Constitutional Law 719 n. 122 (2013) (explaining that “[m]ost of the studies involved were ex post surveys of parties who had participated in televised trials asking their self-assessment of the camera’s effect on their behavior.”); Kaufman, A.R., Williams, L. and Thorley, D., _Performative Judging: Measuring the Effect of Video Recording on Judicial Behavior in Circuit Court Oral Arguments_ (2025)(same), https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=5283589 .<sup>57</sup> Both 

> 57 The Tillery article also addresses the reliance in Judge Kozinski’s article and in other sources on the broadcast-press-as-proxy conceit. She correctly points out that “[a]dvocates have been 

Page 28 of 36 

Slater and Hans (1982) and Borgida et al. (1990) identify significant methodological flaws in research evaluating cameras in the courtroom. Examples of these limitations are summarized below. 

44) The Slater and Hans study notes that most state-level evaluations were not true experiments. They relied on post-hoc surveys and interviews rather than controlled experimental designs, lacking randomization, control groups, and systematic manipulation of variables (Slater & Hans, 1982). Nearly all research commissioned by states considering cameras in courtrooms consisted of nonrepresentative surveys and anecdotal evidence (Arkansas Supreme Court Committee, 1979; Cameras-in-the-Courtroom Test Committee, 1974; Hawaii State Bar Association, 1982; Humphries, 1978; Supreme Court of Florida, 1978; Supreme Court of Wisconsin, 1979). Chief Justice Burger in _Chandler v. Florida_ , 449 U.S. 560, 576 n. 11 (1981) noted that the available data were “ ‘limited and non-scientific’, quoting _In re Petition of PostNewsweek Stations, Florida, Inc._ , 370 So. 2d 764, 781, 768 (1979). 

45) The Slater and Hans article also notes that this reliance on survey research is problematic because surveys capture perceptions but cannot isolate causal effects of cameras versus conventional media coverage (Craig, 1979; Judicial Planning Coordination Unit, 1978). Many studies were conducted only at the appellate level, where there are no jurors or witnesses—the groups most likely to be affected. For example, Delaware’s one-year test excluded trial courts, limiting its relevance for concerns about juror and witness impact (Slater & Hans, 1982). Additionally, trials were often selected based on media interest rather than 

implying for some time that cameras are inherently likely to realize public values in the same way that actual public attendance or camera-free media would.159 But this implication wilts under empirical scrutiny.” Tillery, _supra_ , at 727. 

Page 29 of 36 

randomly assigned to coverage conditions. Without randomization, differences may reflect trial characteristics rather than the presence of cameras. 

46) Borgida et al. (1990) highlight further issues in state surveys, such as the 1978 Florida study (Supreme Court of Florida, 1978). Participants were asked to recall experiences from televised trials over the prior year, without accounting for elapsed time or the date questionnaires were completed. The unreliability of self-report data over such intervals was compounded by asking witnesses to reconstruct their prior attitudes toward cameras. Two-thirds of those contacted refused to participate. Despite these problems, the committee concluded cameras had few adverse effects (Borgida et al., 1990, p. 491). 

47) Borgida et al. further notes that California courts employed a multimethod approach, collecting surveys, interviews, and observational data (Short, 1981). Although this evaluation attempted to compare trials with extended coverage versus conventional coverage, contamination occurred when trials switched coverage conditions midstream or were retried under different conditions. Five trials allowed direct comparisons between media types while controlling for case type, but the sample was too small for reliable conclusions. These design flaws reduced internal validity and made causal inference unreliable. 

48) Borgida et al. further notes that early laboratory studies also suffered from methodological shortcomings. Hoyt (1977) and Kassin (1983) used small samples (12 and 25 participants per condition, respectively), likely resulting in underpowered analyses. These studies also failed to include a conventional media condition (e.g., journalist or sketch artist), an essential comparison group. Without this comparison, no valid statement can be made about the effects of cameras relative to conventional media coverage (Borgida et al., 1990). 

Page 30 of 36 

49) In addition to not acknowledging these critiques, the 2012 Final Report’s 

description of the existing studies is flawed.  As indicated above, the Final Report claims that the Kozinski law review article at page 1114 “summarize[ed] state and federal studies on electronic media coverage of judicial proceedings and conclud[ed] that the empirical research demonstrates no detrimental impact on trial participants or on courtroom decorum.” The only study discussed in any detail in the Kozinski article at pp. 1112-1114 is the 1994 federal civil trial pilot program, and regarding that program Judge Kozinski acknowledged that “[t]he program didn’t directly survey witnesses and jurors, as they lack the experience needed to meaningfully compare their experiences to a courtroom without cameras…” _Id_ . at 1113 n. 26. 

50) The 2012 Final Report’s description of the outcome of the 1994 federal civil trial pilot program is as follows: “The pilot program was in effect from 1991 through 1994. Notwithstanding the Federal Judicial Center’s ultimate recommendation that federal trial courts allow cameras in civil proceedings, the federal judiciary declined to continue the program when the study period expired.” Final Report at 10. However, that description does not tell the whole story. As explained by the United States Supreme Court in _Hollingsworth v. Perry_ , 558 U.S. 183, 193 (2010): 

In 1996, the Judicial Conference of the United States adopted a policy opposing the public broadcast of court proceedings. This policy was adopted after a multiyear study of the issue by the Federal Judicial Center which drew on data from six district and two appellate courts, as well as state-court data. In light of the study's findings, _the Judicial Conference concluded that “the intimidating effect of cameras on some witnesses and jurors [is] cause for concern.”_ Administrative Office of United States Courts, Report of Proceedings of Judicial Conference of the United States 47 (Sept. 20, 1994). 

In more than a decade since its adoption the Judicial Conference has continued to adhere to its position on the broadcast of court proceedings. 

(emphasis added) 

Page 31 of 36 

51) _Hollingsworth v. Perry_ itself recognizes that “[t]his Court has recognized that witness testimony may be chilled if broadcast.” _Id_ . at 195, citing _Estes v. Texas,_ 381 U.S. 532, 547 (1965); _id.,_ at 591 (Harlan, J., concurring). The 2012 Final Report does not discuss _Hollingsworth_ . 

52) It should also be noted that the state surveys and the one federal survey discussed in the 2012 Final Report attempted to measure, quite imperfectly, whether having cameras in the courtroom during a civil trial caused jurors to be distracted, to be influenced in their deliberations by the mere presence of the cameras during trial, to feel that the presence of cameras highlighted the importance of the trial or a particular witness, or to be reluctant to serve as jurors. 2012 Study at 124-125. None of these surveys assessed, or could assess, whether live streaming of all aspects of a high profile criminal case in the age of social media could cause irreparable conscious and unconscious bias and trauma to prospective jurors or caused untoward effects in other trial participants 

53) Finally, the 2012 Final Report does not address the pre-2012 empirical research discussed above and could not have considered the substantial empirical research that has taken place since 2012, also discussed above. In addition, and especially relevant in this case, although early PTP research focused primarily on factual, incriminating information about the defendant, more recent work has expanded the conceptualization of PTP to include emotional, community based, victim focused content, and attire of the defendant. This broader approach has revealed that PTP effects are not limited to cognitive contamination from inadmissible facts but also arise from social, emotional, and narrative mechanisms that shape jurors’ pretrial schemas and subsequent decision making. This research too is not addressed in the 2012 report. I briefly review that literature here. 

Page 32 of 36 

### 54) **Community Outrage and Emotional PTP** : Zimmerman et al. (2016) addressed 

a longstanding gap between legal concerns and empirical research by isolating the effects of community outrage—a form of PTP frequently cited by courts but rarely studied 

independently.<sup>58</sup> Using a factorial design that separated factual PTP from community sentiment PTP, the authors found that exposure to community outrage and victim hardship increased mock jurors’ perceptions of the defendant’s guilt, even in the absence of incriminating facts. Notably, neither factual PTP nor anticipated social interaction with community members influenced guilt judgments. These findings suggest that emotional and community based narratives may exert a unique influence on jurors by activating moral intuitions, empathic responses, or perceived social norms, rather than by supplying case relevant information. Zimmerman et al.’s work highlights the need to conceptualize PTP not merely as a source of factual contamination but as a vehicle for communicating collective sentiment that may implicitly pressure jurors toward conviction or death verdicts. 

55) **Victim Pretrial Publicity** : Research demonstrates that exposure to victimfocused pretrial publicity (victim-PTP) significantly influences juror decision-making, perceptions, and cognitive processes. The more recent research on this issue includes only negative-victim PTP (painting the victim in a negative light) exposure, while older studies explored positive-victim attributes directly conveyed to mock juries in trial simulations. 

56) Jurors exposed to negative-victim PTP consistently exhibit a pro-defense bias, 

making them less likely to render guilty verdicts and more likely to interpret trial evidence in a manner favorable to the defense, regardless of the actual probative value of that evidence (Ruva 

58 Zimmerman, D., Rodriguez, D., Bergold, A., & Penrod, S. (2016). The influence of pretrial exposure to community outrage and victim hardship on guilt judgments. _Psychology, Crime & Law_ , _22_ (5), 435–454. https://doi.org/10.1080/1068316X.2015.1120870 

Page 33 of 36 

& Guenther, 2017; Ruva et al., 2022). This bias is explained by predecisional distortion theory, which posits that jurors distort trial evidence to align with their favored side, and by the story model, which suggests that jurors incorporate PTP into mental schemas that guide interpretation of trial evidence (Hope et al., 2004; Ruva et al., 2011; Ruva & Guenther, 2017). 

57) Negative-victim PTP also affects perceptions of the defendant, with jurors exposed to such publicity rating the defendant as more credible than jurors exposed to negativedefendant PTP or irrelevant PTP (Ruva & Guenther, 2017). These credibility assessments mediate the effect of PTP on guilt ratings, pushing verdict preferences toward acquittal when negative-victim PTP is present (Ruva & Guenther, 2017). 

58) Regarding memory, negative-victim PTP exposure leads to source monitoring errors, where jurors misattribute PTP information to the trial. Although negative-victim PTP jurors initially demonstrate somewhat better recall of trial facts than no-PTP jurors—likely due to multiple exposures to case-related information—they also make more critical source memory errors, incorporating PTP details into their recollection of trial evidence (Ruva & Guenther, 2017). Deliberations do not reliably correct these errors and may even facilitate social contagion, spreading misinformation among jurors (Ruva & Guenther, 2017; Ruva et al., 2022). 

59) These findings collectively indicate that victim-PTP can significantly distort jurors’ verdicts, perceptions of credibility, interpretation of evidence, and memory accuracy, and that deliberations are not a reliable corrective mechanism. Instead, deliberations may perpetuate or spread bias, undermining the fairness of trial outcomes. 

60) Supporting these studies are older studies in which the effect of positive-victim attributes are directly conveyed to mock juries in trial simulations. Greene, E., Koehring, H., & Quiat, M., _Victim impact evidence in capital cases: Does the victim's character matter?_ 28 

Page 34 of 36 

_Journal of Applied Social Psychology_ (1998) 145. doi:10.1111/j.1559-1816.1998.tb01697.x, mock jurors watched a reenactment of the penalty phase in the case of _Booth v. Maryland_ . The authors manipulated the respectability of the victim described in the victim impact evidence and found that jurors' judgments of the victims, victims' survivors, and severity of the crime were all affected by this description. The authors also found that jurors disregarded the defendant’s mitigating circumstances (being brought up in a bad neighborhood) if the defendant was on trial for killing a highly respectable victim. The authors noted that their finding that information on the character of the victims influenced mock jurors’ willingness to consider mitigating evidence replicated the same finding made in Burkhead, M., Luginbuhl, J., & Wrenn, S, Victim impact evidence in capital trials: What are its effects?, Poster presented at the meetings of the American Psychology-Law Association, Santa Fe, NM., 

61) **Defendant’s Attire** : Street, Kylene L., et al. _The Cloak of Innocence: Perception of Attire in the Courtroom_ , 40 J. of Police & Criminal Psychology 732 (2025), 

https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s11896-024-09710-w was a mock jury study that found that a defendant dressed in a prison jumpsuit versus a dress suit was seen as “more aggressive”, and that this perceived aggressiveness resulted in defendants being found 5.27 times more likely than mock jurors in the formal attire condition to render a guilty verdict than a not guilty verdict. 

62) In an earlier study, Barnett, _Guilty and Threatening: Visual Bias in Television News Crime Stories_ , 5 Journalism and Communication Monographs 104 (2003), <u>https://doi.org/10.1177/152263790300500301, forty subjects watched television crime news</u> stories, which contained either visual (dressed in an orange suit, wearing handcuffs and being restrained by a police officer) or aural (mention of a prior record) bias. Results showed that subjects who saw the visual bias evaluated the accused as more threatening, dangerous and guilty 

Page 35 of 36 

than those who did not see the bias. Those who heard about a prior record evaluated the accused as more threatening and guilty, but not as more dangerous than those who did not hear about the record. These impressions of guilt remained after a two-week delay. “Findings illustrate how common TV news portrayals of a person accused of a crime can prejudice the general public. Also, accidental viewing of a criminal defendant in prison clothes or being restrained can affect snap judgments about that person during the trial.” _Id._ at 105. 

### **<u>CONCLUSION</u>** 

Substantial empirical evidence exists demonstrating that live streaming of all proceedings in a notorious criminal case can cause bias because (a) live streaming of notorious crimes can be pervasive, slanted, sensational, and emotional—all of which can lead to bias.; and (b) publicity of alleged assassinations, terrorist attacks, or other mass acts of violence may cause trauma and significant anger, disgust, fear, and related bias. Substantial empirical evidence also exists 

demonstrating that pre-trial publicity bias is difficult to recognize and set aside and can distort jury deliberations because (a) biased jurors interpret information differently than unbiased juror; (b) biased jurors may not recognize their biases—undermining voir dire and the effectiveness of limiting instructions; and (c) biased jurors distort jury deliberations. Finally, the empirical research discussed in the 2012 Judicial Council final report is limited and dated and is based on unreliable survey research.

### **<u>Jury Consulting and Expert Testimony</u>** 

- This work includes consulting on high-profile cases, writing reports for the court, and testifying in court as an expert witness regarding the impact of pretrial publicity on defendants’ right to a fair trial guaranteed under the Sixth Amendment of the US Constitution. I have also consulted other jury consultants in high-profile cases.

IN THE DISTRICT COURT OF THE SECOND JUDICIAL DISTRICT OF 

THE STATE OF IDAHO, IN AND FOR THE COUNTY OF LATAH 

) STATE OF IDAHO, ) Case No. CR29-22-2805 ) Plaintiff, ) ) ORDER DENYING SECOND vs. ) MOTION TO INTERVENE AND ) GRANTING DEFENDANT’S BRYAN C. KOHBERGER, ) MOTION TO REMOVE MEDIA ) CAMERAS FROM THE Defendant. ) COURTROOM a ee | | 

I, INTRODUCTION 

This Order addresses the Motion to Remove Cameras from Courtroom filed by Defendant 

Bryan C. Kohberger (“Kohberger”) on August 24, 2023. On September 6. 2023, the State responded to Kohberger’s Motion requesting that the Court “prohibit cameras in the courtroom, both still and video, at a minimum during trial and during any other court proceedings at which victims . .. might be called to testify.” State’s Resp. to Def."s Mot. to Remove Cameras from the Courtroom at 3. 

The On August 30, 2023, a coalition of 14 media outlets,' that will be collectively referred to as the “Associated Press,” filed a second Motion to Intervene in the case. Mot. to Intervene at fn. 

' The Associated Press; Radio Television Digital News Association; Tegna Inc./KREM (Spokane): The Lewiston Tribune; Washington State Association of Broadcasters; Idaho Press Club: KXLY-TV/4 News Now and KAPP/KVEW-TV-Morgan Murphy Media KXLY-TV/4 News Now; Scripps Media, Inc., dba KIVI-TV, a Delaware corporation: The Spokesman Review/Cowles Company; LawNewz, Inc.; ABC, Inc.; Society of Professional Journalists; The McClatchy Company, LLC: and CNN. 

ORDER DENYING SECOND MOTION TO INTERVENE AND GRANTING DEFENDANT'S MOTION TO REMOVE MEDIA CAMERAS FROM THE COURTROOM1 

1. On September 8, 2023, the Associated Press filed an Opposition to Motion to Remove Cameras from the Courtroom. The Associated Press argues that “continuing to allow audio/visual coverage of the proceedings is the only way to ensure that the many community members affected by these crimes throughout the University of Idaho. Moscow. the State of Idaho, and the cities in which the victims’ family members reside have an opportunity to see and hear the proceedings for themselves.” Intervenors’ Opposition to Mot. to Remove Cameras form the Courtroom at 2. 

For the reasons stated below, the Court grants Kohberger’s Motion to Remove Cameras from Courtroom as it pertains to cameras, both still and video, operated by the media. However, the Court will continue to allow the proceedings to be videoed by a Court operated camera system. This will help to alleviate the concerns raised by both the defense and the State, but at the same time will ensure the public still has access to see the proceedings for themselves if they cannot attend hearings in-person. 

fl. BACKGROUND 

On November 13, 2022, four University of Idaho students, Kaylee Goncalves, Madison Mogen. Xana Kernodle, and Ethan Chapin, were found deceased in Goncalves. Mogen. and Kernodle’s off-campus home in Moscow, Idaho. The cause ofdeath for each was ruled a homicide. As news of the tragedy broke, media outlets from around the country descended upon Moscow. As. law enforcement investigated, news stations, newspapers, and social media were flooded with stories and speculation about the homicides. 

On December 30, 2022. Kohberger was arrested and charged with four counts of Murder in the First Degree and one count of Burglary. Again, media outlets descended upon Moscow and the news coverage quickly focused on Kohberger. 

ORDER DENYING SECOND MOTION TO INTERVENE AND GRANTING DEFENDANT’S MOTION TO REMOVE MEDIA CAMERAS FROM THE COURTROOM- 2 

Given the high-profile nature of the case and concerns surrounding Kohberger’s 

fundamental right to a fair trial by an impartial jury, the parties stipulated to entry of a Nondissemination Order. On May 1, 2023, a coalition of20 media outlets, which encompasses the 14 media outlets now at issue, filed a Motion to Intervene and a Motion to Vacate the Amended Nondissemination Order. At a scheduling conference conducted on May 22. 2023, the Court asked the parties and the Associated Press to brief the issue of whether cameras should continue to be allowed in the courtroom during proceedings in this case. 

Oral argument on the issue was heard on June 9, 2023. The State was represented by William W. Thompson, Jr., and Bradley J. Rudley, Latah County Prosecutor’s Office. Defendant Bryan C. Kohberger (“Kohberger™) was represented by Anne C. Taylor and Jay W. Logsdon, 

Kootenai County Public Defender’s Office. The Associated Press was represented by Wendy Olson and Cory Carone. At that time, both the defense and the prosecution were opposed to cameras in the courtroom, especially at trial. Nevertheless, the Court reserved its ruling on the issue and has continued to allow cameras operated by the media at all public hearings. 

As the case has progressed, media coverage, both by legitimate media outlets and journalists and by individuals on social media platforms, continues to be significant nationwide. On August 24. 2023, the defense filed a Motion to Remove Cameras from Courtroom. On August 30. 2023, the Associated Press filed a second Motion to Intervene in the case, and on September 8. 2023. filed an opposition to Kohberger’s motion. On September 6, 2023, the State filed a response to Kohberger’s motion arguing that cameras should at least be banned during any hearings, including trial, where vulnerable witnesses and/or victims are called to testify. 

On September 13, 2023, a hearing on Kohberger’s motion was held. The State was 

represented by William W. Thompson, Jr., Bradley J. Rudley, Jeff Nye, and Ingrid Batey. ORDER DENYING SECOND MOTION TO INTERVENE AND GRANTING DEFENDANT'S 

MOTION TO REMOVE MEDIA CAMERAS FROM THE COURTROOM- 3 

Kohberger was represented by Anne C, Taylor, Jay W. Logsdon, and Elisa Massoth. The 

Associated Press was represented by Wendy Olson and Cory Carone. 

Ill. ISSUES PRESENTED 

The first issue pending before this Court is whether the Associated Press has a right to intervene in the case to challenge Kohberger’s motion. For the reasons stated below and articulated on the record at the hearing on September 13, 2023, the Court holds that the Associated Press does not have standing to intervene in the case at this juncture. Nevertheless, the Court will allow the Associated Press to participate as an Interested Media Coalition. 

The next issue is whether the Court should exercise its discretion and remove cameras from the courtroom to protect Kohberger’s right to a fair trial and to protect vulnerable witnesses and victims who may be called to testify at trial and other hearings. For the reasons stated below. the Court will prohibit still, video, and audio coverage by journalists and the public. However. a Court operated video and audio system will be used to record and stream the proceedings allowing the media and the public access to view and hear for themselves what is taking place in the courtroom. This will alleviate some of the concerns raised by the parties while addressing the media’s concerns with removing cameras from the courtroom. 

Iv. ANALYSIS 

#### 1) Intervention 

There is not a “widespread right of the press — or anyone else — to routinely intervene in Idaho's criminal proceedings.” Jn re Petitionfor Writ ofMandamus or Writ ofProhibition, No. 50482, 2023 WL 3050829 (Idaho Apr. 24, 2023). Instead, the Idaho Supreme Court has endorsed “a limited right, applicable whena trial court’s responsibility to balance the Sixth Amendment right of 

the accused with the First Amendment interests of the media becomes an issue.” /d. ORDER DENYING SECOND MOTION TO INTERVENE AND GRANTING DEFENDANT'S MOTION TO REMOVE MEDIA CAMERAS FROM THE COURTROOM- 4 

There is no First Amendment or other constitutional right to record, either by audio or visual transmission, any court proceedings in Idaho, I.C.A.R. 45. As noted by the 9" Circuit in Radio & Television News Ass'n ofS. California v. U.S. Dist. Ct. for Cent. Dist. ofCalifornia, 781 F.2d 1443, 1444 (9th Cir. 1986), while “[t]he press does enjoy a constitutional interest in access to our criminal courts and criminal justice process. . . the [U.S. Supreme] Court described that right only as a right to sit, listen, watch, and report.” (emphasis added) (citing Richmond Newspapers, 448 U.S. 555, 576, 100 S.Ct. 2814, 2827. 65 L.Ed.2d 973 (1980) (plurality)). 

Because the Associated Press does not have a constitutional right at stake when it comes to cameras in the courtroom, intervention is not appropriate. Thus, the Associated Press’s second Motion to Intervene is denied. However, the Court will treat the Associated Press like amicus curiae. Amicus Curiae is “someone who is not a party to a lawsuit but who petitions the court or is requested by the court to file a brief in the action because that person has a strong interest in the subject matter.” AMICUS CURIAE, Black's Law Dictionary (11th ed. 2019). 

### 2) Cameras in the Courtroom 

In Idaho, the decision to “limit, restrict, or prohibit audio/visual coverage at any proceeding” is left to the discretion of the judge presiding over the case. I.C.A.R. 45(b). Such decisions are not 

subject to appellate review. /d. 

The presiding judge maintains the right to limit audio/visual coverage ofany public hearing when the interests of the administration of<sup>justicerequires.Authorization</sup> may be revoked at any time, without prior notice. when in the discretion of the court it appears that audio/visual coverage is interfering in any way with the proper administration ofjustice. 

LC.ALR. 45(a). 

“Few, if any, interests under the Constitution are more fundamental than the right to a fair trial by ‘impartial’ jurors.” Gentile v. State Bar ofNevada, 501 U.S. 1030, 1075. 111 8. Ct, 2720. ORDER DENYING SECOND MOTION TO INTERVENE AND GRANTING DEFENDANT'S MOTION TO REMOVE MEDIA CAMERAS FROM THE COURTROOM- 5 

2745, 115 L.Ed.2d 888 (1991). The U.S, Supreme Court has recognized a defendant's right to “a trial by an impartial jury free from outside influences” in the face of “massive, pervasive and prejudicial publicity.” Sheppard v. Maxwell, 384 U.S. 333, 335, 362. 86 S. Ct. 1507, 1508, 1522. 16 L. Ed. 2d 600 (1966). While acknowledging that “[a] responsible press has always been regarded as the handmaiden of effective judicial administration, especially in the criminal field.” id. at 350. 86 S. Ct. at 1515, the Court chastised the trial judge for not taking “strong measures” to ensure 

Sheppard's right to a fair trial. /d. at 362. 86 S. Ct. at 1522. While “pretrial publicity even pervasive. adverse publicity does not inevitably lead to an unfair trial,” “[t]he capacity of the jury eventually impaneled to decide the case fairly is influenced by the tone and extent ofthe publicity.” Nebraska Press Ass'n v. Stuart, 427 U.S. 539, 554-55. 96 S.Ct. 2791. 2800-01, 49 L.Ed.2d 683 (1976) (emphasis added). 

While “[o]nly the occasional case presents a danger of prejudice from pretrial publicity.” Gentile, 501 U.S. at 1054, 111 S. Ct. at 2734. this case. as recognized by the Idaho Supreme Court. “has drawn widespread publicity, gamering worldwide media attention and much speculation” and. therefore, pretrial publicity does present a real danger of prejudice. Jn re Petition for Writ of Mandamus or Writ ofProhibition, No. 50482, 2023 WL 3050829, at *1 (Idaho Apr. 24, 2023), “Recognizing the high-profile nature of the case and the extensive coverage it has received, along with the need to minimize possible pretrial prejudice,” the parties agree that cameras should be prohibited from the courtroom, especially at trial and any other hearings where vulnerable victims and witnesses may be called to testify. Jd. As was noted by District Judge Steven W. Boyce in his Memorandum Decision and Order Prohibiting Video and Photographic Coverage in the case of State ofIdaho v. Lori Norene Vallow aka Lori Norene Vallow Daybell, CR22-21-1624, 

ORDER DENYING SECOND MOTION TO INTERVENE AND GRANTING DEFENDANT'S MOTION TO REMOVE MEDIA CAMERAS FROM THE COURTROOM- 6 

“[a]greement between the State and Defense on any issue ina capital case is rare. further confirming to the Court the legitimacy and level of concern counsel have raised.” The same is true in this case. As demonstrated by some of the examples presented in the defense’s Motion to Remove 

Cameras from Courtroom, media cameras, both still and video, have and continue to zoom in on Kohberger despite the Court’s order that images should capture the courtroom in its entirety. At least some of<sup>theimagesareof Kohbergerentering andexitingthecourtroom.Thisisindirect</sup> violation of the Court's previous directives not to film or take photographs until the Court is on the record and to immediately stop recording and taking photographs when the hearing concludes. It is the intense focus on Kohberger and his every move, along with adverse headlines and news articles, that leads the Court to conclude that continued photograph and video coverage inside the courtroom by the media should no longer be permitted. This step should help ensure Kohberger’s right to a fair trial by an impartial jury and achieve a proper administration of<sup>justice.</sup> 

Additionally, defense counsel has raised continued concerns with video footage and 

photographs capturing private documents on counsel table. This is again in violation of the Court’s directives. The State has also raised issues ofvulnerable victims and witnesses being filmed during testimony. At this juncture, the Court has no contidence that directives to stop photographing or videoing during such testimony would be adhered to. Further, the duty on the bailiffs within the courtroom to ensure a safe and secure environment is being eclipsed by the burden to monitor media and the public who may be audio recording. videoing. or photographing the proceedings. 

To be clear, the Court is not accusing all journalists and media outlets ofviolating the 

Court’s orders. However, a Court operated video system will give the Court greater control over what is being videoed, will lessen the burden on the bailiffs, will help to alleviate counsels’ 

concerns, and will allow the media and the public access to the video footage of the proceedings. ORDER DENYING SECOND MOTION TO INTERVENE AND GRANTING DEFENDANT'S MOTION TO REMOVE MEDIA CAMERAS FROM THE COURTROOM- 7 

Vv. CONCLUSION 

For the reasons set forth above, audio/visual coverage ofany proceedings in this case will 

be exclusively done by the Court. Media and the public are not allowed to video, photograph, audio record, or otherwise transmit during the proceedings. 

Future public hearings in this case will be video recorded and that live video stream will be available for the public and media to view at the Court’s YouTube channel: 

hups://www.youtube.com/channel/UCAOFrQpvitreQHRSzhY-zhCA 

The Court reserves the right to amend or prohibit audio/visual coverage in the future if it is determined that videoing the proceedings is interfering in any way with the proper administration ofjustice. 

SO ORDERED this 17"" day of November 2023.
